Case 19-G-0310 - Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules

INITIAL BRIEF OF  
THE BROOKLYN UNION GAS COMPANY D/B/A NATIONAL GRID NY 
AND 
KEYSPAN GAS EAST CORPORATION D/B/A NATIONAL GRID 
Honorable James A. Costello 
Honorable Maureen F. Leary
I.
INTRODUCTION
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (“KEDNY”) and KeySpan 
Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”) are seeking 
to reset their rates for the twelve-month period ending March 31, 2021 (“Rate Year”).  Tr. 2065, 
lines 20-21; Tr. 2303, lines 2-5.  As demonstrated in a record that contains the testimony and 
exhibits of more than 70 KEDNY and KEDLI witnesses, including direct, corrections and updates, 
rebuttal, and three supplemental testimonies, updated rates are required to provide the Companies 
with the financial resources necessary not only to enhance the Companies’ core gas operations and 
provide better service to customers, but also to support New York State’s energy policy objectives, 
which are evolving rapidly.  This pace of change is exemplified by the events that took place during 
these rate proceedings – (i) New York’s adoption of the Climate Leadership and Community 
1 of 310 


--- Page 7 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
2 of 310 
Protection Act of 2019 (“CLCPA”), the most progressive decarbonization legislation in the United 
States, (ii) unexpected delays in the construction and operation of new upstream pipeline capacity, 
(iii) the settlement of issues arising from a moratorium on new gas services,1 and (iv) the New 
York State Public Service Commission’s (the “Commission”) aggressive new energy efficiency 
targets2 – all of which will impact the Companies’ cost of providing services to gas customers in 
New York.  These developments have also contributed to these proceedings extending well beyond 
the traditional eleven-month period, with a final order likely to be released no sooner than July 
2020. 
 In the near term, no event will have a greater impact on the Companies customers than the 
coronavirus (“COVID-19”) global pandemic rapidly spreading across the country.  New York 
State has declared a state of emergency in response to COVID-19.  The government has directed 
the closure of schools statewide and limited public gatherings.  Many businesses, including retail 
stores, gyms, theaters, and casinos, have been directed to close and restaurants may only serve 
food on a delivery or take-out basis.  Other essential businesses, such as grocery stores, gas 
stations, pharmacies, and medical facilities, are operating on limited hours.  These precautions, 
while necessary for public health and safety, will financially impact residents in the Companies’ 
service territories and across the State.  COVID-19 has also impacted the Companies’ operations, 
as the Companies have suspended non-essential work activities out of concern for public and 
employee safety.  The Companies have also temporarily suspended collections-related activities, 
                     
1 Case 19-G-0678, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission to Investigate Denials of Service Requests by 
National Grid USA, The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY and KeySpan Gas East

November 26, 2019) (“Case 19-G-0678 Order”). 
2 Case 18-M-0084, In the Matter of a Comprehensive Energy Efficiency Initiative, Order Authorizing

January 16, 2020) (“NE:NY Order”).

19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
3 of 310 
including service disconnections, to lessen the financial hardship caused by the COVID-19 
pandemic.  These policies will remain in effect at least until the end of April – but New York State 
and its residents will emerge from this difficult situation and the Companies must be positioned to 
support our customers’ energy needs.  
During a period of great change in the energy industry, and in the face of uncertainty in the 
wider economy, the Commission’s support is vital to enable the Companies to deliver the critical 
investments that will pave the way for the transition to New York’s energy future.  These 
investments include projects to modernize the gas networks such as critical reliability projects that 
will support new customer connections in the Companies’ service territories, the upgrade of the 
Companies’ outdated customer information system, the implementation of new digital information 
technology solutions such as the Gas Business Enablement (“GBE”) program, and innovative 
projects that will enable non-traditional supply options such as renewable natural gas and hydrogen 
to play an appropriate role in delivering New York’s energy needs.  Recognizing the dual 
imperative to advance energy policy and manage customers’ bills, these filings balanced the need 
for continued investment with the need to maintain affordability and protect our most vulnerable 
customers.  Among other efforts to moderate bills, the Companies undertook an aggressive cost 
efficiency effort, known as the Accelerate Program, that reduced the Companies’ revenue 
requirements by approximately $50 million – an unprecedented level of savings to be delivered in 
a single year.  Tr. 2014, lines 16-18; Tr. 2264-65, lines 20-21 and 1. 
The record demonstrates that the Companies have advanced comprehensive, measured 
proposals that will enable the critical investments necessary to support the State’s sustainability 
goals while mitigating bill impacts through aggressive efficiency efforts.  Unfortunately, the 
adjustments and proposals made by the Department of Public Service Staff (“Staff”), if adopted, 


--- Page 9 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
4 of 310 
would effectively deny the Companies the funding necessary to modernize their systems and 
deliver on the shared goals of the Companies, the Commission, and the State.  The revenue 
requirements proposed by Staff do not provide the Companies with the revenues needed to provide 
safe and reliable service to customers, earn a reasonable return on their investments, continue to 
attract capital on commercially reasonable terms, and deliver on the Commission and State’s clean 
energy goals.  If the Staff recommendations are substantially adopted, the Companies face the very 
real possibility of having their credit ratings downgraded, resulting in higher costs to customers in 
the future.   
Staff arrives at this result by proposing a return on equity (“ROE”) of just 8.2 percent, 
which would be the lowest ROE approved for a gas distribution utility in the United States in more 
than 40 years.  This proposed ROE implausibly implies that the Companies, who face an uncertain 
future for securing adequate supplies of natural gas, as well as the risks associated with maintaining 
and operating natural gas distribution systems in a world that is increasingly focused on 
decarbonization, are the least risky energy utilities in the United States.  Moreover, although Staff 
says throughout its testimony that the Companies should invest at levels the Companies deem 
appropriate, Staff’s proposals effectively deny the Companies funding for the programs and 
investments they believe are critical to advancing safety and reliability.  Indeed, in many cases, 
Staff’s proposals would require KEDNY and KEDLI to absorb material costs of doing business.  
Specific examples include: 
• Information Technology.  A disallowance of $114.4 million of the Companies’ 
projected capital information technology (“IT”) investments that must be incurred 
to modernize KEDNY and KEDLI’s aging information systems infrastructure and 


--- Page 10 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
5 of 310 
enable key customer and operations programs such as the new Customer 
Information System (“CIS”).   
• GBE Program.  A disallowance of more than $57 million in costs that are being 
incurred for the benefit of customers to progress the GBE project, which is needed 
to build a modern platform that enhances safety and supports customer 
requirements. 
• Unidentified Savings.  An adjustment to impute more than $13 million of 
incremental, unidentified savings on top of the more than $50 million in savings 
already reflected in the Rate Year. 
• Newtown Creek.  An adjustment that effectively requires KEDNY to absorb a 
portion of the costs of the Newtown Creek project notwithstanding (i) the 
agreement in the Joint Proposal in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Case Order3 
that the Company would proceed with the project, (ii) the Commissions’ recently-
articulated position that the Companies should be doing more to develop additional 
local renewable natural gas resources,4 and (iii) the project aligns with New York 
City’s and the State’s climate change goals. 
• Future of Heat.  Virtually no funding for the Companies’  Future of Heat programs, 
which are designed to advance cost-effective, reliable, heating sources that help 
reduce carbon emissions from the gas network in alignment with the State’s goals.

(“2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Case Order”).

Tr. 1982-83, lines 7-20 and 1-6; Tr. 2240, lines 5-19. 
To support many of their positions, Staff essentially creates new ratemaking standards.  For 
decades, the standard has been that in the absence of imprudence a utility is entitled to recover its 
costs.5  Moreover, since 1977, the Commission has followed its Policy Statement on Test Periods 
in Major Rate Proceedings6 that permits utilities to utilize a forward-looking test year and reflect 
updates for material changes that arise after a rate filing is made and are known and measurable.  
Certain of Staff’s proposed adjustments turn those standards upside down.  For instance, Staff 
proposes to disallow the costs associated with a new damage prevention mark out contract that 
was entered two months ago, effectively denying the Companies’ the right to update their filings 
in the manner permitted under the Commission’s Test Period Policy Statement, primarily because 
the Companies failed to give advance notice that the update might be coming prior to it becoming 
known and measurable.  Tr. 3636-38.  Staff also proposes to deny recovery of a management wage 
increase that was paid out to employees last July and is therefore known and measurable because 
Staff, in its opinion, believes a lesser increase is simply more appropriate.  Tr. 2366, lines 3-8; Tr. 
5275, lines 22-24.  In addition, in proposing more stringent gas safety targets for calendar year 
2020, Staff’s position is that the Companies should have begun implementing the new targets – 
because Staff made the proposals in their testimony – and that Commission should now adopt these 
new targets six or seven months into the performance year, even though Staff acknowledged that 
                     
5 See Matter of National Fuel Gas Distrib. Corp. v Public Serv. Commn. of the State of N.Y., 16 N.Y.3d 
360, 369 (2011) (“a utility company seeking a rate change has the burden proving that the requested 
regulatory action is ‘just and reasonable.’  However, a utility’s decision to expend monetary resources is 
presumed to have been made in the exercise of reasonable managerial judgment.  DPS carries the initial 
burden of providing a rational basis to infer that the utility may have acted imprudently before the burden 
shifts to the utility to demonstrate that its decision was prudent when made.”). 
6 Statement of Policy on Test Periods in Major Rate Proceedings, 17 NYPSC 25-R (1977) (“Test Period 
Policy Statement”).

Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
7 of 310 
the Companies need to plan ahead of time to meet these new targets.  Tr. 4144-45, lines 15-25 and 
2-10.  These recommendations are neither just nor reasonable under any circumstances.   
In addition to proposing significant disallowances of costs that the Companies will need to 
incur in the Rate Year, Staff also proposes a series of downward-only true-ups for costs associated 
with full-time equivalents, paving, and credit card costs.  Tr. 1983, lines 7-13.  At the same time, 
while the Companies have agreed to tracking mechanisms on the costs associated investments in 
utility plant and information systems, Staff rejects the limited true-ups sought by the Companies 
for costs over which they have limited control such as property taxes and City State Construction 
(“CSC”) operations and maintenance (“O&M”) cost spending.  Id.  The proposed downward only 
true-ups effectively preclude the Companies from retaining efficiencies they may find to offset the 
costs that Staff proposes to disallow and thus achieve the additional productivity that Staff 
proposes to impute.  On top of this, Staff proposes to modify the Companies’ performance metrics 
substantially, adding more stringent targets, but not providing funding to meet these new targets.  
These proposals not only disregard recent Commission precedent holding that performance metrics 
are not appropriate in a one-year case, they also render it unlikely that the Companies will be able 
to meet these more challenging targets if they take effect in the middle of a calendar year.  These 
proposals would effectively result in further disallowances of costs by increasing the likelihood 
that the Companies will incur negative revenue adjustments.   
The combined effect of all of Staff’s adjustments is that the Companies would be required 
to manage unprecedented risks associated with an uncertain supply situation and the impacts of 
rapidly evolving decarbonization and climate change policies, while being saddled with the lowest 
ROE authorized for a gas utility in more than 40 years and then effectively precluded from earning 


--- Page 13 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
8 of 310 
it.  This end-result is unreasonable and unjustified and creates grave concern as to whether the 
Companies will be able to continue to attract necessary capital on commercially reasonable terms.     
For decades, the combination of stable earnings, generally manageable business risks, and 
declining or stable market interest rates have allowed the Companies to access capital markets on 
favorable terms and reduce borrowing costs for the benefit of customers.  Without a reasonable 
return on equity, funding for incremental capital investments, and support for timely recovery of 
operating expenses, the Companies are at risk of credit quality downgrades that would further 
increase the Companies’ risks and raise costs for customers. 
Over the term of their current rate plans, the Companies have invested billions of dollars 
in their gas distribution networks to support safe operations, met and exceeded all customer and 
gas performance metrics, and improved their compliance performance year over year.  In only the 
last year, the regulatory landscape has seen significant changes that will have material impacts on 
KEDNY and KEDLI’s costs and programs going forward.  The Companies’ proposed rate plan 
was designed to address in a balanced manner issues raised by Staff and other stakeholders and 
take important step changes necessary to lead the transition to New York’s energy future.  To that 
end, the Companies believed – and continue to believe – that a multi-year settlement was and will 
be in the best interests of all parties, as it would provide the Companies with the financial resources 
necessary to run the business while enabling all parties to work collaboratively on efforts to further 
the future of gas in New York.  The Companies provided three additional years of data to facilitate 
such a settlement, and also proposed to carry forward the $50 million in forecast Rate Year savings 
to each of the Data Years7 as a way to further mitigate the rate requests.  Despite these efforts, it 
does not appear that a multi-year settlement is possible at this time.  Accordingly, the Companies 
                     
7 The Data Years are the twelve months ending March 31, 2022 (“Data Year 1”), March 31, 2023 (“Data

urge the Commission to determine the contested issues in these proceedings in a manner that 
preserves the Companies’ financial integrity and funds the necessary investment to assist the 
Companies in maintaining and enhancing their operations and supporting the State’s long-term 
energy goals. 
II. 
 PROCEDURAL BACKGROUND 
A. 
Initial Filing 
On April 30, 2019, KEDNY and KEDLI filed tariff leaves and supporting testimony and 
exhibits for new rates and charges for gas service to be effective April 1, 2020.   The new tariffs 
were designed to increase gas delivery revenues for KEDNY and KEDLI by approximately $237 
million and $49 million, respectively.  The twelve months ended December 31, 2018 was the 
Historic Test Year.  The testimony and exhibits were prepared under the assumption that 
Transcontinental Gas Pipe Line, LLC’s Northeast Supply Enhancement (“NESE”) project would 
be in-service by the winter of 2020/2021.   
The initial filing included cost projections for the three Data Years beyond the Rate Year 
in the interest of exploring a multi-year rate plan settlement between the Companies and the parties 
that would allow for a phase in of the revenue increases and the ability to manage the impact on 
customers’ bills.   
B. 
Conferences and Discovery 
Administrative Law Judges (“ALJs”) James A. Costello and Maureen F. Leary were 
appointed to conduct the proceedings.  On May 30, 2019, the ALJs held a procedural conference 
that was immediately followed by a technical presentation by the Companies discussing various 
aspects of the rate filings.   


--- Page 15 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
10 of 310 
On June 5, 2019, the ALJs issued a Ruling on Schedule that provided dates for certain 
activities in the cases, including the filing of parties’ initial and rebuttal testimony and hearings.  
The active parties engaged in extensive discovery throughout the proceedings with the Companies 
responding to more than 1,650 information requests.   
C. 
Supplemental Testimony 
As agreed with Staff and discussed with the active parties at the procedural conference held 
on May 30, 2019, the Companies filed supplemental testimony on June 11, 2019 to illustrate the 
potential revenue requirement and other impacts to the Companies’ rate filings in the event the 
NESE project was not available during the proposed rate plan period.   
D. 
Corrections and Updates 
KEDNY and KEDLI filed corrections and updates testimony and exhibits on July 3, 2019, 
updating the revenue requirement to approximately $196 million and $61 million, respectively.   
E. 
Parties’ Testimony 
On August 30, 2019, ten parties, including Staff, filed testimony and exhibits addressing 
the Companies’ filings.  These parties included: the City of New York (“NYC”), the Public Utility 
Law Project (“PULP”), the Environmental Defense Fund (“EDF”), the Utility Intervention Unit of 
the New York Department of State’s Division of Consumer Protection (“UIU”), the Long Island 
Power Authority (“LIPA”), Estates NY Estate Services LLC (“Estates”), the Sane Energy Project 
(“Sane”), New York State Laborers’ Organizing Fund, and Bob Wyman.   
F. 
Rebuttal Testimony 
KEDNY and KEDLI and four other parties each filed rebuttal testimony and exhibits on 
September 18, 2019.  In their rebuttal testimony, the Companies adjusted the revenue requirements 
to assume the NESE project would not be in service during the Rate Year.  The updated revenue 


--- Page 16 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
11 of 310 
requirements for KEDNY and KEDLI were approximately $190 million and $67 million, 
respectively. 
G. 
Settlement Discussions and Make Whole and Other Provisions 
On September 10, 2019, the Companies filed with the Commission a Notice of Impending 
Settlement Discussions.  To facilitate settlement discussions and promote the orderly conduct of 
these proceedings, the Companies filed requests to extend the suspension period and hearing dates 
on September 25, October 31, November 12, and December 9, 2019.   
The Companies were agreeable to the extensions with the understanding that Staff 
supported a make whole provision that would assure the Companies will be restored to the same 
financial position they would have been in had there been no extension and new rates went into 
effect on April 1, 2020 and specific additional provisions relating to the Companies’ next rate 
filings.  These additional provisions included: (i) a Commission grant of a limited waiver of the 
Commission’s Test Period Policy Statement permitting the Companies to use a historic test year 
consisting of the twelve months ending December 31, 2019 in their next base rate filings; and (ii) 
a Commission determination that a make whole provision will be implemented effective April 1, 
2021, in connection with new rates established by the Commission in response to the Companies’ 
next base rate filings.   The purpose of this make whole provision would be to keep the Companies 
and their customers in the same position they would have been had the Companies filed for new 
rates by April 30, 2020, with the intention that the Companies would file their next base rate filings 
on or about 15 days following issuance of a Commission order in the instant proceedings.

IV. 
REVENUES 
A. 
Gas Sales Forecast 
1. 
The Commission Should Adopt The Companies’ Gas Sales Forecasts 
During these proceedings, the Companies filed several updated versions of their gas sales 
forecasts as well as the pre-filed testimony and exhibits of Company Witness Theodore Poe Jr. to 
support those forecasts.  Updates to the forecasts were provided to reflect the changing effects of 
the gas moratorium settlement in Case 19-G-0678, implement certain corrections and updates, and 
incorporate the effect of the NE:NY Order.  The Companies presented testimony and exhibits in 
support of their final gas sales forecasts on January 30, 2020 in Company Witness Poe’s third 
supplemental testimony.  Tr. 5198-5201; Exhibits 395-402.  Other than the limited issues noted 
below, all versions of the Companies’ gas sales forecasts were essentially uncontested on the 
record by Staff and other intervening parties.  As discussed below, the Companies believe that the 
issues raised have already been addressed or lack sufficient support for the Commission to modify 
the Companies’ final gas sales forecast.  Therefore, based on the evidence presented, the 
Companies’ gas sales forecast is reasonable and should be adopted.

gas usage reductions from the NE:NY Order.  Staff agreed with the Companies’ incorporation of 
energy efficiency measures in the gas sales forecasts in their response to IR NG-88.  Exhibit 733

B. 
Newtown Creek Imputation 
1. 
KEDNY Should Be Afforded The Opportunity To Recover The Full Costs 
Of The Newtown Creek Renewable Natural Gas Project 
KEDNY’s Newtown Creek project, located at the NYC Newtown Creek Wastewater 
Treatment Plant, seeks to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by promoting renewable natural gas 
(“RNG”) as a long-term supply source.  Tr. 102.  The project will permit KEDNY to capture the 
biogas generated from the wastewater plant, filter it through a purification system, and upgrade it 
to pipeline quality natural gas.  Id.  The gas can then be odorized and injected into KEDNY’s 
distribution system.  Id. 
The record shows that the Newtown Creek project will provide several benefits that align 
with New York’s clean energy goals.  Specifically, the project will expand the concept of RNG as 
a viable alternative to conventional natural gas, thus diversifying the supply of gas.  Tr. 103.  The 
project will also provide a new method for managing local waste resources and reduce greenhouse 
gas emissions.  Id.  In total, KEDNY anticipates that the Newtown Creek project will produce 
approximately 277,500 dekatherms of pipeline quality RNG, reducing Carbon Dioxide (“CO2”) 
emissions by approximately 16,000 tons annually.  Id.  Moreover, in a separate partnership, NYC 
is working with Waste Management to incorporate processed food waste into the wastewater 
sludge at National Grid.  Id.  The combined projects have the potential to reduce annual CO2 
emissions by 90,000 metric tons.  Id.   
In KEDNY’s previous base rate case, Case 16-G-0059, the parties agreed in the Joint 
Proposal resolving the case to the following provision concerning the Newtown Creek project: 
KEDNY will continue to develop the Newtown Creek biogas purification 
project. Because the ultimate in service date and final capital costs are not 


--- Page 22 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
17 of 310 
known at this time, the costs are not included in the revenue requirement.  
Once the project is in service, KEDNY is permitted to defer the return of 
and return on the Newtown Creek project and have those costs reviewed for 
potential recovery in its next base rate filing.  The revenue requirement for 
the Newtown Creek project will be subject to a $1.6 million annual 
exclusion (prorated from the in service date) for 20 years.  The $1.6 million 
amount and the 20 year time period will be subject to review for possible 
modification in KEDNY’s next rate case.  The revenue requirement 
associated with the project will reflect a 40 year depreciable life.  CNY 
agrees to support KEDNY’s reasonable efforts to mitigate the effects of the 
revenue requirement exclusion, including, but not limited to, supporting 
KEDNY’s application(s) for property tax abatements, to the extent such 
efforts are permitted by applicable law.  Further, any revenues from 
KEDNY’s share of the sale of gas at a premium (i.e., in excess of the 
weighted average cost of gas) or environmental credits generated from the 
project will be used to offset the revenue requirement exclusion.11 
KEDNY proposes to include the full costs of the Newtown Creek project in its revenue 
requirement in this proceeding.12  Tr. 104.  In addition, KEDNY expects to generate revenues from 
the project associated with the sale of RNG produced from the project as well as from monetizing 
the environmental attributes of the project.  Tr. 105.  KEDNY proposes to true up revenue from 
the project to the amount reflected in rates.  Id.  KEDNY has included a revenue estimate of $1.9 
million per year comprised of $1 million in revenue from gas sales and $0.9 million in revenue 
from the sale of environmental attributes.  Tr. 105-106.  Any revenues realized above the level 
necessary to fully reimburse customers for project costs would be shared equally between 
customers and NYC beginning in the fifth year after the project becomes operational.  Tr. 106.  As 
permitted under the Joint Proposal, KEDNY also proposes to do away with the $1.6 million

2. 
Staff’s Proposed Revenue Imputation Associated With The Newtown 
Project Is Unreasonable And At Odds With Commission Policy 
Staff’s proposed imputation of $3.3 million of revenues associated with the Newtown 
Creek project should be rejected for a number of reasons.  First, in Case 16-G-0059, Staff agreed 
with KEDNY and the other parties that KEDNY would continue to develop the Newtown Creek 
project even though the record shows that Staff was aware that the capital costs of the project 
would exceed $30 million and that the revenues from sales of gas from the project would not 
exceed the revenue requirement.  Tr. 3675-76.  It would be unreasonable and unfair for the 
Commission to adopt Staff’s proposed revenue adjustment and impose the risk of recovering the 
costs of the project on KEDNY after Staff agreed to, and the Commission approved, a joint 
proposal that required the Company to complete the project. 
More importantly, however, Staff’s proposed revenue imputation is at odds with New York 
State policy that encourages the development of RNG resources even where such resources may 
be more expensive than conventional gas resources.  Specifically, in Consolidated Edison 
Company of New York, Inc.’s (“Con Edison”) most recent base rate proceeding in Case 19-G-
                     
13 These benefits include the property tax exemption obtained from NYC.  Tr. 107. 
 


--- Page 24 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
19 of 310 
0066, the Commission approved a provision of a joint proposal that permits Con Edison to 
purchase RNG even though it is more expensive than conventional natural gas supplies.14  This 
decision was consistent with the Commission’s previous statements in the Companies’ service 
moratorium proceeding in Case 19-G-0678 that the Companies should have been more aggressive 
in seeking out, inter alia, RNG as an alternative source of local gas supply.15 
As the Companies’ Future of Heat panel testified, the fact that the unit cost of RNG is 
greater today than conventionally produced gas is not surprising and is reminiscent of similar 
comparisons between wind and solar electric renewables to fossil fuels a decade ago.  Tr. 182.  If 
the cost disparity between RNG and traditional natural gas is ever going to be narrowed, and if 
RNG is going to become more prevalent, there must be regulatory support for investments such as 
the Newtown Creek project.  Tr. 187.  Adoption of the Staff recommendation to require the revenue 
from an RNG facility to fully offset the cost of an RNG project at this time would place an 
unmanageable level of risk on RNG development and discourage the future development of RNG 
in New York. 
In addition, the SPP’s revenue imputation recommendation unreasonably fails to recognize 
that the benefits of the Newtown Creek project extend far beyond the cost of the RNG produced.  
The Newtown Creek project has been highlighted as a case study for utility RNG interconnections, 
as well as the potential for public-private partnerships to reduce metropolitan greenhouse gas

V. 
 EXPENSES 
A. 
O&M Expense 
 
1. 
Consultants – AlixPartners (KEDNY and KEDLI) 
Staff proposes to disallow $1.146 million and $0.714 million of costs for KEDNY and 
KEDLI, respectively, associated with work performed by AlixPartners, a consultant utilized by the 
Companies in the Historic Test Year to support delivery of their IT portfolios.  Tr. 2349, lines 13-
15; Tr. 2346, lines 20-23; Tr. 1986, lines 19-21.  The basis for the adjustment is Staff’s opinion 
that the charges will not recur in the Rate Year.  Tr. 2347, lines 9-13.  The record demonstrates, 
however, that Staff’s opinion is incorrect and that similar costs will indeed recur in the Rate Year. 
a. Consultants Will Be Used To Assist With The Efficient Delivery Of 
The 
IT 
Portfolio, 
Deliver 
The 
Process 
Improvements 
Recommended By The Staff IT Panel, And Address Cybersecurity 
Risks, Among Other Things, In The Rate Year 
In proposing the adjustment, Staff takes a very narrow view of what constitutes recurring 
work for purposes of forecasting Rate Year consultant costs.  Specifically, Staff’s position is that 
the individual work performed by AlixPartners in the Historic Test Year, such as work to refresh 
the Companies’ IT strategy and assist with the efficient delivery of the IT portfolio, will not recur 
in the Rate Year because this work was already undertaken by AlixPartners.  Tr. 2348-49.  This is 
an incorrect assumption for several reasons.   
First, as Staff conceded during cross-examination, in reviewing Historic Test Year 
consultant costs to determine if normalizing adjustments are required, the purpose of the review is 
not to see if the same consultant will perform the same exact work in the Rate Year but whether 


--- Page 27 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
22 of 310 
the costs are representative of what will be incurred in the Rate Year.  Tr. 2461-62.  Specifically, 
Staff testified: 
Q. 
No, I appreciate your position.  I understand it. I'll talk about it 
further in a minute, but I would like an answer to my question.  And 
my question was, you're not looking to see when you're reviewing 
that list, if the precise cost will recur or if the same consultant will 
perform the same work.  You're all looking to see whether the costs 
in the historic test year are representative of what being incurred in 
the rate year. Is that correct? 
 
A.  
Yes. 
Tr. 2462, lines 3-14. 
In that regard, the Companies, like all utilities today, utilize consultants like AlixPartners 
to provide services aimed at ensuring the efficient delivery of the IT portfolio given the rapidly 
evolving and increasingly complex nature of IT work.  While Staff contends that “it is not 
reasonable” for the Companies to use a consultant to perform this type of work again in the Rate 
Year, the Companies submit that it would be unreasonable if they did not.  Tr. 2349, lines 3-7.  
While the Companies may not use AlixPartners again in the Rate Year, the Companies will most 
certainly continue to engage consultants like AlixPartners to proactively look for ways to maintain 
existing IT efficiencies and ensure the cost-effective operation of the IT portfolio.  Tr. 1987, lines 
3-11.  This work would be expected of any entity with an IT portfolio as large as the Companies 
– especially given the benefits that can accrue to customers from this work.  Id.  Therefore, these 
costs are fully expected to recur in the Rate Year and should not be adjusted from the cost of 
service.  Id.   
Second, the precise work performed by AlixPartners in the Historic Test Year is but one 
item under a broad category of work performed by consultants to assist with the prudent 
management of the Companies’ IT portfolio.  The Companies agree that some of the work 
performed by AlixPartners, such as refreshing the Companies’ IT strategy or executing a 


--- Page 28 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
23 of 310 
transformation plan, may not recur in the Rate Year.  Tr. 2348-49.  However, that does not mean 
that other work performed by IT consultants such as AlixPartners to evaluate and assess the IT 
portfolio will not recur.   
For instance, Staff conveniently ignores that in addition to helping the Companies refresh 
their IT strategy and execute a transformation plan – the work that Staff contends will not recur in 
the Rate Year (Tr. 2348-49) – AlixPartners also performed work to: (i) identify IT efficiency 
opportunities; (ii) benchmark and identify best practices of other IT organizations,; (iii) analyze 
system investment opportunities; (iv) assist with contract negotiations to improve vendor spend;  
and (v) identify and assess cyber security and compliance risks.  This work is set forth in the 
purchase orders found in Exhibit 504 (SRRP-3), pages 803 and 810-11 of 943.  This work will 
continue to be required in the Rate Year whether performed by AlixPartners or another consultant, 
as it helps ensure, among other things, that IT costs are delivered at the lowest costs for customers.  
In fact, the work performed by AlixPartners resulted in significant savings to customers that are 
reflected in the revenue requirements.17  
Additionally, the Staff IT Panel made a number of recommendations in these proceedings 
to enhance the Companies’ IT performance, such as the development of key performance 
indicators.  Tr. 1010-16.  Surprisingly, the Staff Revenue Requirements Panel, which supports the 
adjustment to consultant costs, testified that it was entirely unaware of this recommendation.  Tr. 
2463, lines 17-24.  The Staff IT Panel also testified that “[t]he field of cybersecurity is one in 
which the risks, threat actors/vectors, and technologies involved are constantly changing and 
increasing in complexity at a breakneck pace.”  Tr. 982, lines 19-23.  As a result, the Staff IT Panel 
                     
17 The revenue requirement includes savings of $5.5 million in the Historic Test Year and $16.6 million of

recommended that “utilities remain constantly vigilant and seek new and innovative ways to

2. 
Contractors – Snow Removal Costs (KEDNY Only) 
Staff proposes a downward adjustment to snow removal costs for KEDNY only based on 
the use of a three-year average.  Tr. 2353, lines 19-24.  Staff claims that a three-year average will 
“smooth out” year to year changes and “produce a more reasonable Rate Year forecast.”  Tr. 2354, 
lines 1-4.  The adjustment reduces KEDNY’s Historic Test Year contractor expense of $3.337 
million by $1.263 million, to $2.074 million.  Tr. 2352, lines 21-23; Tr. 2354, lines 7-9.  KEDNY 
disagrees with Staff’s adjustment and believes that use of the Historic Test Year to forecast snow 
removal costs is reasonable and appropriate.  There are two main reasons that KEDNY’s position 
is reasonable and should be adopted.   


--- Page 30 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
25 of 310 
a. KEDNY’s Forecast Of Snow Removal Costs Is Reasonable and 
Been Consistently Forecast In The Same Manner 
First, use of the Historic Test Year treats customers and the Company fairly over time.  
This expense has been forecast the same way in several past KEDNY, KEDLI, and Niagara 
Mohawk rate cases, and Staff has never proposed using a different methodology.  Tr. 1990, lines 
2-4.  In KEDNY’s last rate case (Case 16-G-0059), the rate allowance for snow removal expense 
was approximately $0.689 million.  Id., lines 4-5.  Actual expenses in calendar years 2017 and 
2018 (rate years 1 and 2 of the current rate plan) were $1.8 million and $3.337 million, 
respectively.  Id., lines 5-7.  Calendar year 2019 (rate year three) expense at the time KEDNY’s 
rebuttal testimony was filed in September 2019 was $2.115 million – more than three times the 
rate allowance.  Tr. 1990, lines 7-9.  Therefore, KEDNY did not fully recover its actual snow 
removal costs at any time during the current rate plan.  Utilizing Staff’s proposed three-year 
average in this case is inconsistent with the historic forecasting methodology and would continue 
the inequitable result of including a rate allowance that prohibits KEDNY from fully recovering 
its costs of this expense.  There is no reason to believe that Staff’s approach will result in a more 
accurate forecast of Rate Year expense.     
Second, use of a consistent forecasting methodology helps minimize controversy by 
avoiding situations such as the one here where parties pick and choose different methodologies 
depending on the end-result.  Notably, Staff is not proposing to use a three-year average to 
calculate KEDLI’s snow removal expense, but instead is using the Historic Test Year expense as 
the basis for the Rate Year forecast.  Tr. 1990-91, lines 18-20 and 1. 
For all these reasons, Staff’s adjustment should be rejected.      


--- Page 31 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
26 of 310 
3. 
Other Expense – Fines and Summonses (KEDNY Only) 
Staff proposes a 25 percent disallowance of KEDNY’s Historic Test Year costs associated 
with fines and summonses incurred by KEDNY for work performed in New York City.  Tr. 2563, 
lines 16-20; Tr. 2596, lines 4-9.  According to Staff, the proposed disallowance is a “conservative 
approach” to reflect the process improvements undertaken by KEDNY to reduce the number of 
fines and summonses and account for those that Staff contends result from circumstances under 
KEDNY’s control.  Tr. 2563-64, lines 20-24 and 1-4.  The disallowance reduces the revenue 
requirement by $0.920 million.  Tr. 2564, lines 9-13.  There are several reasons why Staff’s 
proposed adjustment should be rejected.    
a. Staff’s Adjustment Is A Double-Count Of An Adjustment KEDNY 
Made To Reflect The Benefits Of The GBE Program 
The first reason why Staff’s adjustment should be rejected is that KEDNY’s revenue 
requirement already reflects savings associated with a forecast reduction in fines and summonses 
in the Rate Year, resulting from process improvements planned to be delivered by the GBE 
Program.  Specifically, Exhibit 56 (CJC-5), Schedule 2, shows a reduction to KEDNY’s revenue 
requirement of $1.423 million in operating expenses in the Rate Year.  KEDNY has therefore 
included an adjustment in excess of the one proposed by Staff.  Staff was entirely unaware of this 
adjustment when asked about it during cross-examination, and Staff’s lack of awareness resulted 
in a double-count of the anticipated savings associated with KEDNY’s efforts to reduce fines and 


--- Page 32 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
27 of 310 
summonses in the Rate Year.  Tr. 2608, lines 5-7.  For this reason alone, Staff’s proposed 
disallowance should be rejected. 18  
b. Staff’s Adjustment Demonstrates A Fundamental Lack of 
Understanding Of The Realities of Performing Work in NYC, the 
Significant Efforts Undertaken By KEDNY To Reduce Its Level Of 
Fines and Summonses, And Does Not Include Any Evidence 
Showing That Reductions Above Historic Test Year Levels Can Be 
Achieved   
Staff’s claim that fines and summonses are under KEDNY’s control and, as a result, 
KEDNY should bear a portion of these costs demonstrates a fundamental lack of understanding 
about the reasons why fines and summonses are incurred and KEDNY’s ability to cost-effectively 
avoid them.  These costs are incurred in the ordinary course of business as KEDNY must regularly 
excavate in streets and sidewalks in one of the largest and most densely populated metropolitan 
areas in the country.  Tr. 1992-93, lines 5-21 and 1-3.  While KEDNY takes precautions to avoid 
fines and summonses, given the nature of many construction projects, these costs are not cost-
effectively avoidable above the level of reductions that KEDNY has already achieved in the 
Historic Test Year.  Id.  Fines and summonses are often the result of unanticipated field conditions 
(e.g., subsurface facilities, parking conditions, heavy traffic) that require KEDNY to work in a 
larger area or for a longer period than was contemplated at the time the permit was secured.  Id.  
KEDNY could take additional measures solely for purposes of avoiding violations, such as 
repeated demobilizations and remobilizations to avoid working past permit times, but such 
measures would not be as efficient and would result in more customer disruptions and increased 
                     
18 It is important to note that the savings included by KEDNY in the revenue requirement are an annual

inappropriate to add Staff’s adjustment on top of KEDNY’s adjustment given that the projected savings 
from the GBE Program will not materialize as anticipated.  If the Commission were to agree with Staff’s 
adjustment, the savings of $1.423 million associated with the process improvements to reduce fines and 
summonses due to implementation of the GBE Program must be removed from the revenue requirement.

As part of the Joint Proposal in Case 16-G-0059, KEDNY was required to file an annual

c. Staff’s Adjustment Ignores The Impact Of The Increased Capital

Another reason why Staff’s adjustment should be rejected is that it ignores the impact that

incremental CSC work and leak prone pipe removal work above Historic Test Year levels.  Tr. 
1994, lines 11-17.  As explained in the CY 2018 report and the response to IR DPS-928, an increase 
in the amount of permitted work will likely result in more fines and summonses despite KEDNY’s 
best efforts to avoid these costs.  Exhibit 411 (SAP-1), page 32 of 503; Exhibit 350 (RRP-4R), 
page 40-42 of 46.  Indeed, page 9 of the CY 2018 report contains a table that depicts the violations 
per permit ratio normalized from 2016 to 2018.  Exhibit 411 (SAP-1), page 32 of 503.  The table 
shows a reduction of approximately 40 percent in the number of violations incurred by KEDNY 
in the Historic Test Year on a normalized basis if the volume of work had not increased.  Id.; Tr. 
2604, lines 18-23.  In that regard, Staff agreed that KEDNY could improve its performance in 
terms of the number of violations per permit but that the number and costs associated with 
violations could increase or remain the same if the number of permits increase.21  Tr. 2602-03, 
lines 23-25 and 2-14.   
                     
21 As shown in the CY 2018 report, KEDNY has experienced a 230 percent increase in the number of street

Importantly, to forecast Rate Year costs, KEDNY used the Historic Test Year as a base

4. 
Service Company Rent Expense – IT Costs (KEDNY and KEDLI) 
a. The Companies’ Proposed IT Capital Investment Program Is 
Necessary To Provide Customers With Safe And Adequate Service 
And Ensure The Security Of Systems And Networks 
 
IT capital projects and investments that are shared across operating companies are 
implemented and owned by National Grid USA Service Company (“Service Company”). Tr. 825, 
lines 16-18.  The costs of these investments are allocated to KEDNY and KEDLI (and the other 
operating companies that utilize and/or benefit from the investments) in the form of rent expense.  
Tr. 825, lines 18-20.  National Grid’s proposed Rate Year IT capital investment is $243.846 million 
and total IT operating expense is $318.7 million.  Exhibits 280 and 281.   
 
Staff proposes to reduce National Grid’s Rate Year IT capital spend by nearly half, from 
the proposed level of $243.846 million to $129.4 million. Tr. 1019, lines 4-8; Exhibit 519, p. 1 of 
6.  The proposed IT capital program cuts would have significant impacts on the rent expense

recovery Staff recommends the Companies should be allowed.  Exhibit 519, p. 1 of 6.  Staff also

would severely affect National Grid’s ability to develop and advance very important IT projects 
for customers.  Staff’s proposed adjustments to the Companies’ IT capital plans and operating

b. Staff’s Proposed Adjustment to Remove $57.4 million from the 
GBE Project Forecast is Arbitrary and Would Result in An 
Unreasonable Disallowance of Prudent Costs Needed to Provide 
Safe and Adequate Service 
 
The Staff Information Technology Panel (“SITP”) proposes an adjustment to reduce the 
investment forecast for the GBE Program by $57.4 million, comprised of: (i) $12.2 million related 
to testing and training; (ii) $21.17 million related to increased legacy project integration 
complexity; and (iii) a $24.03 million reduction for allowance for funds used during construction 
(“AFUDC”).  Tr. 989-90, lines 20-24 and 1-13.  Staff’s proposed adjustment is not based on a 
determination that some portion of the GBE program is unnecessary or unwarranted, and Staff 
does not make any recommendation as to what the Companies should have done differently.  Tr. 
1053-54, lines 17-25 and 2-4; and Tr. 1061, lines 15-19.  Rather, Staff bases its recommendation 
to reduce the GBE project forecast based on what it believes would be a “reasonable budget.”  Tr. 
1061, lines 8-13.  Staff’s proposed adjustment has no factual basis and should be rejected.   
 
Between the 2017 Niagara Mohawk Rate Case filing (Cases 17-E-0238 and 17-G-0239) 
and the filings in these cases, the total planned budget for the GBE Program increased from $458.1 
million to $515.4 million. Tr. 3422, lines 13-16.  This amount excludes $20.1 million of GBE 
Program development costs incurred by the Companies for which they are not seeking recovery.

19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
33 of 310 
Tr. 3422, lines 16-19.  Since the 2017 Niagara Mohawk Rate Case, National Grid has completed 
the procurement process as well as launched the Detailed Design and Delivery Phase of the GBE 
Program, which firmed up the program spending details. Tr. 3423, lines 8-10.  The implementation 
of the project has also revealed unanticipated program complexities and upward cost pressures due 
to several factors, including challenges replacing aged systems and developing legacy system 
interfaces, increased training and software costs and an underestimation of AFUDC. Tr. 3423,

The SITP’s general sense of a “reasonable budget” is not informed by any finding that the 
Companies’ efforts to implement the GBE Program are unreasonable.  Further, as the SITP

large information technology project or program.  Tr. 1054-55, lines 25 and 2-9.  The Companies 
agree the costs of the GBE Program are significant; however, they are being incurred so the

foundation for enhanced capabilities that support operational and performance improvements.  The

savings to be delivered once the GBE Program is fully implemented are projected to significantly

and 1-3; Exhibit 56 (CJC-5).  Staff’s proposed $57.4 million downward adjustment is not based 
on any factual support or expertise and the Commission should reject it.

d. Staff’s Proposed 25.5 Percent Forecasting Adjustment is Arbitrary 
and Unreasonable, Not Supported by Historical Performance, and 
Would Result in Disallowing Prudent Costs Incurred for Projects 
Needed to Provide Safe and Adequate Service 
To achieve its recommended Rate Year IT cost recovery, Staff first removes the entire CIS 
project capital investment amount from the case, and then applies an additional 25.5 percent 
($44.214 million) downward “forecasting adjustment” to the remaining Rate Year IT capital 
budget.  Exhibit 519, p. 1 of 6.  Thus, Staff recommends that the Companies’ Rate Year IT capital 
budget be based on total spending of $129.396 million, compared to the $243.846 million budget 
supported by the Companies—a $114.45 million, or 47 percent, reduction.  Id.   
To arrive at this 25.5 percent adjustment, Staff considered National Grid’s actual and 
budgeted IT capital expenditures for fiscal years (“FY”) 2015 through 2019.  Staff selected four 
of the five years of results and applied a linear regression analysis to predict what the FY 2021 
capital budget should be.  Tr. 996-97, lines 15-24 and 1-17.  Staff intentionally excluded the 


--- Page 41 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
36 of 310 
FY2017 results from its analysis—the mid-year of the five-year range Staff reviewed, and the only 
year in that period where actual spend exceeded the budget.  Staff used only those years where the 
IT capital spend was below the budget for its regression analysis.  Tr. 1030-31, lines 10-25 and 2.   
Contrary to Staff’s approach to setting the IT capital budget of looking at past spend and 
predicting what future spend should be, the budgets National Grid proposed are based largely on 
a bottom-up determination of individual projects that the Companies plan to invest in before the 
end of the Rate Year, including the CIS and SAP S/4 HANA projects.  Tr. 889, lines 4-12.  The 
project-by-project budget is set forth in Exhibit 280 (ITP-4CU).  Although the Companies do not 
support Staff’s historic regression analysis to establish the future IT capital budget, there is no 
dispute that if Staff had used actual results from the entire five-year period, the regression analysis 
would have performed differently and would have produced a higher predicted Rate Year value.  
Tr. 893, lines 11-18, Exhibit 341 (ITP-1R), p. 2 of 2.  
Staff’s proposed forecasting adjustment also is not supported by the historical results that 
Staff says it considered.  National Grid’s actual annual spending over the five-year period FY2015-
FY2019 was on average eight percent below budget, not 25.5 percent.  Tr. 893-94, lines 21-22 and 
1-8.  This eight percent average annual underspend equates to approximately $8.8 million each

f. The Service Company’s Capital Structure And Long Term Cost of 
Debt Should Be Used To Determine The Service Company Asset 
Recovery Charge 
The Service Company owns a number of assets that are used either to provide service to 
affiliates or are used by affiliates on a shared basis.  Tr. 5219.  These assets include IT equipment 
and software.  Id.  When the Service Company owns the assets, it charges affiliates, including the 
Companies, an asset recovery charge that includes the pre-tax return on the asset net of deferred 
taxes.  Id.  The Service Company’s return is based on its capital structure which consists of 50 
percent long-term debt and 50 percent equity.  Id.  The Service Company’s long-term debt is priced 
at 3.7049 percent.  Tr. 5246.  All parties agree that the Service Company cost of equity should be 
identical to that determined for the Companies in these proceedings.  Tr. 5254, 1005. 
The SITP proposes that the SFP’s recommended weighted average cost of capital should 
be used for the Service Company return.  Tr. 1005.  Their recommendation unreasonably ignores 
the facts that (i) the Service Company maintains its own capital structure separate and distinct from 
the Companies, and (ii) the Service Company’s long-term debt is lower than the Companies’ costs.  
Tr. 5224.  The Service Company’s capital structure and costs of long-term debt are conservative 
and should be sued for ratemaking purposes in these proceedings. 
g. Staff Recommends Certain IT-Related Process Changes That Are 
Unnecessary And Would Impair National Grid’s Ability To 
Manage The IT Organization And Investment Plan     
 
The SITP proposes several process and reporting changes for KEDNY and KEDLI that the 
Companies agree with.  For example, the Companies agree to provide periodic and annual 
reporting on their IT programs as was adopted for Niagara Mohawk in Cases 17-E-0238 and 17-


--- Page 44 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
39 of 310 
G-0239.  Tr. 904-05, lines 1-20 and 1-15.  The Companies also agree to include enhanced reporting 
on the progress of cyber security projects through National Grid’s investment sanctioning process.  
Tr. 903, lines 13-18.  However, the Companies oppose Staff’s recommendation that the Companies 
develop performance indicators relating to sanction papers.  Tr. 1015-16, lines 22-24 and 1.   
 
As the Companies’ IT Panel testified, the metrics Staff proposes would require substantial 
resources and time to develop and track.  Tr. 903, lines 4-6.  Further, Staff does not explain what 
value the information it seeks to track would provide even if the Companies could readily measure 
it.  As stated above, the Companies’ have agreed to comprehensive information reporting and 
periodic meetings with Staff regarding their IT programs.  Given the resources it would take to try 
to capture such metrics, and the absence of any identified value to be gained from such information 
even if it could be captured, there is no basis for adopting such a metric.  The context of a litigated 
one-year rate case further supports rejecting Staff’s recommendation to impose such a metric.22   
 
Staff also recommends that in future rate filings, the Companies proposed IT capital 
budgets be supported by sanction papers, that National Grid’s Chief Information Officer (“CIO”) 
should attest to the costs and benefits of individual programs and projects that make up the 
approved budget, and that after projects are closed, the CIO should attest to and report on the actual 
costs and benefits achieved through the IT investment plan.  Tr. 3786, lines 1-9.    
 
The Companies oppose these recommendations.  First, as noted above, the Companies have 
agreed to comprehensive reporting on their IT investment plans that would provide Staff with the 
information and transparency to evaluate the reasonableness and status of the Companies’ IT

5. 
Labor (KEDNY and KEDLI) 
a. Management Wage Increase 
i. Staff’s Proposed Management Wage Increase Adjustment Is 
Arbitrary And Unsupported  
Staff recommends disallowing $0.270 million and $0.167 million of operating expenses 
for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively, based on the claim that a 3.0 percent management wage 
increase is “more reasonable” than the actual 3.35 percent increase that was paid to employees in 
July 2019 and the 3.10 percent annual wage increase projected for July 1, 2020.  Tr. 2366, lines 3-
8; Tr. 5275, lines 22-24.  Staff supports its adjustment by citation to the “World at Work” survey, 
which shows a forecast of “Total Salary Budget Increases” of 3.0 percent for the actual 2019 
median and projected 2020 median.  Tr. 5275, lines 17-20.  Staff’s position is without merit and 
should be rejected.      
In these filings, the Companies presented a compensation study showing that National 
Grid’s management compensation is below the market median of a representative peer group of 
utility and non-utility companies’ total compensation.  Exhibit 116 (MPH-2), Schedule 1, Page 1 
of 1.23  Staff performed an independent assessment of the results of the study and concluded that 
National Grid’s total compensation was within the reasonable range of plus or minus 10 percent 
of median total compensation of its similarly situated peers, which Staff acknowledged is the 
standard for determining whether a company’s compensation is market competitive and has been 
used by Staff to evaluate compensation benchmarking results in numerous prior rate cases.  Tr. 
5267-68, lines 22-24 and 1-11; Tr. 5270, lines 13-18.   
                     
23 National Grid’s total compensation is at 96 percent of the median total compensation of the peer group.  
Exhibit 116 (MPH-2), Schedule 1, Page 1 of 1.

Further, the record shows that the 3.35 percent increase paid to employees in July 2019,

career path progressions, does not cause National Grid’s total management compensation to 
exceed the median compensation of the peer group.  Tr. 4945, lines 5-9.  The 3.1 percent annual 
wage increases for July 2020 similarly will not cause National Grid’s total management 
compensation to exceed the median compensation of the peer group.  Id., lines 9-12.  
Specifically, as depicted in Table 1 below, the compensation study shows that National 
Grid’s total cash compensation is $127.31 million compared to the peer group’s median total cash

were increased by 3.35 percent – the wage increase that was paid to employees in July 2019 – the 
result would be $131.57 million, which continues to be below the median cash compensation of 
the peer group.  If National Grid’s total cash compensation of $127.31 million were increased 
further by 6.45 percent – which is 3.35 percent plus 3.1 percent (the wage increase forecast for 
July 2020) – the result would be $135.52 million, which is still below the median cash 
compensation of the peer group.

Lastly, Staff ignored the fact that included within the 3.35 percent increase was a 0.25

contradiction of the evidence – that a 3.0 percent wage increase is more reasonable.  This is 
completely arbitrary and unsupported, and, moreover, seeks to adjust a known and measurable 
change.  For these reasons, the adjustment should be denied. 
b. Vacancy Rates 
i. There Is No Justification For Staff’s Vacancy Adjustment, As The 
Companies’ Labor Expense Forecast Accounts For Vacancies  
Staff proposes imputing a vacancy adjustment to the Companies’ labor expense forecast.  
Tr. 2358, lines 14-16.  The adjustment is specific to full-time equivalents (“FTEs”) who directly 
charge their time to either KEDNY or KEDLI and does not include Service Company employees 
who may allocate their time to KEDNY or KEDLI.  Tr. 2356, lines 13-17.  The basis for this 
adjustment is Staff’s assumption that the Companies do not account for vacancies when forecasting 
Rate Year labor expense.  Id., lines 10-12.  Staff claims a vacancy rate is warranted citing the 
difference in the number of KEDNY and KEDLI FTEs authorized in the 2016 KEDNY and 


--- Page 50 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
45 of 310 
KEDLI Rate Case Order and the number of FTEs at the end of the Historic Test Year in the present 
cases, which is a difference of 48 FTEs for KEDNY and 63 FTEs for KEDLI.  Tr. 2360, lines 7-
17.  Staff’s vacancy adjustment results in a reduction to KEDNY and KEDLI’s Rate Year labor 
expense of $2.759 million and $3.229 million, respectively.  Tr. 2365, lines 20-23.  There are 
several reasons why Staff’s adjustment is without merit and should be denied. 
To forecast Rate Year labor expense, the Companies used as a base the actual employees 
on payroll as of December 31, 2018 (the end of the Historic Test Year).  Tr. 1999, lines 19-21.  By 
using this methodology, the forecast specifically accounts for vacancies that existed as of the end 
of the Historic Test Year.  Tr. 1999-00, lines 17-21 and 1-19.  Indeed, Staff agreed during cross-
examination that the Rate Year forecast includes only those positions that were actually filled as 
of December 31, 2018, and excludes positions that were open or vacant as of that date, even though 
such positions existed.  Tr. 2477-78, lines 16-25 and 2-17.  Specifically, the record shows:

payroll as of December 31st, 2018 to develop the rate year labor

In rebuttal, the Companies submitted evidence showing there were 617 management 
positions and 202 union positions for KEDNY, KEDLI, and the Service Company vacant at the 
end of the Historic Test Year and therefore not reflected in the Rate Year labor expense forecast.  
Tr. 2000-01, lines 5-19 and table 1.  Despite acknowledging that the forecast includes vacancies, 
Staff testified that, in calculating their adjustment, they did not add back the vacancies that existed 
as of December 31, 2018.  Tr. 2480, lines 3-7.  This is clearly unreasonable and a double-count of 
vacant positions. 
It is unclear if Staff’s proposed vacancy adjustment was an intentional double count or 
Staff was simply confused as to how the forecast was developed.  While National Grid has 
consistently used the same methodology to forecast labor expense over the last three rate 
proceedings dating back to 2012, this is the first time that Staff has ever proposed a vacancy 
adjustment.  Tr. 2003, lines 1-8.  Staff’s response to various IRs appears to show some confusion 
on their part.  In IR NG-07, the Companies asked if it was Staff’s position that there were no 
vacancies included in the December 31, 2018 headcount.  Exhibit 636.  Staff responded that “the 
Companies did not account for vacancies from the end of the Historic Test Year, through the Rate 
Year” in developing the labor expense forecast.  Id.  In that same response, however, Staff 
acknowledged that “the Companies based their Rate Year labor forecast on the actual number of 
employees on the payroll at December 31, 2018.”  Id.  Subsequently, in the response to IR NG-42,

c. New Hire True Up 
i. Staff’s Proposed New Hire True Up Is Unwarranted In A One-
Year Litigated Case 
In addition to their vacancy adjustment, Staff proposes to “continue” the downward-only 
new hire true-up that was set forth in the Joint Proposal in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate 
Cases.  Tr. 2379-80, lines 21-24 and 1-2.  This recommendation should be rejected as there is no 
basis for a downward-only true up of labor expense, especially in a one-year litigated proceeding.  
The new hire true-up was agreed to as part of the comprehensive settlement establishing a multi-
year rate plan in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.  Tr. 2493, lines 20-23.  A similar 
reconciliation was not included in the Joint Proposal in the 2017 NMPC Rate Case.  Tr. 2011, lines 
8-13.  The Companies are unaware of any Commission precedent authorizing a downward-only 
true up in a one-year case.  Therefore, Staff’s proposal should be denied.     
d. Energy Efficiency FTEs 
In their initial proposal in this case, the Companies proposed adding seven incremental 
FTEs to support their significantly expanded energy efficiency portfolio design and 
implementation.  Tr. 52-53, lines 19-20 and 1-10.  Staff agreed it is reasonable for the Companies 
to add the seven proposed incremental FTEs. Tr. 680-81, lines 23-24 and 1-2.  In support of its 
position, Staff noted that: “[t]he Companies are significantly ramping up energy efficiency-related 
initiatives. Specifically, the KEDNY energy efficiency target increases by 32% in 2019 and 55%

19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
50 of 310 
in 2020 and for KEDLI the energy efficiency target increases by 26% in 2019 and 45% in 2020.”

As noted in the Companies’ Supplemental Testimony filed January 29, 2020, the NE:NY 
Order “ramps up the energy efficiency targets by 114 percent and 22 percent for KEDLI and 
KEDNY, respectively for 2021 through 2025.”  Tr. 229, lines 16.  To enable them to meet the 
increased energy efficiency targets in the NE:NY Order, the Companies proposed adding eight

additional FTEs was explained by the Companies in testimony (Tr. 230-32).  The addition of the 
eight incremental FTEs would begin in the Rate Year and would be staggered over time.  Id.  
Adding these FTEs is vital to enabling the Companies to meet the targets of the NE:NY Order and 
achieve the State’s clean energy goals, and should be approved. 
6. 
Transportation Expense – Forecast of Vehicle Replacements (KEDNY 
and KEDLI) 
Staff proposes a downward adjustment of $0.619 million for KEDNY and $0.559 million 
for KEDLI to the lease component of transportation expense.  Tr. 2374, lines 8-20.  The adjustment 
is based on Staff’s belief that the Rate Year costs are “excessive” because the Company forecasts 
replacing 180 and 163 more vehicles for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively, from the end of the 
Historic Test Year through the end of the Rate Year than it had during the prior three-year period.  
Tr. 2369, lines 9-18; Tr. 2371, lines 2-5.  Staff’s adjustment should be rejected.   
a. Staff’s Adjustment Unreasonably Ignores The Companies’ Detailed 
Fleet Plan 
Specifically, the record demonstrates that the Companies’ forecasts are supported by a 
detailed fleet plan that carefully considers the type of vehicle, the age of the vehicle, maintenance 
history, and work for which the vehicle is utilized to develop a projected replacement date for each 
vehicle.  Tr. 2004, lines 15-18.  Staff has submitted no evidence that rebuts or contradicts the 


--- Page 56 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
51 of 310 
reasonableness of the Companies’ replacement schedule, and instead unreasonably substitutes its 
own judgement in place of the Companies’ detailed fleet plan, which is used to manage the 
reliability of the fleet of vehicles used to deliver safe and reliable service to customers throughout 
downstate New York.   
In the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, Staff proposed a combined adjustment of 
$1.671 million in transportation lease expense, which was reflected in the Joint Proposal adopted 
by the Commission.  Tr. 2004, lines 20-21; Tr. 2256, lines 1-3.  To manage vehicle replacements 
within this rate allowance, the Companies modified their vehicle replacement schedule to stretch 
out the lifecycle of certain vehicles.  Tr. 2005, lines 1-10; Tr. 2256, lines 3-13.  This adjustment 
increased the average age of vehicles that are needed to provide service to the Companies’ 
customers and help maintain and operate the gas network.  Id.  KEDNY and KEDLI estimate that 
replacements of approximately 88 and 96 vehicles, respectively, were delayed by Staff’s prior 
adjustment and therefore included in the forecast of replacements in these rate proceedings.   
While Staff opines that it is “inappropriate” to assume that vehicles with expiring leases 
will be replaced in the Rate Year because the Companies in the past have used vehicles past their 
lease dates, Staff inappropriately assumes that a management practice adopted to address a one-
time circumstance can be continued indefinitely.  Tr. 2371, lines 5-10.  If Staff’s adjustment were 
adopted, the Companies’ ability to maintain a reliable fleet would be compromised.  Tr. 2005-06, 
lines 13-20 and 1-18.  The lease end date is based on the expected lifecycle of each vehicle.  Id.  
While the timing of replacements varies depending on the specific conditions of the vehicles (e.g., 
depending on the performance or usage of the vehicle, the asset life may fall short of or exceed the 
planned lifecycle), in general each vehicle with a lease term that has expired should be replaced to 
ensure reliable fleet operations.  Id.  While the Companies were able to increase the average age 


--- Page 57 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
52 of 310 
of their fleet over the past three years to stay within the budgeted rate allowance without unduly 
compromising reliability, this cannot continue indefinitely if the Companies hopes to maintain a 
reliable vehicle fleet.  Id.  The Companies need to replace more vehicles than they have in the past 
three years to restore the reliability of the vehicle fleet to its optimal level given the adjustments 
made by Staff in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.  Id.  A further adjustment in these 
proceedings would be unwarranted. 
In sum, the Companies do not believe that deviating from the planned lifecycle replacement 
is reasonable or in the best interests of customers, as it will lead to higher maintenance costs and 
potentially compromise the integrity of KEDNY and KEDLI’s vehicle fleet.   Tr. 2006, lines 9-
13; Tr. 2257, lines 10-14.  Staff has submitted no evidence to contradict the soundness of the 
Companies’ fleet replacement plan.  For these reasons, Staff’s adjustment must be denied. 
7. 
Uncollectible Accounts (KEDNY and KEDLI) 
a. The Commission Should Forecast Uncollectible Expense Using the 
Three-Year Average As Recommended By The Companies 
In their direct testimony, the Companies calculated their net-write off rates using a three-
year aggregated average ratio of uncollectible amounts to total regulated revenues based on the 
three calendar year periods ending December 2016, 2017, and 2018 (the Historic Test Year).  Tr. 
1109-10.  This resulted in net write-off rates of 0.9142 percent and 0.6341 percent in the Rate Year 
for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively.  Tr. 1110, lines 1-11; Exhibit 192, Schedule 1; Exhibit 193, 
Schedule 1.  The Companies used these rates to develop the Rate Year uncollectible expense 
forecast of $16.23 million for KEDNY and $7.31 million for KEDLI.  Tr. 1112, lines 1-6. 
Staff proposes to use the three-year period ending May 2019 to calculate the net write-off 
rates.  Tr. 2552, lines 7-16.  Staff asserts that use of this data will produce a more accurate level of 
uncollectible expense in the Rate Year as it will incorporate programs recently implemented by 


--- Page 58 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
53 of 310 
the Companies, including targeted outbound calling campaigns, email campaigns, and an outbound 
calling group, that will reduce uncollectible expense in the Rate Year.  Tr. 2552-54.  If adopted, 
Staff’s proposal would result in decreases to the Companies’ forecast of uncollectible expense in 
the Rate Year of $1.081 million and $0.442 million for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively.  Tr. 
2551, lines 7-10. 
Staff’s proposal should be rejected as the Companies’ efforts to reduce collections expense 
are already reflected in the Companies’ calculation and the three-year period proposed by Staff is 
not aligned to the Rate Year.  While the Companies may continue these and other collections-
related programs during the Rate Year, the mere operation of such programs is not a guarantee that 
the Companies will be actually able to further reduce this expense, as the expense is entirely reliant 
upon external factors beyond the Companies’ control – the customers’ ability to pay their bills.  
Indeed, while the Companies’ efforts have moderated uncollectible expense over the past few 
years, this expense experiences annual variability and is actually projected to increase due to the 
Companies’ recent reduction in the number of accounts terminated for non-payment, along with 
the Companies’ recent suspension of collection activities, including service disconnections, to 
assist customers in light of the COVID-19 pandemic.28  Tr. 1107-08.   
Additionally, the Companies’ current rate plans are based on calendar years (i.e. the period 
running from January 1 – December 31) and the Rate Year will be based on the Companies’ fiscal 
year (i.e. the period running from April 1 – March 31).  Tr. 1176, lines 3-7.  The three-year period 
of data proposed by Staff to forecast uncollectible expense bears no rational relationship to either 
calendar year or fiscal year data, which serve as the points of reference for virtually all other

calculations and targets proposed in these cases.  Staff’s proposal is not tied to a specific triggering 
event and indeed Staff does not propose to use all of the 2019 data.  Rather, Staff randomly selects

8. 
Site Investigation and Remediation (“SIR”) (KEDNY and KEDLI) 
On behalf of the Companies, Charles F. Willard testified regarding the Companies’ SIR 
Programs and the recovery of costs incurred under those Programs.  Tr. 3444-3518.  The 
Companies’ SIR Programs and cost recovery were also addressed by the Staff SIR Panel (Tr. 3519-

to the management, investigation, and remediation of activities at manufactured gas plant (“MGP”) 
and federal Superfund sites.  Tr. 3454-58, 3584-88.  Currently, KEDNY has whole or partial 
responsibility for 27 MGP sites and two federal Superfund Sites.  Tr. 3554-55; Exhibit 172.  
KEDLI is wholly or partially responsible for 29 MGP sites; 16 of these 29 MGP sites are actively 
managed, while the remaining 13 sites have received a “No Further Action” determination with 
no ongoing obligation.  Tr. 3484-85; Exhibit 182.

provide sworn testimony (1) establishing that the remediation process is in 
compliance with existing timetables and DEC requirements, or providing

b. The Commission Should Authorize Full Recovery Of The 
Companies’ Forecast SIR Costs As Set Forth In Mr. Willard’s 
Testimony 
As discussed above, it is undisputed that the Companies are in compliance with all 
applicable regulatory requirements and timetables, including those set by the DEC and EPA for 
the Companies’ SIR sites and by the Commission in the SIR Generic Order.  It is also 
uncontroverted that the Companies have effectively controlled costs related to their SIR Programs.  
As such, the Companies should be permitted to recover their full forecast SIR costs, as proposed 
in Mr. Willard’s testimony. 
Mr. Willard testified that the Companies should be permitted to recover their forecast SIR 
costs in base rates, along with the amortized historic SIR costs as authorized in the 2016 KEDNY 
and KEDLI Rate Cases.  At KEDNY, the requested base rate recovery includes: (i) $66.088 million 
in forecast MGP-related SIR costs; (ii) $20 million associated with the remediation of KEDNY’s 
Superfund sites, Gowanus Canal and Newtown Creek; and (iii) the current amortization of $18.521 
million, representing one-tenth of the forecast deferral balance at December 31, 2016.  Tr. 3472.  
At KEDLI, the requested base rate recovery includes: (i) $6.6 million in forecast MGP-related SIR 
costs; and (ii) the current amortization of $14.168 million, representing one-tenth of the forecast 
deferral balance at December 31, 2016.  Tr. 3492. 
Mr. Willard described the Companies’ process for forecasting SIR costs at their MGP sites 
in the Rate Year.  For MGP sites, because the DEC schedule for the Rate Year is not yet available, 
the Companies used the current approved DEC schedule for fiscal year 2019 and the anticipated 
work for fiscal year 2020 to forecast the scope of work.  The Companies considered the status of 
each site and worked with project managers for the sites to account for site progress and potential 
delays, including delays that KEDLI negotiated with the DEC to best manage program and project 
resources.  After determining the scope of work, the Companies forecast the cost of the work,

taking into account the nature of work and expected remedy, site specific conditions, past

Tr. 3546.  Although the Staff SIR Panel recognized the Companies’ effort to account for potential
