recommended downward adjustments to Rate Year SIR expense of $15.168 million for KEDNY 
and $1.734 million at KEDLI.  Tr. 3551.  
The Companies submit that the Commission should adopt the Companies’ Rate Year SIR 
forecasts, and reject Staff’s use of a projected four year average, for several reasons.  As discussed 
by Mr. Willard, the DEC only approves a one-year schedule covering the current fiscal year, with 
a less formal discussion of “the anticipated logical progression of work at each site for an additional 
year.”  Tr. 3456-57.  As such, the Companies’ forecast for the current fiscal year benefit from a 
clearly defined, DEC-approved scope of work, while future years are more speculative.  As such, 
the Commission should rely on the Companies’ forecasts, which rely on these more certain, 
approved work schedules, rather than Staff’s projections that take into account more speculative 
schedules in future years.  Mr. Willard testified in his rebuttal testimony that “[b]ased on the latest 
available information, the Companies believe that key work activities driving the Companies’ 
forecast of SIR expenditures during the Rate Year will occur,” and, therefore, the Companies’ 
forecast should be used.  Tr. 3501.   
Further, Staff’s stated objective to “levelize” forecasts and “stabilize rate impacts” could 
be undermined by the use of a four-year average.  Staff believes that its four-year average will 


--- Page 65 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
60 of 310 
stabilize bill impacts because it assumes SIR costs will be lower than forecast in the Rate Year.  
However, as the Staff SIR Panel notes, “SIR costs are subject to a high degree of variability, which 
leads to a high degree of risk that actual costs could be more or less than anticipated.”  Tr. 3548-
49 (emphasis added).  The Companies’ SIR costs are subject to full deferral and reconciliation.  
Tr. 3473, 3492-93.  Should actual SIR costs be more than anticipated, for which Staff 
acknowledges there is a “high degree of risk,” future customers could experience increased bill 
impacts resulting from the recovery of the difference through the reconciliation mechanism.  
Further, SIR costs significantly above the Rate Year forecasts could trigger the SIR Recovery 
Surcharge at KEDNY, resulting in further bill impacts.  Tr. 3460-61.  As Staff’s four-year average 
relies on less reliable work schedules, and does not account for the significant risks in increases in 
SIR costs, it should be rejected.  The Commission should instead approve the Companies’ proposal 
to recover its forecast Rate Year SIR costs.

Companies’ Share Of Earning In Excess Of Its ROE To Reduce SIR

PULP Witness Yates further recommends that the Commission require KEDNY to “use 
100% of its share of any earnings-sharing mechanism adopted in these proceedings…to reduce 
deferred [SIR] costs.”  Tr. 1629.  This recommendation should be rejected.  As discussed below, 
the Companies oppose the adoption of an earnings sharing mechanism.  However, even if an 
earnings sharing mechanism is adopted, the SIR Generic Order clearly provides that sharing of 
SIR costs is not appropriate in this case.  As Mr. Yates notes, the SIR Generic Order “identified 
two circumstances in which sharing of SIR costs may be appropriate.”  Tr. 1630.  The first is “as 
an incentive to constrain SIR costs for utilities that appear to need such an incentive.”30  The second 
                     
30 SIR Generic Order at 24.

is “as part of the negotiation for rate plans where an earnings sharing mechanism is

9. 
Savings (KEDNY and KEDLI) 
a. The Revenue Requirements Reflect $50 Million In Forecast Savings 
From An Ambitious Savings Program Undertaken By The 
Companies To Reduce Costs For Customers – A Program That 
Goes Above and Beyond the Commission’s Policy On Efficiencies 
In A One-Year Litigated Case 
 
The Commission’s policy with respect to savings opportunities is well established: 
Our established policy requires that utilities subject to our jurisdiction 
pursue any and all opportunities available to them to improve operating 
efficiency.  Absent clear and convincing evidence which indicates that 
potential productivity improvements have been factored into a company’s 
forecast of rate year operations, we apply a small but reasonable 
productivity adjustment to account for additional rate year operating 
efficiency that reasonably can be expected to be achieved.32   
 
 
The Commission’s productivity adjustment is based on the premise that well-run companies 
should be able to find a modest measure of efficiencies in the rate year.33  The adjustment is 
                     
31 Id. 
32 Case 27567, Spring Valley Water Company, Opinion No. 80-16 (issued and effective April 24, 1980) 
(“Spring Valley Water”). 
33 Case 07-G-0141, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and 
Regulations of National Fuel Gas Distribution Corporation for Gas Service, Order Establishing Rates for 
Gas Service (issued and effective December 21, 2007) at 8.

typically calculated as one percent of total labor expense and payroll taxes in recognition of how 
difficult it is to achieve savings year after year.  Tr. 2014, lines 12-14; Tr. 2511 lines 4-6.34   
In these cases, the traditional productivity adjustment would result in efficiencies totaling 
$3.894 million and $2.020 million for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively.  Tr. 2014, lines 14-15; 
Tr. 2264, lines 18-20; Exhibits 304 and 314, Schedule 28.  The Companies, however, have 
challenged themselves to deliver an even greater level of efficiencies through an ambitious effort 
known as the Accelerate Program.  Tr. 1890.  As a result of these efforts, the Companies’ revenue 
requirements reflect more than $35 million in savings for KEDNY and approximately $15 million 
for KEDLI – or a total of approximately $50 million in savings that the Companies would need to 
achieve in the Rate Year.  Tr. 2014, lines 16-18; Tr. 2264-65, lines 20-21 and 1.  This level of 
savings is more than eight times (for KEDNY) and seven times (for KEDLI) the Commission’s 
traditional productivity adjustment, demonstrating the efforts the Companies have undertaken 
above and beyond the traditional one percent in efficiencies that the Commission’s policy 
encourages.  Id.   
Despite this unprecedented level of savings, Staff proposes imputing more than $13 million 
in additional savings associated with aspirational initiatives in the Accelerate Program35 that the 
record reflects have not and may never be implemented.  Tr. 2393, lines 10-15.  This imputation 
would require the Companies to achieve approximately $63 million in savings in a single year – 
that has already begun.  If the Commission were to adopt Staff’s position, it would equate to

b. The Accelerate Program Contains Detailed Implementation Levels 
To Provide Visibility And Transparency To The Level Of Savings 
That Can Reasonably Be Expected to Be Achieved In The Rate 
Year 
The Accelerate Program is a three-year efficiency challenge undertaken by National Grid 
in FY 2019 to enable ongoing critical investments, while mitigating customers’ energy costs in a 
manner that maintains affordability.  Tr. 1891, lines 6-11; Tr. 1892, lines 4-16.  To that end, 
National Grid set an aspirational target of a 20 percent productivity improvement in O&M and 
capital expenses across the U.S. business by FY 2021.  Id.  National Grid purposefully set an 
ambitious target in the interest of driving maximum customer value, and it always was and still is 
uncertain whether this level of efficiencies can be achieved.  Tr. 1891, lines 13-21; Tr. 1892, lines 
1-2; Tr. 2015, lines 4-13.  Indeed, in even a mature business it is challenging to achieve the 
Commission’s standard one percent productivity adjustment – let alone efficiencies of the 
magnitude reflected in the instant rate filings.  Tr. 1891, lines 15-19.   
To provide transparency to the business and regulators, National Grid implemented a web-
based tool, known as Wave, to track and report progress on the Accelerate Program initiatives.  Tr. 
1893, lines 8-13; Tr. 2017-18, lines 12-20 and 1-2.  Each Accelerate Program initiative is placed 
at an implementation level (“IL”) based on its stage of development, from purely an idea (IL 0) to 
fully implemented (IL 5).  Tr. 1893, lines 17-19.  ILs 0 through 2 cover the stages of idea 
formulation to an initial estimate of potential benefits.  Tr. 1893, lines 19-20.  In these stages, 
teams brainstorm initiatives to be evaluated as opportunities to deliver process improvements, 


--- Page 69 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
64 of 310 
assigning a rough estimate of the potential benefit as an initial proxy until better information is 
developed to quantify the benefit.  Tr. 1893-94, lines 20-21 and 1-2.  Teams are encouraged to 
include as many ideas as possible.  Tr. 1894, lines 2-4.  As such, many initiatives do not move 
beyond IL 2.  Id.  The potential benefit becomes more refined when an initiative advances to IL 3, 
where National Grid’s Finance function validates that the initiative has the potential to deliver 
savings, and the business puts together a plan to implement the initiative.  Tr. 1894, lines 4-13.  
An IL 3 benefit is still aspirational, however, as the initiative may not be implemented for a variety 
of reasons.  Id.  For example, an initiative could require a new contract to be negotiated with a 
vendor or the implementation of a new IT solution; without the new contract or IT solution, the 
feasibility or economics of the initiative may be impacted.  Id.  Similarly, in the process of 
implementing the initiative, the business could realize that assumptions have changed and 
therefore the initiative cannot be implemented.  Id. 
It is not until IL 4 where an initiative moves from conceptual to deliverable.  Tr. 1894, lines 
15-19.  In IL 4, while savings have not been realized, they are projected to be realized because the 
business has completed all activities required to deliver the initiative.  Id.  In IL 5, Finance has 
confirmed that savings have been realized and the actual amount of the savings, which may have 
changed from the original estimate.  Id.   
The Companies’ revenue requirements reflect savings associated with ILs 4 and 5 
initiatives, as these are reasonably achievable and likely to deliver benefits to customers in the 
Rate Year.  Tr. 1895, lines 3-5.  Given the uncertainty associated with initiatives in ILs 0 through 
3, the Companies did not make a discrete adjustment to the revenue requirements for these 
initiatives because it is simply unknown whether they will be implemented, and, if they are, the 
extent to which any savings will materialize.  Tr. 1895, lines 5-8.  Instead, while a productivity 


--- Page 70 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
65 of 310 
adjustment would not be warranted under the Commission’s policy given the “clear and 
convincing”36 evidence of efficiency opportunities reflected in the Rate Year, the Companies 
nevertheless included the traditional one percent productivity adjustment to account for the 
possibility that some of these initiatives could move into ILs 4 or 5 in the Rate Year.  Tr. 1897, 
lines 16-21.  The Companies submit that this was a reasonable approach.  The combined level of 
savings reflected in the filing in the Rate Year is:

In support of their position, Staff claims that the IL 3 initiatives are “identified and

rejected, as will be discussed.  Additionally, Staff proposes to disallow the costs to achieve, which 
are de minimis compared to the overall savings reflected.  Tr. 2385, lines 13-15.   
c. Staff’s Position That The Savings Associated With IL 3 Initiatives 
Are Identified And Quantified Is Based On Speculation And Not 
Supported By The Evidentiary Record 
There is no meaningful evidentiary basis for the Commission to adopt Staff’s position.  
Staff’s position is essentially that because the Company identified initiatives and assigned them an 
aspirational savings estimate, these estimates should be imputed in the revenue requirement.  
Staff’s position is built around the possibility that IL 3 initiatives could move to ILs 4 or 5 in the 
Rate Year.  This position ignores two critical facts, however.  That is, the IL 3 initiatives have not 


--- Page 72 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
67 of 310 
been implemented to date and it is unknown if they will ever be implemented – and the Companies 
included the one percent productivity adjustment to account for this unknown.  Staff’s position is 
based on nothing more than speculation and conjecture.  Indeed, as will be discussed below, during 
cross-examination, aside from repeating over and over their “belief” that IL 3 initiatives will 
deliver savings in the Rate Year, the record demonstrates that Staff’s belief is not based on any 
evidence or analysis of the initiatives in the current IL 3 queue.  Tr. 2521-25.  In the absence of 
evidence that the IL 3 initiatives can be achieved, which the Companies submit should be the same 
“clear and convincing” standard used by the Commission to avoid application of the traditional 
productivity adjustment, Staff’s proposed adjustment should be denied.  To hold otherwise would 
penalize the Companies for undertaking ambitious and innovative steps designed to reduce costs 
for customers.   
The first reason cited by Staff in support of imputing the IL 3 initiatives is that National 
Grid used the phrase “estimates sound enough to ‘take to the bank’ in budget” to describe the level 
of estimate required for an initiative to be moved to IL 3.  Tr. 2388-89, lines 23-24 and lines 1-13; 
Exhibit 504 (SRRP-3), at 362 of 943.  That phrase, however, refers only to the quality of the 
aspirational savings estimate in IL3 – not to whether the initiatives can be implemented.  As 
explained above, for an initiative to move to IL 3, National Grid’s Finance function must review 
the estimate to provide assurance there is some basis for cost reductions if the initiative can 
successfully be delivered.  Tr. 1894, lines 4-13.  As the Companies explained in the response to 
IR DPS-755 (Exhibit 504 (SRRP-3), at 618 of 943), National Grid has limited resources and

initiative.  Id.  This does not change the undisputed fact, however, that the IL 3 initiatives have not

A second reason Staff provides in support of imputing the IL 3 savings is that “once an

that Staff calculated pertained to initiatives that were undertaken early in the Accelerate Program.  
Tr. 2018, lines 10-15.  These initiatives moved quickly because they were already in flight at the 
beginning of the Program.  Id.  Now that the Accelerate Program is past its initial period, it is 
becoming harder and harder for initiatives to deliver savings as the business has moved from 
initiatives that were easier to implement to ones that are more challenging.  Tr. 2018, lines 10-19.

initiatives have been stalled in IL 3, it is highly unlikely they will progress to implementation and 
deliver the aspirational savings estimated in the Rate Year.  Tr. 2019, lines 2-5.

initiatives they are proposing to impute have been in IL 3.  Exhibit 635 (part 1), pages 534-42.  
The response showed that these initiatives have remained in IL 3 on average 154 days.  Id. at 537.  
The Companies submit a monthly response to IR DPS-757 showing the status of initiatives in IL3.

Importantly, the inability to progress the current IL 3 initiatives is not due to any lack of 
effort on the Companies’ part.  Rather, it is the normal course of any aspirational program such as 
the Accelerate Program.  The program starts strong as the business identifies ways to enhance 
performance and reduce costs.  As the program progresses, it naturally becomes harder and harder 
to find further efficiencies as ideas are exhausted – especially given the level already reflected in 
these filings.44  This is precisely why the Commission’s policy on efficiencies is one percent.  To 
reduce costs of the magnitude the Companies proposed in these filings requires major changes to 
people, processes, and systems.  Tr. 2015-2016, lines 15-21 and 1-3.  There are risks savings will 
not materialize despite best efforts due to competing priorities such as incremental work, new 
regulatory requirements, or unforeseen circumstances such as the downstate supply constraints and 
COVID-19 – the latter of which has shut down all but essential operations, further cementing the 
inability to achieve the IL 3 savings that Staff is proposing to impute.  Id.   
In IR DPS-1026, Staff asked the Companies to provide the reasons why each initiative has 
been in IL 3 longer than 100 days and the current estimate for when the initiatives will move to IL 
4.  Exhibit 635 (part 1), IR DPS-1026, pages 544-47.  In each case, the Companies explained the 
reasons and made clear that it was simply unknown when the initiatives would move.  Id.  The 
reasons included: 
• Contract negotiations were unsuccessful;

Moreover, it should be noted that the IL 3 savings amounts that Staff are proposing to

e. If Staff’s Position Were Adopted, It Would Chill Future Utility 
Savings Efforts  
By imputing aspirational savings estimates from initiatives that have not come to fruition 
and may never will, Staff is effectively creating a policy that punishes the Companies for looking 
for innovative ways to reduce costs for customers.  The Staff in the recent NYSEG and RG&E rate 
cases (Cases 19-E-0378, et al.) addressed utility savings efforts.  In that case, in deviating from 
the traditional one percent productivity adjustment, Staff specifically referenced the ambitious 
savings initiative undertaken by KEDNY and KEDLI in these cases to support its proposed two 
percent productivity adjustment.45  Specifically, Staff testified: 
Q. 
Do you have any additional comments about your recommended 
productivity adjustment? 
 
                     
45 Cases 19-E-0378, et al,  NYSEG and RG&E, Staff Accounting Panel (September 2019) at 46-50.

A. 
Yes.  In other recent rate cases, utilities have reflected savings from 
ambitious company-initiated programs in revenue requirements for 
the benefit of ratepayers.  The savings reflected proactively in 
revenue requirements by Con Ed, KEDLI, and KEDNY were 
greater than the Commission’s traditional one percent productivity 
adjustment imputation.46  
  
Staff in those proceedings remarked they “would encourage” NYSEG and RG&E to 
consider pursuing such initiatives in the future.47  Staff here, in contrast, argues in favor of a policy 
that essentially penalizes utilities for seeking to improve efficiency.  Rather than encouraging 
utilities to pursue ambitious savings programs, Staff is effectively sending the opposite message 
and discouraging utilities from pursuing such programs that benefit customers.  Utilities should be 
encouraged to be innovative and test concepts without the fear that preliminary and aspirational 
estimates will be held against them.  Otherwise, the Companies would have been better off not 
pursuing the Accelerate Program in the first instance and instead reflecting the Commission’s 
traditional one percent efficiency adjustment in the filings.    
Moreover, Staff’s proposal to include both the IL 3 initiatives and the one percent 
productivity adjustment is a further demonstration of the sheer unreasonableness of their approach.  
Not only does it ignore Commission policy, but it ignores that the Companies included the 
productivity adjustment to account for the IL 3 initiatives.  Staff’s position is therefore a double 
count of the productivity adjustment.48   
In sum, Commission policy is to impute one percent of efficiencies in the absence of 
savings.  The policy is based on the premise that well-run companies should be able to find a

speculation and is unwarranted.  To appreciate the magnitude and sheer unreasonableness of

contrary to Commission policy in a one-year case which encourages a small but reasonable 
imputation and would have a chilling effect on future utility savings efforts.   
f. The Costs to Achieve The Accelerate Program Savings Are 
Prudently Incurred Costs That Should Not Be Disallowed 
Staff’s recommendation to disallow the costs to achieve the Accelerate Program savings 
should be rejected.  Tr. 2384, lines 20-22.  Specifically, the costs to achieve are a de minimis 
$0.845 million for KEDNY and $0.527 million for KEDLI compared to the overall $50 million in 
savings reflected in the revenue requirements.  Tr. 2385, lines 13-15.  It is undisputed that these 
costs were incurred to deliver an unprecedented level of savings that lowers the revenue 
requirements for customers.  In the 2010 Niagara Mohawk electric rate case, Staff testified: 
Q. 
Please explain why you recommend, as an alternative, removing 
both the savings and the costs for these initiatives from the rate year 
forecast. 
 
A. 
. . .. [I]f the rate year costs estimates are removed, the expected 
associated savings should not be reflected.  It is not reasonable to exclude 
costs and include expected savings, or vice versa.49 
 
It is simply unreasonable to disallow the costs without also removing the savings. 
                     
49 Case 10-E-0050, Niagara Mohawk – Electric Rates, Evidentiary Transcript dated September 14, 2010, 
Tr. 2852, lines 13-16; Tr. 2854, lines 3-7 (DMM # 299).  


--- Page 83 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
78 of 310 
Further, the reasons cited by Staff are unavailing.  The first reason cited is that the costs to 
achieve are one-time in nature.  Tr. 2386, lines 2-4.  The Companies are unaware of any 
Commission precedent disallowing costs to achieve because they were incurred on a one-time 
basis to enable a utility to deliver savings over a multi-year period.  In fact, such a position would 
be illogical and serve as a disincentive for utility efforts to identify savings.  The second reason is 
that the Companies booked the costs to achieve to expense.  Id., lines 4-7.  This argument fails 
because all expenses incurred in the Historic Test Year were booked to expense.  The third and 
final reason is that the Companies are not sharing with customers any savings that were achieved 
before the Rate Year.  Id., lines 13-18.  This position also has no merit, as the requirement 
requirements fully reflect the savings at the same level (plus inflation) that were incurred in the 
Historic Test Year.   
For all these reasons, Staff’s disallowance should be denied.   
10. 
Paving Expense – Downward Only Deferral (KEDNY Only) 
a. There Is No Basis For A Downward Only Paving Expense Deferral 
The record reflects agreement between the Companies and Staff on the level of Rate Year 
paving expense reflected in the revenue requirements.  Tr. 2025, lines 9-12.  The only dispute 
relates to Staff’s proposal to implement a downward-only paving expense deferral for KEDNY.  
The basis for Staff’s recommendation is that the Historic Test Year is the first full year of the new 
paving regulations in New York City and therefore, according to Staff, there is some uncertainty 
regarding these costs.  Tr. 2397-98, lines 13-24 and 1-3.  Additionally, Staff alleges that a 
downward-only deferral would incent KEDNY to manage its costs although there is no evidence 
in the record to demonstrate that KEDNY has been inefficient or imprudent in managing its paving 
expense.  Tr. 2398, lines 3-7. 


--- Page 84 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
79 of 310 
Staff’s recommendation should be rejected, as there is no basis for the Commission to 
establish a downward-only paving expense deferral.  Staff remarked several times throughout its 
pre-filed testimony opposing the few symmetrical true-ups proposed by the Companies (e.g., 
property tax, CSC O&M expense) that deferrals are generally not authorized in a one-year rate 
case.  Tr. 2583-84, lines 21-24 and 1-12; Tr. 4701, lines 5-20.  The main reason cited by Staff for 
the need for a paving expense deferral – namely, that there is only one year of data reflecting the 
impact of the new paving regulations – holds equally true to support the need for a full 
reconciliation of paving costs instead of a downward-only reconciliation.  In that regard, it cannot 
be ignored that KEDNY’s actual paving expenses in the Historic Test Year ($37.672 million) 
exceeded the rate allowance ($9.088 million) by more than $27 million.  Tr. 2531, lines 2-8; 
Exhibit 504 (SRRP-3), page 324 of 943.   
Under Staff’s proposal, if paving costs again exceed the rate allowance, KEDNY would be 
precluded from recovering its prudently incurred costs.  Accordingly, if the Commission believes 
a reconciliation is required, KEDNY submits that a full reconciliation should be adopted to avoid 
a situation whereby KEDNY would again incur a material level of paving costs above the rate 
allowance with no right to defer those costs.   
11. 
Pipeline Integrity Management Programs 
The Companies’ transmission pipeline integrity management program (“IMP”) is a safety 
program mandated by the Pipeline Safety Improvement Act of 2002 and corresponding safety 
regulations administered by the U.S. Department of Transportation (“DOT”), Pipeline and 
Hazardous Materials Safety Administration (“PHMSA”).  Tr. 4415, lines 11-13.  The IMP 
identifies and addresses potential issues affecting the physical soundness of the Companies’ gas 
pipeline facilities before they become safety or performance issues.  Id., lines 13-15.  Under the 
IMP framework dictated by PHMSA (49 CFR 192), the Companies conduct baseline and periodic 


--- Page 85 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
80 of 310 
reassessments of transmission facilities to identify and evaluate potential threats to “Covered 
Segments” of pipelines, i.e., transmission pipelines that could affect High Consequence Areas 
(areas where a pipeline failure could have significant adverse consequences) and remediate defects 
discovered during such assessments.  In regions of the United States where older gas distribution 
systems are common, including areas of downstate New York served by the Companies, IMPs are 
a key component of gas pipeline safety.  Tr. 4264-4265, line 20 and lines 1-2.  The Companies rate 
filing includes incremental non-labor O&M in the amount of approximately $4.3 million and $2.1 
million for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively, in the Rate Year and an additional 5.5 FTEs in the 
Rate Year to perform the required IMP inspections.  Tr. 4484, lines 4-9; Exhibit 528 (SGIOP-12).  
The O&M costs to perform necessary integrity inspections and assessments include costs for 
excavation and support for external corrosion direct assessment (ECDA), evaluation of testing 
data, non-capital repairs, and on-site material testing.  Tr. 4484, lines 9-12.   
The Staff Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel (“SGIOP”) acknowledges that the 
Companies must adhere to federal gas safety regulations in this area, including the specific IMP 
requirements, and supports the Companies’ integrity management program.  Tr. 4692, line 18.  
Notwithstanding, the SGIOP recommends a 100 percent reduction to the IMP non-labor O&M 
budget in the Rate Year – effectively eliminating the Companies’ funding to perform these 
mandated activities.  Tr. 4548, lines 18-19.  The SGIOP similarly proposes to eliminate the 
incremental positions needed to perform this work.  Tr. 4549, lines 10-11.  The SGIOP explains 
these adjustments are based on its perception that “neither the capital budget, or the OpEx 
workload for the Rate Year is being increased as compared to the most recent FY or the HTY; 
therefore, the existing OpEx budget should be able to support the needs of this work in the Rate 


--- Page 86 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
81 of 310 
Year.”  Tr. 4693, lines 5-10.  The SGIOP’s rationales for totally eliminating the incremental O&M 
funding and FTEs for these mandated programs are misplaced for several reasons.   
Significantly, each year’s O&M budget for the IMP program is based on the Companies’ 
identified workplan and, therefore, is highly variable from year-to-year because different pipeline 
assets of varied size and operating characteristics might be in the work plan in any given year.  Tr. 
4549, lines 1-4.  Merely comparing the number of projects (or “workload”) year-on-year without 
considering the asset size and complexity, among other factors, fails to take into account the actual 
drivers of O&M cost for the IMP program in the Rate Year.   At the same time, the SGIOP’s 
assumption that IMP O&M is somehow proportionally tied to the capital budget is simply 
incorrect.  In fact, the Companies’ Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel (“GIOP”) testified that 
the O&M budget is not tied to the capital IMP program in any given year.  Tr. 4548, lines 22-23.  
Rather, the level of O&M expense is a function of the amount of mandated inspections and records 
reviews performed in a year, not the level of IMP capital improvements.  Tr. 4548-4549, line 23 
and line 1.  The SGIOP’s proposed reduction in O&M for IMP inspections will not allow the 
Companies to meet their regulatory requirements in the Rate Year.  Tr. 4549, lines 5-7.  For the 
same reasons stated above, the FTEs to support IMP/IVP are needed to support the increased O&M 
workload that is expected in the Rate Years and Data Years.  Id., lines 10-11. 
The IMP program is an example where simply looking at prior year’s spending, without 
fully considering the emerging compliance requirements and system needs, is an inadequate basis 
for proposing adjustments.  As the SGIOP acknowledged, the budget for the IMP program is “zero 
based” – meaning the Companies will build the budget from the bottom-up based on the work 
identified in each year.  Tr. 4796-4797, lines 20-25 and lines 1-10.  O&M programs with zero-


--- Page 87 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
82 of 310 
based budgets can experience significant variability year-to-year depending on the project 
identified in the particular year.  
In summary, because the proposed IMP work is mandated by federal safety regulations, 
the GIOP has identified specific projects identified in the Rate Year that the Companies deem 
necessary, and the Staff’s basis for proposed adjustments is misplaced, the SGIOP’s proposed 
adjustments in this area should be rejected. 
12. 
Damage Prevention Contractor Costs  
Damage to gas facilities from third parties digging near pipelines is a major cause of gas 
accidents and a contributing factor to some of the worst incidents in the history of the natural gas 
industry.  In addition to the detrimental impacts on safety and reliability, utilities spend millions 
of dollars every year repairing damage caused by these incidents. Tr. 3734, lines 17-21.  To 
mitigate the risk of third-party damage, the Companies’ Damage Prevention group performs mark 
outs of the Companies’ underground utilities to allow for safe excavation and prevent damage to 
existing facilities, as required by the Commission’s regulations.  Damage prevention mark outs 
are performed when required to support one of the Companies’ projects or when customers, third 
parties, and municipalities contact New York State’s Dig Safe One Call Center.  Tr. 4063-4064, 
lines 8-24 and lines 1-12.  The volume of one-call tickets has increased annually and is expected 
to continue increasing in the Rate Year due to the success of the NY 811 program outreach and 
education and increasing construction activities.  Tr. 3735-3736, lines 19-21 and line 1.  KEDNY 
uses internal employees to locate facilities on company-related jobs and a contractor for all third-
party Dig Safe mark out requests.  Conversely, KEDLI sends all Dig Safe mark out requests, 
whether internal or external, to a contractor to mark out facilities.  The costs associated with 
damage prevention mark outs are included in KEDNY’s O&M expense and KEDLI’s O&M and 
capital expense.  Tr. 4577, lines 17-18. 


--- Page 88 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
83 of 310 
In its initial filing, the Companies increased the damage prevention expense components 
from the Historic Test Year by standard annual inflation to arrive at the Rate Year forecast.  On 
August 19, 2019, the Companies solicited contractor bids for their damage prevention mark out 
work.  Bids were received from contractors on September 16, 2019.  Thereafter, bidders were 
evaluated both commercially and on their ability to execute technical requirements, and the 
Companies subsequently entered into new contracts in 2020.50 
Because the new contracts will raise the labor rates for these services in excess of the 
standard annual inflation rate used to derive the Companies’ Rate Year forecasts, the Companies 
updated their capital and O&M forecasts to include these increased costs.  Tr. 4578, lines 19-21 
and Tr. 4580, lines 1-12.  The increase is attributable to three factors: (i) the prior contractor labor 
rates were based on long-term, dated agreements; (ii) recent unionization of the contractor labor 
force; and (iii) an increased scope of work to incorporate locator assurance technology.  The 
Companies had not bid this work in over a decade.  While the prior agreements provided for 
minimal price increases, the rates paid to those contractors were generally lower than market prices 
at the time. Tr. 4579, lines 2-8.  Additionally, the contractors providing services under the selected 
agreements unionized during the late Spring and early Summer of 2019, which increased labor 
rates significantly as compared to rates reflected in the Companies’ initial filing. Id., lines 9-14.  
In addition, the new agreements require that the contractors use updated technology (locator 
assurance technology that enables real-time tracking of technician performance against industry 
standards to improve overall effectiveness of locating field work) when marking out facilities. This

The Companies’ procurement and contracting activities do not stop because the they are 
engaged in rate cases.  Where, as here, a rate case extends over a significant period of time, 
contracts will expire and need to be renegotiated in the ordinary course of business.  Here, the 
updated costs for damage prevention will have known and measurable implications for the 
Companies’ cost of providing markout service required under the Commission’s regulations in the 
Rate Year and, therefore, are appropriately recovered through rates.       
The Commission’s Test Period Policy Statement provides that the Commission’s goal in 
setting rates is “to ascertain to the best [it can], what the utility's revenues, operating expenses and 
conditions [will be] in the period for which [it is] setting rates.”51  In recognition of the fact that 
                     
51 Test Period Policy Statement at p.3.   


--- Page 90 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
85 of 310 
costs may change over the course of a rate proceeding, the Test Period Policy Statement provides 
that “[r]evisions for known changes in cost rates (such as for wage increases and tax increases), to 
the extent they are material, may be made as late as the Company's initial brief on exceptions.”52 
In this instance, the Companies provided updated information on their contractor costs in 
their Second Supplemental testimony, filed on December 13, 2019.  Staff has, to this point, had 
nearly four months to analyze the adjustments required by the updated damage prevention 
contractor costs, conduct discovery, and cross examine the Companies’ witness panels on the 
reasonableness of these costs.  Staff notes in its testimony that it has, in fact, asked information 
requests on the increased damage prevention costs.  Tr. 3637, lines 4-5.  In the Companies’ 
response to DPS-1068 (Exhibit 510 (SPP-4), dated January 17, 2020), the Companies provided 
information on the contractor bids, the Companies’ evaluation of the bids, a summary of the 
negotiations, and copies of the executed agreements with pricing.  This is exactly the type of 
information the parties would need to assess the reasonableness of these costs.  Thus, there is 
simply no legal or equitable basis for disallowing the Companies’ costs in this area based solely 
on the timing of the Companies’ updated costs – especially when there is a very legitimate reason 
for the timing of the update (i.e., the expiration of the damage prevention contracts).  It is 
indisputable that the increased damage prevention costs from the new contract rates going into 
effect on March 1, 2020 will impact Companies’ cost of providing service in the Rate Year. 
Staff also suggests that the Commission should disallow the increasing contractor costs 
because the Companies did not identify any potential savings.  The panel indicates that there would 
be “some benefits realized such as operational efficiencies of the damage prevention program and 
increased productivity by the workforce.”  Tr. 3640, lines 2-4.  However, Staff fails to cite any 
                     
52 Id at p.8.   


--- Page 91 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
86 of 310 
evidence or analysis of these alleged savings, or even attempt to quantify the level of any potential 
savings.  Mere speculation there may be some unidentified, unquantified efficiencies associated 
with the Companies’ new damage prevention contracts is no basis for an adjustment in this case.  
 
For these reasons, Staff’s proposed adjustments to the Companies’ updated damage 
prevention costs should be rejected.    
B. 
Amortization of Regulatory Deferrals 
1. 
Use of Gas Safety Negative Revenue Adjustments and Unexpended 
Energy Efficiency Funds  
KEDNY and KEDLI have on their books regulatory deferral balances that are each in a net 
asset position, meaning that regulatory balances are owed to the Companies by customers.  Tr. 
1922, lines 9-19; Tr. 2184, lines 2-13; Exhibits 1 and 14.  To mitigate the rate increases, the 
Companies did not propose to recover these balances in these rate filings.  Id.  Instead the 
Companies proposed to continue applying carrying charges to these balances, as previously 
approved by the Commission.  Id.  No party filed testimony objecting to the Companies’ proposal.   
In their pre-filed testimony, Staff proposed to utilize the portion of the deferral balance 
related to gas safety negative revenue adjustments (“NRAs”), unexpended energy efficiency 
amounts, and unexpended economic development funds that are in a regulatory liability position 
to offset the proposed rate increases.  Tr. 2399, lines 2-12.   Specifically, Staff proposed using the 
following: $5.852 million and $0.526 million for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively, related to gas 
safety NRAs; $7.295 million and $2.050 million for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively, related to 
unexpended energy efficiency amounts; and $1.750 million and $2.250 million for  KEDNY and 
KEDLI, respectively in economic development amounts.  Tr. 2399-2400, lines 13-24 and 1-4; Tr. 
2441-42, lines 7-24 and 1-9; Exhibit 514 (SRRP-5); Exhibit 515 (SRRP-6).

fact, the Companies’ depreciation expert, Paul M. Normand of Management Application 
Consulting, Inc.,54 determined that there have been no meaningful changes in the circumstances

a. The Companies’ Proposal To Recover The Remining Costs Of Its 
LPP Assets Over The Expected 20-Year Remaining Life Is Fair And 
Reasonable 
As the SRP agreed, reasonable depreciation rates will permit the Companies to recover the 
cost of their investment in facilities, plus or minus net salvage, over the lives of those facilities.  
Tr. 3005.  In this case, the record shows that the Companies are on track to complete the 
replacement of their LPP assets over a 20-year period.  Tr. 4047.  While the Companies appreciate 
that the SRP’s proposed adjustments to the Companies’ proposed LPP depreciation rates are 
motivated by concerns about bill impacts, the Companies believe that these concerns do not 
provide a basis to reject the Companies’ proposals in these cases for the following reasons: 
(1) Adopting the depreciation recommendations of either the SRP or NYC witness Kollen 
would unreasonably require future customers to pay both a significant portion of the 
costs of retired LPP assets as well as the full cost of replacement facilities.  Tr. 1734.  


--- Page 96 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
91 of 310 
In making recommendations that future customers be required to bear these costs, 
neither the SRP nor Mr. Kollen has provided any analysis that shows that the cost of 
replacement facilities is likely to be cheaper in the future.  At the same time however, 
the record shows that it is important to protect future customers by establishing an 
equitable method of LPP cost recovery at this time because the Companies’ customers 
counts may drop in the future due to reduction in the use of fossil fuels.  Tr. 1734.  
Requiring a potentially diminished customer base in the future to bear the cost of retired 
LPP assets for the benefit of current customers would be particularly unfair.  Id. 
(2) The actual revenue impacts associated with the SFP’s recommended adjustments to 
KEDNY and KEDLI’s depreciation rates are very modest57 in comparison to the 
Companies’ revenue requirements, constituting only a 0.7 percent reduction to 
KEDNY’s forecast Rate Year revenues under Staff’s proposed revenue requirement58 
and a 0.9 percent reduction to KEDLI’s forecast Rate Year revenues under Staff’s 
proposed revenue requirement.59   
(3) The Companies’ LPP assets have been in service for many years,60 and during that time 
past and current customers did not pay an equitable share of what the total costs of the 
LPP assets will turn out to be.61

proposal approximately $56.8 million of this 2019 cost would be left to future customers.  Such a

requirement proposals are adopted in these cases.  NYC’s depreciation recommendation would 
worsen this situation.64  For this reason alone, NYC’s recommendation should be rejected. 
D. 
Property Taxes 
KEDNY and KEDLI’s forecasts of property tax expenses for the Rate Year are $168.093 
million and $181.880 million, respectively.  Exhibit 306, Schedule 1, page 1 of 4; Exhibit 316, 
Schedule 1, page 1 of 4.  The Companies derived their forecasts by using actual calendar year 2018 
year-end property tax amounts of $150.253 million for KEDNY and $154.800 million for KEDLI.  
Tr. 1089, lines 1-6.  The Companies then normalized their Historic Test Year property tax expense 
by reversing property tax deferrals recorded that year, adjusting the Historic Test Year for known 
changes in obsolescence, applying a 12-month growth factor to account for the Bridge Period 
                     
64 Mr. Kollen acknowledged he conducted no analysis of the impact of his recommendation on the 
Companies’ cash flow metrics.  Exhibit 629 at 9. 


--- Page 100 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
95 of 310 
(January 2019 – March 2020) between the Historic Test Year and the Rate Year, and adding the 
forecast property tax expense associated with incremental additions to Plant in Service.  Tr. 1090, 
lines 5-19.  After normalizing the Historic Test Year, the Companies applied growth rates of 5.04 
percent for KEDNY and 3.78 percent for KEDLI to account for expected increases in property tax 
based on the two-year growth rate from FY 2017-19.  Tr. 1166, lines 2-10.  Finally, due to the 
uncertainty associated with forecasting property tax expenses, the Companies proposed continuing 
the existing property tax reconciliation, with a modification to change the reconciliation from an 
85%/15% sharing to a full symmetrical reconciliation mechanism to ensure that customers would 
pay no more and no less than the actual property tax expense incurred.  Tr. 1102-03. 
Staff generally agreed with the Companies’ property tax forecasts with the exception of the 
proposed growth rate.  Tr. 2572-85.  Staff proposes growth rates of 4.60 percent and 3.12 percent 
for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively, based upon three calendar years of data from 2016-18.  Tr. 
2580, lines 11-15.  Staff’s proposed growth rates would reduce Rate Year property tax expense by 
$1.274 million for KEDNY and $2.326 million for KEDLI.  Tr. 2581-82.  Additionally, Staff 
proposes to eliminate the property tax reconciliation mechanism for KEDNY and KEDLI in a one-
year case.  Tr. 2583-84. 
As outlined below, the Commission should establish rates that reflect the forecast level of 
property taxes presented in the Companies’ testimony and exhibits and authorize the Companies 
to fully reconcile any difference between the forecast level of property taxes and the actual level 
of property taxes incurred by the Companies in the Rate Year. 
1. 
The Companies’ Growth Rates Are Reasonable And Supported By The 
Record 
Staff does not believe a two-year growth rate contains enough data points to show a trend 
and that their proposed three-year average growth rate is “necessary” to appropriately produce a 


--- Page 101 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
96 of 310 
trend to forecast property tax expense.  Tr. 2579-80.  Further, Staff argues that the three-year 
average should be based on calendar year 2016-2018 as opposed to the Companies’ fiscal years.  
Id.  Staff alleges that use of fiscal years to forecast property tax would result in a double count of 
the January-March 2019 data in the growth rate calculation – first in calculating the growth rate 
and then when forecasting property tax expense.  Tr. 2580-81. 
 
Staff’s arguments are belied by the record and inconsistent with how this expense has been 
forecast in prior National Grid rate cases.  While the Companies agree that three years provide 
more data points, a more accurate growth rate is not an automatic corollary of the inclusion of 
additional data points.  Tr. 1172, lines 11-12.  Indeed, if a three-year average growth rate had been 
used in KEDNY’s current rate plan in Case 16-G-0059, the growth rate would have been -2.08 
percent; however, the actual growth rate from that time period (2015-2018) was 4.60 percent – a 
6.68 percent difference.  Id., lines 12-15.  A 6.68 percent difference in growth rate could result in 
a multi-million dollar deviation from actual expense when applied to the significant amount of 
property tax that will be incurred by the Companies in the Rate Year.  This unnecessarily places a 
risk of non-recovery on the Companies, a risk that would be further increased if Staff’s proposal 
to disallow a reconciliation of property tax expense were adopted.  Staff’s use of calendar years 
rather than fiscal years is similarly flawed.  The Rate Year will encompass the Companies’ fiscal 
year, beginning April 1, 2020 and ending March 21, 2021, not calendar year 2020.  Use of calendar 
years 2016-2018 does not provide an accurate comparison to the Rate Year as the misalignment in 
the beginning and end points of each year could result in certain property tax events being 
erroneously excluded from the data.  Additionally, while Staff indicates its proposal expands the 
number of data points, use of the calendar years 2016-2018 actually excludes relevant data points 
from January-March 2019, the most current, known property tax expense included in the record.  


--- Page 102 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
97 of 310 
Therefore, use of fiscal year data to forecast property tax expense growth, as proposed by the 
Companies, appropriately aligns the forecast with the Rate Year.  Tr. 1173, lines 4-6.  Accordingly, 
use of the most recent two fiscal years is more reflective of recent property trends and is likely a 
better indicator of the actual property tax expense that will be incurred in the Rate Year. 
 
Finally, use of the most recent two fiscal years does not result in a double count of any time 
period in the Rate Year calculation.  As indicated above, the Companies’ forecasts began with 
property tax expense from calendar year-end 2018 data.  Tr. 1089, lines 1-6; Tr. 1173, lines 12-
13.  The Companies then applied a 12-month growth rate to this data to cover the fifteen-month 
Bridge Period between the Historic Test Year and the Rate Year.  Tr. 1173, lines 13-16.  By using 
a 12-month rather than 15-month growth rate, the Companies’ methodology specifically avoided 
the potential double count of costs from January-March 2019 as the growth rate factor was only 
applied once to this period.  Id.  Accordingly, as the data for this period was not inappropriately 
escalated twice by the growth factor, there is no reason to exclude this period from the calculation 
of the Company’s growth rate to develop the forecast property tax expense for the Rate Year. 
2. 
The Symmetrical Property Tax Reconciliation Mechanisms Proposed by 
the Companies are Reasonable and Should be Adopted by the 
Commission  
The Companies proposed modifying the existing mechanism which defers 85 percent of 
the difference between actual property and special franchise tax expense and the amounts reflected 
in rates, to a fully symmetrical reconciliation mechanism wherein the Companies only recover, 
and customers are only charged, the actual property and special francize tax expense incurred by 
the Companies.  Tr. 1102, lines 6-14.  The Companies submit that this proposal is a reasonable 
and appropriate approach to the recovery of property tax expense given the variability, uncertainty, 
and difficulties experienced with forecasting property tax expense.  Id., lines 11-14.   A property 
tax reconciliation has been a key feature of the Companies’ rate plans for nearly a decade and in 


--- Page 103 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
98 of 310 
no way has it detracted KEDNY or KEDLI from prudently managing its property tax expenses, a 
statement with which Staff agreed.  Tr. 2616, lines 18-23.  Indeed, the Companies have a long 
record of successfully challenging unlawful property tax assessments and mitigating property tax 
expense for customers, including: (i) the reflection of more than $20 million in property tax refunds 
in the current rate plan relating to various tax assessment challenges made by KEDLI; (ii) savings 
of $2.5 million for each of KEDNY and KEDLI associated with proactive efforts by the Companies 
to reduce their property tax expenses in these filings; and (iii) a recent notice of tax refund filed by 
KEDLI to address an approximately $50 million judgment received by KEDLI in a litigation with 
the Town of Hempstead in Case 18-M-0270, which dates all the way back to 2002.  Tr. 2613-16.   
Staff opposed the Companies’ proposal, claiming that a property tax reconciliation 
mechanism is not needed in a one-year rate case.  Tr. 2584, lines 10-12.  Staff cited the short term 
of rates and the Companies’ opportunity to update property tax expense for material changes in 
their brief on exceptions in support of its argument.  Tr. 2583-84. 
Staff’s position is unreasonable and does not serve to mitigate the potential volatility that 
may be experienced in property tax expense, even in a one-year case, thereby placing an undue 
risk on the Companies and their customers.  Staff does not dispute that, even with the most current 
information available, forecast levels of property tax expense could materially differ from the 
expense actually incurred by the Companies.  There is similarly no dispute that such deviations 
are primarily attributable to forces outside of the Companies control (e.g., changes in municipal 
property tax rates, obsolescence rates,), notwithstanding the Companies’ aggressive efforts to seek 
property tax reductions and refunds.  Accordingly, a reconciliation mechanism is appropriate and 
necessary to protect the Companies and their customers from the risk associated with this expense 
category.  Unlike the other deferral mechanisms proposed by Staff in these filings, such as the 


--- Page 104 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
99 of 310 
downward-only credit card and new hire reconciliations, property tax expense is far more material 
and far more uncertain and therefore appropriate in a one-year case.  The Commission has adopted 
similar reconciliation mechanisms in several recent rate cases in the context of joint proposals65 
and there is Commission precedent to support adoption of a property tax reconciliation mechanism 
in a one-year, litigated case.66   
For these reasons, the Commission should deny Staff’s proposal and adopt the property tax 
reconciliation mechanism proposed by the Companies.   
E.

of Amortization/Return to Ratepayers of Annual Excess Deferred (EDFIT) for 
Unprotected Plant And Non-Plant Balances 
In their filings, the Companies proposed to amortize the annual excess deferred income 
taxes (“EDIT”) for the unprotected plant and non-plant balances resulting from the Tax Cuts and 
Jobs Act of 2017 over 44 years for KEDNY and 47 years for KEDLI consistent with the manner 
in which protected EDIT is amortized under the average rate assumption method (“ARAM”) 
required by the Internal Revenue Service’s normalization rules.  Tr. 2028, lines 5-12; Tr. 2276,

changes could result in the Companies having to collect money from customers that it previously 
returned.  Tr. 2029, lines 7-9; Tr. 2317, lines 17-23.   
Further, Staff and PULP are proposing to shorten the amortization to mitigate rate impacts 
in the present.  The other material disallowances proposed by Staff in these filings, however, have 
effectively already accomplished this result to the detriment of the Companies.  Therefore, if the 
Commission were to adopt the adjustments proposed by Staff in these filings, use of the 
unprotected balances would do nothing more than further reduce rates that are already reduced to 
levels lower than cost of service, thereby creating a greater impact on customers in the future when 
the amortization ends.

proposal were adopted.  Tr. 2314-16, lines 25, 2-15, and 2-3.  The Companies’ Revenue

Companies and their customers.  
F. 
Amortization of Expenses  
Because the Companies’ initial filing proposed a multi-year rate plan, certain expenses, 
such as management audit and rate case expenses, were amortized over the term of the proposed 
four-year rate plan.  As the Companies and parties are now litigating a one-year rate case that will 
set rates for the period April 1, 2020 through March 31, 2021, these costs should be fully recovered 
in the Rate Year.   
Staff’s position that these costs should be addressed in the next rate case make little sense, 
especially considering that the Companies will be filing a new rate case in the next few months.  
Tr. 2532, lines 6-14.  That case will include its own rate case expense and the Companies submit 
it would be inequitable to have to wait to recover rate case expenses that were incurred for these 
cases in the next case.  These costs have been prudently incurred and there is no basis not to provide 
the Companies full and immediate recovery of these costs in the Rate Year.

G. 
CSC O&M Tracker  
KEDNY proposes to implement an O&M tracker for costs associated with the CSC67 
program that would fully reconcile the difference between forecast and actual spending in the non-
reimbursable “Support & Protect” O&M expense category.  Tr. 4278, lines 8-13.  Support & 
Protect work consists of the O&M work required to protect KEDNY’s gas assets during City 
construction projects (e.g., shoring facilities).  The Commission should approve this mechanism 
because it will ensure that KEDNY recovers prudently incurred costs to perform this mandated 
work and, importantly, provide assurance that KEDNY’s customers are not overcharged to the 
extent expenses are lower than expected in any given year.  The SGIOP opposes the proposed 
tracker to reconcile the O&M expense associated with CSC.  Tr. 4701, line 14.   
The CSC program scope is continuing to grow as NYC is investing billions of dollars to 
improve its water and sewer systems.  Tr. 4247, lines 17-19.  Because KEDNY’s CSC Program is 
completely reactive to NYC’s construction activities, the annual O&M expenditures (including 
non-reimbursable “Support and Protect” expenditures) are subject to significant volatility.  As 
shown in Exhibit 63 (GIOP- 6), KEDNY’s actual spending on non-reimbursable O&M varied from 
forecast expenditures by 75 percent between FY 2016 and FY 2017, and by 118 percent between 
FY 2017 and FY 2018.  The dramatic year-to-year fluctuation of the CSC Program’s O&M 
requirements, which are entirely a function of NYC’s workload, is a significant impediment to 
creation of a reliable multiyear financial O&M forecast.   
The SGIOP acknowledges that KEDNY’s CSC-related O&M expense is a function of the 
work required by NYC (Tr. 4772, lines 19-22), that KEDNY is obligated to provide O&M support 
for the City’s project (Tr. 4772-4773, lines 23-25 and line 2), and, importantly, that the level of 
                     
67 As discussed in Section VI.A.2.a below, the Company’s CSC program involves the relocation and/or 
protection of gas facilities that conflict with municipal public works projects.

O&M expense work is not within KEDNY’s complete control (Tr. 4773, lines 3-6).  These factors 
strongly support a two-way tracker for KEDNY’s CSC O&M expense – and the SGIOP provides 
no justification for opposing an O&M reconciliation mechanism for this program.   
KEDNY’s CSC program is unique in its challenges to accurately forecast, and, for that 
reason, capital and O&M reconciliation mechanisms are appropriate to protect both customers and 
the Company.  Even if KEDNY manages this program to maximize efficiencies and controllable 
costs to the highest standard, the inability to predict future program needs would still justify the 
need for a reconciliation mechanism.  To the extent KEDNY’s O&M expense is materially higher 
or lower than the rate case allowance for reasons outside its control, KEDNY and its customers 
should not be asked to bear the risk of any volatility in this area.   
VI. 
RATE BASE ISSUES 
A. 
Capital Projects And Expenditures 
The Companies, as the operators of gas networks that serve nearly two million customers 
and are relied on by millions more people for heating, cooking, and other human needs, must make 
the investments they deem necessary to maintain safe and adequate service.  Tr. 4733, lines 16-20.  
The Companies operate some of the oldest gas networks in the United States.  Tr. 4833, lines 17-
19.  The imperative to modernize gas infrastructure and replace aging assets is driving the need 
for capital investment.  At the same time, expanding federal and state gas safety requirements are 
mandating investments in safety enhancements (Tr. 4393, lines 15-19), while reducing methane 
emissions and eliminating system leaks from the gas network are public policy priorities best 
solved through capital improvements such as main replacements and leak repairs.  Tr. 39, lines 19-
20.   


--- Page 109 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
104 of 310 
Indeed, numerous regulatory, gas safety, and policy developments have required the 
Companies to increase the level of capital investment in recent years.  Tr. 4393, lines 15-19.  For 
example, pipeline safety incidents, such as the tragic events in San Bruno, California, Allentown, 
Pennsylvania, East Harlem, New York, and the more recent incident in Merrimack Valley, 
Massachusetts, have appropriately increased focus on pipeline safety and the need to carefully 
monitor and replace aging pipeline infrastructure.  Weather events, such as Superstorm Sandy and 
the Polar Vortex, and the expectation that similar events will continue to occur, require the 
Companies to find ways to protect their facilities from severe weather.  Tr. 4394, lines 11-19.    
Collectively, these factors are necessitating increasing levels of capital investment.  Indeed, 
over the last several years, with the support of the Commission, KEDNY and KEDLI have 
significantly increased investment in gas infrastructure.  Tr. 4395, lines 1-14.  In fact, when looking 
at total capital spending in recent years, the Companies have significantly overspent their rate 
allowance for capital investment.  As shown in Table 5 below, the SGIOP Exhibit 465 (SGIOP-2) 
analyzes the Companies’ capital budgets, actual investments, and budgets variances over the last 
several years – and demonstrates that operational imperatives have driven the Companies to 
outspend their rate allowances.

In the four most recent years considered in the SGIOP’s analysis (FY16 to FY19), KEDNY 
overspent its rate allowance in each year, an average over-spend of approximately 14.3 
percent/year, and a cumulative overspend of approximately $257 million.  For KEDLI during this

spend of approximately 6.4 percent per year, resulting in a cumulative overspend of approximately 
$66 million.   
 
Going forward, significant capital investment in the Rate Year is required to ensure that 
the gas system continues to operate safely while meeting the demands of customers and to enable 
further modernization of the Companies’ facilities and operations in a manner that achieves 
meaningful methane emissions reductions in furtherance of State and Companies’ shared policy 
goals.   
Given the significant levels of capital investment proposed in these cases, both the 
Companies (Tr. 4401, lines 12-17) and Staff (Tr. 4702, line 23) propose to continue the net plant 
capital tracker mechanism that was approved in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases as part 
of a multi-year rate plan.  This mechanism ensures that any under-spend in the Rate Year, including 
any capital savings realized through the Accelerate Program, will be returned to customers.  For 
example, if the rate allowance for capital investments is higher than the revenue requirement 
associated with Companies’ actual investment in the Rate Year – meaning the Companies 
effectively spend less than the approved budget - the Companies would defer the difference for 
return to customers.  Tr. 4738-39, lines 14-25 and lines 1-4.  But if the rate allowance is lower than 
the revenue requirement for the Companies’ capital investments – meaning the Companies 


--- Page 111 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
106 of 310 
effectively overspend their rate allowance (as occurred in seven of the eight years discussed above) 
– the Companies are not made whole.  Tr. 4739, lines 5-10.68 
Thus, Staff’s severe adjustments to the Companies’ capital plans must be considered in the 
context that (i) there is increasing need to modernize gas infrastructure to enhance safety, 
reliability, and resiliency, (ii) public policy strongly supports reducing methane emissions and 
system leaks, which requires significant level of capital investments,  (iii) the Companies have 
historically overspent their rate allowances for capital investments, and (iv) in the unlikely event 
the Companies underspent their rate allowance for capital investments in the Rate Year, customers 
are fully protected through the net plant tractor.  These factors weigh against the SGIOP’s 
significant adjustments to the Companies’ proposed capital forecasts.      
Turning to the specific adjustments, the SGIOP explains that it generally supports the 
Companies’ capital investment plans (Tr. 4643, line 14) but proposes adjustments for certain 
projects and programs in the mandated, non-infrastructure, and customer connections categories, 
and proposes an overall blanket reduction to the entire Reliability category of spending.  Among 
other adjustments, the SGIOP rejects the Companies’ higher inflation rates and other factors (e.g., 
increasing contract costs) that will increase Rate Year costs for mandated capital programs (e.g., 
CSC and Main Replacement).  Tr. 4657-63.  Where the Companies’ forecasts were derived from 
historic results in FY 2017 and FY 2018, the SGIOP believes that forecasts should “updated” to 
two- or three-year averages to include FY 2019. Tr. 4649, lines 1-6; Tr. 4669, lines 9-14; Tr. 4684-
85, line 24 and lines 1-3.  The SGIOP explains that it lacks confidence in the Companies’ 
Reliability Category forecasts because there have been historic variances between the Companies’

Support For Proposed Capital Investments 
Over the course of nearly a year, the Companies have provided the SGIOP with a 
tremendous amount of information to enable a thorough review of the proposed capital investment 
plan.  The Companies’ direct, corrections and updates, rebuttal, first supplemental, and second 
supplemental testimony, and the numerous supporting exhibits, provided hundreds of pages of 
information on each of the programs and projects proposed in the Rate Year.  This sworn testimony 
of the Companies’ Chief Engineer and other experts described the drivers for historic capital 
spending, evolving system requirements, and gas safety considerations influencing future spending 
and, specifically, the proposed capital investments over the next four years.  The Companies also 
provided a set of “white papers” (Exhibits 62 (GIOP-5)) that contain the same information that is 
typically included in sanctioning documentation, such as detailed project descriptions, project 
needs and justification, forecasting methodologies, cost drivers, applicable regulatory 
requirements, and alternatives analyses.  The Companies also responded to extensive discovery in 
these proceedings, including numerous information requests regarding the details of the capital 
plan.  The Companies’ subject matter experts facilitated technical sessions to present their 
proposals in more detail, and these same experts have made themselves available to answer any 
questions regarding their programs.  Therefore, to the extent Staff still has any questions on the

Companies’ proposed capital investments, this is in no way a function of the Companies’ inability 
to provide cost support or other information on their capital investment plans.    
Blanket Adjustments 
Staff proposes a number of blanket adjustments to programs and projects based on an 
assessment of prior years’ spending and other factors.  Tr. 4783, lines 3-9, 17-25.  These 
adjustments are not supported by sufficient data and analysis and fail to consider the Companies’ 
work requirements in the Rate Year.  Among other deficiencies, the SGIOP ignores known, 
significant cost drivers that will increase the Companies’ capital costs going forward such as the 
need to invest in on-system natural gas injection facilities to maintain reliability and enable new 
customer connections in the near term.  In contrast to the SGIOP’s blanket approach, the 
Companies carefully determined the appropriate basis and methodology for forecasting Rate Year 
investments on a program by-program basis.  Following a detailed analysis of each program, 
including examination of historic spending, the Companies identified the best predictor of Rate 
Year costs.  The Companies determined the projects for which a project estimate, rather than a 
forecast based solely on historic costs, was more appropriate.  In this way, the Companies carefully 
considered any project-specific considerations and anomalies prior to setting their forecasts for 
each program.  The Companies’ methodologies for deriving program forecasts are provided in the 
Companies’ testimony and exhibits and in response to IR DPS-761 (Exhibit 463 (SGIOP-1)), page 
570. 
The SGIOP’s attempt to more closely align the Rate Year forecasts with the Companies’ 
average historic costs and spending levels shifts funds away from the Companies’ highest priority 
mandated, safety, and reliability programs.  Simply applying blanket adjustments to projects and 
programs that SGIOP otherwise supports will impair the Companies’ ability to deliver high priority 


--- Page 114 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
109 of 310 
capital programs that are needed to meet important reliability, safety, and policy goals.  More 
specifically, proposed downward adjustments in the mandated category will challenge the 
Companies’ ability to deliver on aggressive goals for LPP replacement, methane reduction, and 
programs and projects discussed below that are necessary to reduce overall system risks.  Proposed 
adjustments in the Reliability category will compromise the Companies’ ability to replace aging 
facilities and equipment that are critical to system operations and to add new equipment that 
enables the Companies to remotely operate and control their gas systems.  The Companies 
appreciate the SGIOP’s concerns for managing increasing costs; however, the reality is that the 
SGIOP’s proposed adjustments will result in the reprioritization of capital investments in a manner 
that fails to achieve the Companies’ and the Commission’s goals.  This is not the right result from 
a public policy or safety perspective.  The impact of proposed adjustments to individual capital 
programs is discussed below. 
1. 
Customer Connections  
On November 26, 2019, the Commission approved a settlement agreement between the 
Companies and DPS Staff in the Case 19-G-0678 Order where the Companies agreed to lift the 
moratorium for the next two winters (through September 1, 2021).  To enable new customer 
connections, the Companies developed an operational plan and supply solution that provides for 
the expanded use of portable compressed natural gas (“CNG”) injections to maintain system 
pressure during peak conditions, as well as various demand-side measures and the continued 
reliance on city gate peaking supplies. Tr. 5195, lines 1-7.  In their Second Supplemental 
Testimony, the Companies updated their capital forecasts for the Customer Connections programs 
to reflect the costs of new customer additions (e.g., installing main and services, meters, and AMR 
requirements) and the Gas System Reinforcements and Reliability projects necessary to maintain 
system pressures and serve additional load (e.g., transmission upgrades).   The SGIOP agrees that

Companies’ Initial Brief

the costs to connect new customers in the Rate Year should be reflected in the Companies’ rate 
allowance.  Tr. 4740, lines 11-16. 
a. Use Of 2 Versus 3 Year Average Costs; Units Forecast For Install 
Main, Tariff Main, and Meter Purchases 
The SGIOP generally agrees with the Companies’ methodology for deriving the forecasts 
for Customer Connections installations but recommends that the unit costs be calculated based on 
an updated three-year average (FY 2017 to FY 2019) rather than the two-year average the 
Companies used (FY 2017 to FY 2018) for the Install Main, Install Services, Install 
Meter/Regulator, and Automatic Meter Reading programs.   
The SGIOP starts from a reasonable position, i.e., using a three-year average to forecast 
future costs, then applying the unit costs to the forecast number of units in the Rate Year.  Tr. 4715, 
lines 17-19.  However, Staff goes on to make various “normalizing” adjustments that seem 
designed to eliminate years with the highest unit costs from the average under the theory those 
years were outliers.  Indeed, in an attempt to reduce the Companies’ rate allowance in this area, 
the SGIOP was selective in choosing the historic years used to calculate its unit cost forecast for 
different cost components – even within the same category of Customer Connections.    In some 
cases, the SGIOP argued to include more recent data because it was a better indicator of the 
Companies’ Rate Year costs.  Tr. 4748, lines 2-10. In other cases, the SGIOP argues that more 
recent data – when showing increases - should be normalized out.  Tr. 4745, lines 22-23.  This 
“cherry-picking” approach was designed to reduce the Companies’ rate allowance by selectively 
including or excluding historic data when it fit the SGIOP’s purposes.

determinations, the number of units were approximately 50 percent higher – which would 
significantly increase the rate allowance had it been used to forecast units.  This example highlights 
the inequity of picking and choosing historic data to get to an end result.

b. System Reinforcements 
The Gas System Reinforcements Program contains projects intended to ensure that 
minimum system pressures are maintained throughout the gas network during periods of peak 
demand.  In response to demand for gas usage in its service territory, KEDLI determined that it is 
necessary to complete several projects to ensure its ability to meet peak requirements. These 
reinforcement projects are essential to ensure the Companies can continue to serve growing gas 
usage load. Tr. 4437, lines 12-20.  The SGIOP acknowledges that Reinforcement Programs are 
important to maintain system pressure – especially when adding additional gas load from new 
customers.  Tr. 4755, lines 20-25. 
In its Second Supplemental Testimony, the Companies describe the various reinforcement 
projects needed to maintain adequate system pressures (especially considering the Companies will 
be adding new customers in the Rate Year).  Specifically, KEDLI explains that it accelerated Phase 
1 of the Southeast Suffolk project to ensure it will be in service in FY22.  This project is required 
to support new customer connections on eastern Long Island.  Accordingly, KEDLI shifted $5 
million of the Southeast Suffolk project forecast from FY22 to FY21 (the Rate Year).  Tr. 4569, 
lines 14-21. 
The SGIOP supports the need for the Southeast Suffolk Infrastructure project to maintain

$5 million for the project to the Rate Year because it “results in no change to the outcome to

a misconception that KEDLI was proposing to accelerate the in-service date for this project.  As 
explained by the Companies’ GIOP in their Second Supplemental Testimony, “[m]oreover, to 
enable new customer connections, KEDLI has accelerated Phase 1 of the Southeast Suffolk project 
to ensure it will be in service in FY 2022.  Accordingly, Exhibit 369 (GIOP-1SS) reflects a shift

i. CSC Tracker Mechanism  
Pursuant to the Joint Proposal adopted in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, 
KEDNY and KEDLI have a reconciliation mechanism (90/10) for CSC capital expenditures. Tr. 
4700, lines 1-3.  However, the Companies must be allowed to recover funding for their work on 
this mandated program – and a partial deferral mechanism is not a substitute for appropriate rate 
recovery.  The Companies’ forecasts are based on known factors that will increase CSC costs in 
the Rate Year, whereas the reconciliation is intended to cover unknown cost increases. Therefore, 
having a reconciliation in place does not blunt the detrimental effects of removing nearly $25 
million from KEDNY’s CSC program forecasts.   
In light of the increasing volatility in the CSC category, the Companies are proposing to 
adjust the existing partial tracker mechanism to a full reconciliation of the difference between 
forecast and actual spending (from the current 90 percent deferral mechanism). Tr. 4277, lines 18-

Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
117 of 310 
21.  The SGIOP instead recommends that the capital deferral mechanism be changed from the 
current 90 percent/10 percent reconciliation to an 80 percent/20 percent reconciliation based on 
the SGIOP’s belief that the Companies need an incentive to effectively manage program aspects 
and costs that are in the Companies’ control. Tr. 4700-4701, lines 22-24 and lines 1-4.  The 
SGIOP’s position on this issue creates an unreasonable risk that the Companies will be required to 
fund prudently incurred costs to perform mandated work.  The reconciliation mechanism is 
intended to manage the aspects of CSC that are not within the Companies’ control, including the 
unpredictability of the NYC’s construction work that will ultimately dictate the level of CSC 
expenditures required each year.  As demonstrated above, notwithstanding KEDNY’s efforts to

only is the SGIOP’s recommendation unjustified, but it is egregious when coupled with the 
significant reduction to KEDNY’s CSC program budget. There is simply no basis for the 
mechanism to provide less than a full reconciliation, let alone a reduction to the mechanism that 
already exists.
