3. 
Reliability 
The Companies’ reliability investments are designed to maintain safe, reliable service to 
customers.  Tr. 4308, lines 5-7.  The Reliability category includes programs related to gas control, 
heaters, reactive Instrument & Regulation (“I&R”), pressure regulating facilities, valve 
installation/replacement, remote-controlled valves, gas planning, system reliability, water 
intrusion, system automation and control line integrity, special station projects (including over-
pressure protection), storm hardening (remote control valves), and reliability and storm hardening.  


--- Page 127 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
122 of 310 
Tr. 4308, lines 7-13.  Significantly given the current supply constraints in downstate New York, 
reliability investments also include critical projects to provide pressure support, outage 
contingency, and operational flexibility, as well as investments in on-system liquified natural gas 
(“LNG”) and CNG facilities. Tr. 4318. 
The SGIOP generally supports the Companies’ reliability programs and, with few 
exceptions, does not recommend that the Companies eliminate any reliability programs: “the 
[SGIOP] has not recommended elimination of any projects or programs from this category.  And 
generally, we do support, you know, the company’s ability to select, rank, and prioritize projects 
and programs within this category.”  Tr. 4782-4783, lines 21-25 and line 2.    
Instead, the SGIOP applies a blanket “slippage adjustment” – which is an across-the-board 
percentage reduction to the budget based on prior years’ actual spending against the entire budget 
category.  Tr. 4676, lines 15-18.   The SGIOP determined that KEDNY and KEDLI have typically 
spent approximately 74 percent and 71 percent, respectively, of their total budgets in the Reliability 
category.  The SGIOP reasons that the Companies’ spending in the Rate Year and Data Years for 
the entire category should be limited to this trend and, therefore, removes 26 percent and 29 percent 
from the Reliability category total forecast for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively.  Tr. 4676, lines 
12-15. 
a. Slippage Adjustment 
The SGIOP’s blunt method of applying significant adjustments to critical reliability 
projects the panel otherwise supports is flawed and unreasonable.  The adjustments to the projects 
and programs included in the category are in the first place unsupported, and the adjustment will 
hinder the Companies’ ability to deliver important programs that are needed to enable continued 
safe and reliable service in downstate New York.  A blanket adjustment to a spending category 
that fails to carefully consider the unique factors that influence historic spending variances, or the 


--- Page 128 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
123 of 310 
factors considered by the Companies when projecting forecasts for each program, is inappropriate. 
Variances may be experienced for a host of valid reasons, including factors outside the Companies’ 
control. For example, the Metropolitan Reliability Infrastructure (“MRI”) Project experienced 
delays due to a design change required by a municipality, and, as is stated in KEDNY’s GIOP’s 
Direct Testimony, the LNG Salt Water Pump House Project experienced delays resulting from 
unanticipated permit requirements and stipulations imposed by the FDNY.  Tr. 4541, lines 6-10. 
The SGIOP agrees that in a given year projects may move in or out of the Companies work 
plan for any number of legitimate reasons; items like operational priorities, permits, damage, and 
regulatory mandates can impact the investments; and capital projects can be accelerated or deferred 
into another year.  Tr. 4784, lines 2-11.   The SGIOP is not suggesting that the Companies should 
perform 74 percent or 71 percent of the programs in the Reliability category (Tr. 4787, lines 5-9) 
– but are suggesting the Companies can somehow deliver all of this work  at significantly lower 
costs – a proposition that is without support in the record.  
The forecasts for the various projects and programs in the Companies’ Reliability Category 
are based on different needs and forecasting methodologies.  Historic spending trends do not 
necessarily reflect the Companies’ funding requirements in the Rate Year.  For example, in the 
Heaters program, heaters must be replaced as they reach the end of their useful lives, so that the 
program needs can vary significantly depending on the age of assets.  Additionally, station 
refurbishments or replacements are risk-driven, and the work plan is in part dependent on the 
conditions of the assets as they continue to age.  These are the types of factors the Companies 
consider when setting future project and program budgets, and they are wholly ignored by the 
SGIOP’s attempt to adjust an entire category of spending based on historic trends without 


--- Page 129 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
124 of 310 
sufficient analysis or support.  A blanket slippage adjustment does not consider any of these 
factors. Tr. 4541, lines 12-21.  
Simply put, the Companies cannot deliver their reliability programs with 70 to 75 percent 
of the proposed budgets.  The forecasts for critical projects like Northwest Nassau or MRI are built 
on detailed analysis of the workplan requirements and cost elements – and simply trimming these 
program budgets by 25 percent or more is unsupported and unreasonable.  If the Companies divert 
funding to higher priority projects and programs in the spending category, other projects and 
programs may need to be deferred altogether, increasing overall safety and reliability risk on the 
system. 
i. Impact On The Companies’ CNG/LNG Investments 
The Companies’ interim plans to ensure the peak day supply needed to serve new 
customers rely on the expanded deployment of CNG.  Tr. 4570, lines 14-15.  There are two existing 
CNG injection sites in KEDLI’s service territory at Glenwood and Riverhead (available January 
2020), which will be utilized to serve peak demand for the winter 2019/2020.  For the winter 
2020/2021, to meet increased gas demand from new and existing customers, the Glenwood CNG 
facility will be expanded to double its capacity.  Tr. 4570-71, lines 15-21 and line 1.  Two 
additional CNG injection facilities (a third and a fourth CNG facility) will be required in KEDLI’s 
service territory at locations to be determined.  Tr. 4571, lines 1-3. 
The Companies’ plans for interim solutions to the capacity constraints also involve 
additional reliance on the Companies’ LNG capabilities, including updates to the tank upgrade 
programs and to KEDNY’s LNG Truck Load/Unload Station Program.  Additionally, new 
investments are required for KEDNY’s Greenpoint LNG site to add (i) a new portable LNG facility 
and (ii) two additional vaporizers.  Tr. 4572, lines 3-8.  Considering the age of the tanks and the 
importance of reliable operation of the Companies’ LNG facilities, particularly now that the 


--- Page 130 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
125 of 310 
Companies will be adding new customers over the next several winters, the Companies have 
designed a more streamlined and targeted approach to performing inspections and repairs on the 
tanks.  This work scope will ensure that the tanks are in service for the winter heating seasons.  Tr. 
4572-4573, lines 17-21 and lines 6-7.  KEDLI’s Holtsville LNG Tank will be taken out of service 
during the Spring of 2023 and will be back in service by November of 2023. 
The SGIOP’s proposed slippage adjustments in the reliability category are especially 
concerning given the supply constraints impacting downstate New York, the reliance on CNG and 
LNG solutions to support system pressures over the next two winters, and the Companies’ 
commitments to add new customer load on the backs of these solutions.   The Companies’ planning 
in this area requires careful consideration of the timing and impact of various reliability projects 
on the system, which is completely frustrated by the SGIOP’s proposed adjustments in this area.    
b. Storm Hardening Investment Additions (Mini-Gate stations, Meter 
Elevations, Remote Shutoff Valves, Residential Methane Detectors, 
And Flood Study For Greenpoint LNG Site) 
KEDNY and KEDLI are prepared to implement a storm hardening program to install 
remote service shutoff valves on thousands of gas services within the Federal Emergency 
Management Agency’s designated flood zones. Tr. 4315-16, lines 20-21 and lines 1-2.  These 
valves will add a layer of protection for services that are susceptible to storm surge and flooding 
that could cause over pressurization of the gas facilities connecting customers’ premises; will allow 
for remote operation and monitoring in these areas; and will enable the Companies to stop the flow 
of gas as soon as flooding is detected.  Automated valves also provide a real-time count of services 
impacted by flooding to inform the Companies’ storm response about the resources needed to 
restore the affected customers expeditiously and allow the Companies to interrupt only those 
services impacted by flooding.  Tr. 4316-4317, lines 11-21 and lines 1-9.   


--- Page 131 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
126 of 310 
The parties generally support the Companies’ storm hardening program.  Indeed, the 
SGIOP testified that it “supports the company’s ability to prioritize and -- projects and programs 
that seems best fit to reliably and safely provide service with in its ser -- service territory.”  Tr. 
4791, lines 11-15.  However, the SGIOP goes on to recommend, without identifying any specific 
issue with the valve technology, deferring the program for one year to allow for additional field 
testing.  Tr. 4791, lines 16-21.   
The GIOP testified that the Companies are on track to begin deployment of the program in 
the Rate Year.  Tr. 4543, lines 6-7.  Because contracts are in place with vendors for the valves and 
for support systems, and work is required during the Rate Year on communications network, IT 
changes to the Companies’ customer systems, data security, and other supporting systems, the 
costs of these programs cannot easily be deferred.  Tr. 4543, lines 7-10.  As the GIOP explained: 
“[t]he Companies cannot simply cease this ongoing work for one year in the Rate Year and then 
start it up again. Moreover, the Companies and the manufacturer are conducting extensive testing 
on the valves that is expected to be complete by the end of December 2019.  While contractual 
obligations may not allow for complete deferral of the program out of the Rate Year, the 
Companies are evaluating the possibility of extending this program to phase installations more 
slowly and allow for additional in-field testing and trouble-shooting and possibly third-party 
testing.”  Tr. 4543, lines 15-21.  The SGIOP is correct that the Companies need to make the 
investments to safely provide service, and the Companies have made the case that the storm 
hardening program will provide significant safety benefits and is ready for deployment.  For these 
reasons, there is no basis to remove the costs of the program from the Rate Year. 
i. NYC Storm Hardening Recommendations 
NYC makes several recommendations related to storm hardening, including: accelerating 
storm hardening of two mini-gate stations; developing guidelines for standard elevations for new 


--- Page 132 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
127 of 310 
meter set installations in flood zones; and commissioning a third-party flood vulnerability study 
of the Greenpoint LNG facility to be completed by the end of the Rate Year.  Tr. 5435-36. 
As is noted by NYC, the Companies recently hosted a Storm Hardening Collaborative with 
NYC and other interested parties to develop storm hardening recommendations that are detailed 
in a report filed on April 26, 2018 in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, and have 
implemented all of the recommendations in the report.  Tr. 5435, lines 17-19. The Companies’ 
investment plans include specific investments (e.g., LNG projects) that directly resulted from the 
collaborative.  While climate change impacts are a significant consideration, the Companies must 
allocate available resources in any given year toward projects and programs that address various 
risks to ensure safe and reliable service to customers – and the Companies proposals in these cases 
are designed to prioritize projects and allocate funding based on a comprehensive, holistic risk 
assessment.  For these reasons, the Companies do not believe that additional investments are 
warranted in the Rate Year ensure system reliability.  Tr. 4554, lines 10-11. 
ii. Mini-gate stations 
The Companies’ 10-year station work plan is risk-based and considers multiple significant 
factors including the age and current condition of each station.  As a result of the Storm Hardening 
Collaborative, the Companies now specifically consider location in flood zone as a factor in this 
analysis.  However, as NYC states, the Clifton Gate and the Citizens Gate stations, and an 
additional seven located within FEMA flood zones are included in the 10-year plan but are not 
currently scheduled to be addressed until after year five.  This is due to the need to address other 
stations located outside of the flood zone that score higher from a total overall risk standpoint. Tr. 
4554, lines 18-20.  To accelerate work on the stations as NYC suggests, either other stations that 
are riskier would need to be deferred, or the Companies would require significant additional 
resources to convert its 10-year plan into a 5-year plan for all stations.  Even with significant 


--- Page 133 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
128 of 310 
additional funding, however, it is not clear that such an accelerated plan is even possible given 
overall levels of qualified resources available to conduct the work.  Additionally, the Companies 
and NYC note that regarding the Clifton Gate and Citizens Gate Stations, there are other system 
redundancies to mitigate flood risk.  The Companies also re-assess all stations and the work plan 
every three years – so that if total conditions at stations within flood zones warrant, these stations 
may move up in priority in future iterations of the 10-year plan.  In short, the Companies’ approach 
to mini gate station refurbishment and replacements is reasonable and should not be revised based 
solely on flood zone locations.  Tr. 4554-55, lines 20-23 and lines 1-9. 
iii. Meter Set Elevations  
In response to the work coming out of the Storm Hardening Collaborative, the Companies 
conducted an analysis of the feasibility of standardizing guidance for meter set elevations in flood 
zones and do not recommend increasing the standard height design. Tr. 4555, lines 10-13. The 
Companies conducted outreach among other utilities, the majority of whom indicated they were 
not increasing the height of the meter sets or regulators. Tr. 4555, lines 13-15.  The Companies 
also considered the geography of its service territory and found that the flood baseline varies 
drastically, which creates a significant challenge for determining an alternate standard design 
height.  Therefore, increasing the standard height design for regulators and meters sets for new 
installations is not advisable.  
iv. Greenpoint LNG Flood Study 
The Companies conducted a flood study in response to the Storm Hardening Collaborative. 
The study identified the critical components and facilities at the Greenpoint LNG site and used an 
overlay map that included flood risk data NYC provided.  Tr. 4556, lines 3-6.  Several of the 
projects planned for the LNG sites in the Rate Year and Data Years address the risks identified. In 
short, the Companies are aware that the Greenpoint LNG site is subject to flooding and climate 


--- Page 134 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
129 of 310 
change impacts and are now targeting specific critical assets to address flooding risk.  The 
Companies will continue to evaluate their 10-year capital investment plans for opportunities to 
make further improvements that address climate change impacts at this site. Tr. 4556, lines 6-12.  
NYC’s recommendation to conduct a third-party study that is more detailed could be considered 
in the future to inform future investments but would have limited value for Rate Year planning.   
4. 
Non-Infrastructure Programs; Cost Calculation Using Historic Averages 
a. Green Infrastructure Assets; Use Of Six-Foot Clearance For 
Facility Installation 
NYC expresses general concern that the Companies’ construction work is performed 
without adequate consideration of NYC’s Green Infrastructure Assets and recommends that the 
Companies: (i) install their facilities six feet from the curb rather than three feet; (ii) provide notice 
to NYC agencies prior to street openings; and (iii) attend additional training related to the Green 
Infrastructure Assets.  Tr. 5455-56.  As explained by the GIOP, the Companies are willing to 
coordinate with NYC on this issue and can discuss additional training that the Companies assume 
would be provided by NYC.  However, the Companies are unable to commit to a standard six-foot 
clearance for installations.  The Companies’ construction work is varied, and the Companies must 
operate within existing rights-of-ways, avoid conflicts with other infrastructure, and comply with 
other permit stipulations and municipal code requirements.  Tr. 4556-57.  While the Companies 
are open to further discussions with NYC on this issue, and agreeable to coordinating with NYC 
to mitigate the impact on Green Infrastructure, this issue is not appropriately addressed in the 
context of these rate cases.   
b. Union Requirement (Building and Construction Trades Council Of

The New York State Laborers’ Organizing Fund recommends that the Companies hire only 
union labor from construction trade locals affiliated with the Building and Construction Trades

Council of Greater New York.  Tr. 5705, lines 13-15.  The Companies value the expertise and

limit their contractor work force to only union laborers.  Such a restriction would remove market

services at the least cost, and risking cost increases that customers would ultimately bear.

As discussed above, the Companies agree to continue the net plant capital tracker 
mechanism that was adopted in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.

In a one-year rate case, the Companies support capital reporting that is aligned with the 
reporting structure adopted in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.  Because the Companies 
will be filing another rate case almost immediately, Staff and other parties will have opportunity 
to examine the Companies’ capital expenditures in the Rate Year in the context of those 
proceedings.  For these reasons, there are no grounds for implementing additionally burdensome 
reporting requirements in a one-year case. 
D. 
Earnings Base Capitalization / Working Capital - City/State Construction 
Accounts Receivable Balance 
1. 
KEDNY’s Rate Base Should Be Increased By $28.273 Million To Permit 
The Company To Earn A Return On The City-State Construction 
Expenditures That Will Be Placed In Service But Not Reimbursed By 
NYC During The Rate Year 
In its filing, KEDNY proposed to make a normalization adjustment to its proposed 
Earnings Base/Capitalization (“EB/CAP”) calculation and an offsetting adjustment to a separate 
working capital component in the Historic Test Year to isolate and specifically identify a CSC 


--- Page 136 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
131 of 310 
accounts receivable balance of $58.744 million.  Tr. 2037.  The $58.744 million adjustment 
represents the outstanding Historic Test Year balance of capital and operating expenditures that 
NYC is required to reimburse KEDNY for CSC capital expenditures that KEDNY has incurred to 
enable NYC to execute it municipal work plan.  Tr. 2037.  NYC is required to reimburse KEDNY 
for these expenditures under the Gas Facility Cost Allocation Act.  Tr. 2533. 
In addition to the Historic Test Year CSC adjustment, KEDNY proposed to make a CSC-
related working capital adjustment to reflect the projected increase in working capital associated 
with reimbursable CSC expenditures between the end of the Historic Test Year and the end of the 
Rate Year.  Tr. 2037.  This adjustment increases Rate Year rate base by $28.273 million.  Id.  This 
adjustment reflects the fact that the balance of reimbursable CSC costs is projected to increase 
through the Rate Year as a result of both an increase in CSC work and the continued length in time 
it will take NYC to reimburse KEDNY for the CSC expenditures.  Tr. 2038.  While Staff 
acknowledges that there is no revenue requirement impact associated with the Historic Test Year 
CSC adjustment of $58.744 million, Staff opposes both this adjustment as well as the proposed 
Rate Year adjustment.  Id.  Staff’s recommendation would reduce KEDNY’s rate base by $28.273 
million.  Id. 
Staff’s proposed adjustment should be rejected.  The projected CSC-related costs of 
$28.273 million will be prudently incurred by KEDNY to assist NYC in completing its work plan.  
Tr. 2038.  As the record shows, if a portion of these CSC-related costs were not reimbursable under 
the Gas Facility Cost Allocation Act, they would be reflected in rates during the Rate Year.  Tr. 
2533.74  Thus, there is no sound reason for prohibiting KEDNY from recovering the costs of 
financing these reasonable expenditures during the period in which they remain unreimbursed. 
                     
74 As Staff acknowledged on cross-examination, the Commission has historically permitted KEDNY to 
recover CSC-related costs on a current basis. 


--- Page 137 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
132 of 310 
While Staff offered five reasons why the Commission should reject KEDNY’s proposed 
adjustment, none of these withstands scrutiny.  First, contrary to Staff’s claim there is nothing 
inconsistent between the Commission’s longstanding use of the Federal Energy Regulatory 
Commission’s (“FERC”) working capital formula and KEDNY’s proposal in this case.  As the 
Commission recognized in Re Long Island Lighting Co., the FPC working capital formula 
compensates a utility only for the financing requirements “caused by the lag between the provision 
of service and billing for that service.  It does not compensate KEDNY via rates for the unbilled 
revenues which relate to service at the end of the rate period which will not be billed until the next 
rate period.” 75  Similarly, in this case, the FERC working capital formula does not compensate 
KEDNY for the cost of financing reimbursable CSC expenditures that are not and never will be 
reflected in rates because they will be paid for by NYC.  Tr. 2039-40. 
Contrary to Staff’s claim, the proposed recognition of KEDNY’s growing balance of 
reimbursable CSC costs in rates does not obviate the need for, or double count, the cost items 
captured through the use of the FERC working capital formula.  The FERC formula captures costs, 
such as taxes, that are paid in rates.  The FERC formula does not capture the carrying costs on 
unreimbursed CSC expenditures that are not otherwise recovered in rates.76  KEDNY 
acknowledges that as a general matter, the Commission  uses the FERC working capital formula 
to capture and permit recovery of costs associated with KEDNY’s requirement to fund working 
capital associated with costs reflected in rates in the Rate Year.  KEDNY is not seeking to change 
this approach for costs that are otherwise included in rates.  Instead, KEDNY merely seeks to 
                     
75 31 N.Y.P.S.C. 2473, 129 P.U.R. 4th 131 (November 26, 1991).; see also Re Long Island Lighting Co., 
1992 WL 675305 (June 1, 1992). 
76 Similarly, contrary to Staff’s claim (at Tr. 2415-16), the carrying costs for unpaid amounts associated 
with unreimbursable CSC expenditures are not recovered through the Historic Test Year EB/CAP 
adjustment, which, by its definition, applies only to Historic Test Year costs.  Tr. 2042. 


--- Page 138 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
133 of 310 
recover in rates the carrying costs associated with a single cost item that KEDNY is required to 
fund for the benefit of customers, and which cannot be recovered in any other way.  Staff’s failure 
to afford KEDNY any opportunity to recover the unavoidable carrying costs associated with 
unreimbursable CSC expenditure is simply unreasonable. 
Similarly unreasonable is Staff’s claim that KEDNY’s proposed CSC-related working 
capital adjustment should be rejected because KEDNY is “at least partially, at fault for, and 
contributed to, the long lag time in getting reimbursed by the City for its reimbursable CSC costs.”  
Tr. 2416.  As KEDNY’s Revenue Requirement Panel explained: 
The primary cause of the backlog with respect to reimbursable CSC costs 
is the fact that NYC significantly increased its municipal work plan in 2017. 
This in turn increased CSC construction costs as well as requested 
reimbursements. In addition, NYC has also increased the audit scrutiny 
applied to reimbursable CSC costs, further increasing the payment lag. 
While KEDNY has been working with NYC representatives to attempt to 
improve the timing for processing CSC invoices, the simple fact of the 
matter is that NYC has policies and procedures in place that render it likely 
that there will be a 21-month lag between the time reimbursable CSC 
expenditures are booked and the time they are paid. The $28.273 million 
rate base adjustment proposed in this case for the Rate Year reflects 
KEDNY’s reasonable efforts to speed up collection of reimbursable CSC 
costs. The fact that KEDNY has attempted to speed up the recovery process 
provides no basis to require KEDNY to bear the full costs of financing 
unrecovered reimbursable CSC 3 costs. 
Tr. 2043-44. 
Finally, Staff’s claim that KEDNY should not recover the carrying costs on unpaid 
reimbursable CSC expenditure because KEDNY’s cost sharing agreement with NYC does not 
include a deadline for payment or the ability to assess a late payment charge for unpaid amounts 
disregards reality.  The Gas Facility Cost Allocation Act does not require NYC to pay KEDNY 
within a particular timeframe or require NYC to pay late payment charges.  Tr. 2044.  Thus, NYC 
pays its bills when it determines that it is administratively (Tr. 2415) and financially able to do so.  
While KEDNY has tried, and will continue to try, to reach an amended agreement with NYC, it is 


--- Page 139 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
134 of 310 
inconceivable that any such agreement will not permit NYC a significant amount of time to audit, 
process and pay KEDNY’s reimbursable CSC costs.  Tr. 2044.  The 21-month period underlying 
KEDNY’s cost recovery proposal in this case is the expected timeframe for payment for NYC for 
the foreseeable future.  Id. 
The Gas Facility Cost Allocation Act permits KEDNY to obtain substantial benefits for its 
customers by requiring NYC to reimburse KEDNY for certain CSC expenditures.  Once CSC 
expenditures are reimbursed, these costs do not affect customers’ rates.  Thus, KEDNY’s ability 
to obtain reimbursement of CSC-related capital costs has allowed KEDNY’s customers to save 
millions of dollars of costs. At the same time, the ability to obtain these cost savings imposes a 
cost on the Company due to the lag between the time the costs are incurred and the time they are 
paid.  Given the magnitude of the benefit to customers of CSC reimbursement, it is unreasonable 
to require the Company to bear the cost associated with the payment lag between the time the 
Company incurs CSC costs and the time those costs are reimbursed by NYC.  Accordingly, Staff’s 
proposal to the contrary should be rejected.

A. 
Return on Equity 
1. 
Introduction 
The Companies’ proposed return on equity of 9.65 percent is supported primarily by the 
testimony and exhibits of Ann E. Bulkley; a Senior Vice President of Concentric Energy Advisors, 
Inc. Tr. 4951.  Ms. Bulkley has over 20 years of experience in the energy industry and has provided 
expert testimony on cost of capital in more than 30 proceedings before state and federal regulators.  
Id., Exhibit 159. 
To estimate the Companies’ cost of equity, Ms. Bulkley employed three methods that have 
been utilized to establish the cost of equity in proceedings before the Commission for many years: 
(1) a multi-stage Discounted Cash Flow (“DCF”) analysis, and (2) two forms of the Capital Asset 
Pricing Method (“CAPM”).  Tr. 4953, 5000.  To develop her ROE recommendation, Ms. Bulkley 
developed two proxy groups: a Natural Gas Utility Proxy Group that consists entirely of natural 
gas distribution companies and properly recognizes that the return in this case is being established 


--- Page 142 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
137 of 310 
for two natural gas distribution companies, and a Combined Utility Proxy Group that includes both 
electric utilities and natural gas distribution utilities.  Tr. 4953.  To select the Combined Utility 
Proxy Group, Ms. Bulkley began with the companies that Value Line classifies as “Natural Gas 
Distribution Companies” and “Electric Utilities,” a group that includes 49 domestic U.S. utilities.  
Tr. 4991.  From this group, Ms. Bulkley eliminated: 
• companies that are not covered by at least two utility industry equity analysts; 
• companies that do not have investment grade corporate credit ratings and/or senior 
unsecured bond ratings according to Standard & Poor’s (“S&P) and Moody’s;82 
• companies that have not paid regular dividends or do not have positive earnings 
growth projections from at least one source; 
• companies that have owned electric generation comprising greater than 60 percent 
of their Megawatt Hour sales to ultimate customers; 
• companies with less than 70 percent of total operating income derived from 
regulated utility operations; and 
• companies known to be parties to a merger, acquisition or other transformational 
transactions. 
Tr. 4991-92.  Ms. Bulkley’s Combined Utility Proxy Group consisted of 20 gas and/or electric 
utility holding companies.  Tr. 4992-93. 
To select her Natural Gas Utility Proxy Group she began with the ten domestic U.S. utilities 
that fall within this group, and employed essentially the same criteria as for the Combined Utility 
Proxy Group except that she eliminated companies that derive less than 50 percent of their total 
                     
82 S&P and Moody’s, along with Fitch, are the major rating agencies that evaluate the Companies’ debt.  
Tr. 3310, 4990, 5233-34. 


--- Page 143 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
138 of 310 
operating income from regulated natural gas distribution operations.  Tr. 4998.  Ms. Bulkley’s 
Natural Gas Pricing Group consists of seven natural gas distribution utilities.  Tr. 4998-99. 
To obtain her estimate of the DCF83-derived cost of equity, Ms. Bulkley employed a multi-
stage form of the DCF model.  Tr. 5000.  The multi-stage form defines the cost of equity as the 
discount rate that sets the current price equal to discounted value of future cash flows and reflects 
the possibility that near five-year growth rates may not be reasonable in perpetuity, and that 
dividends payout ratios could vary over time.  Tr. 5004.  In the multi-stage model used by Ms. 
Bulkley, the first stage is a short-term growth period that consists of the first five years, the second 
period is a transition period that occurs over years six through ten and the third period is the long 
term growth period that begins in year eleven and continues to perpetuity.  Tr. 5005. 
Ms. Bulkley’s DCF results were based on the average market closing prices for the proxy 
companies over the three months ended February 28, 2019.  Tr. 5007.  In the first stage of the 
model she relied on three-to-five year earnings growth estimates reported by First Call, Zacks and 
Value Line.  Id.  For the third stage she relied on long term projected growth in Gross Domestic 
Product.  Id.  The second stage rate is a transition from the first stage to the long-term growth rate 
on a geometric average basis.  Id.  The long term growth rate of 5.56 percent is based on the real 
GDP growth rate from 1929 through 2018 of 3.22 percent and a projected inflation rate of 2.27 
percent derived from three sources.84  Tr. 5012.  Ms. Bulkley’s DCF analysis produced mean ROEs 
                     
83 The DCF is based on the theory that a stock’s current market price represents the present value of all 
expected future cash flows which are all expected future dividends.  Tr. 5004. 
84 The inflation projections were the (1) the average long-term growth rate in the Consumer Price Index 
(“CPI”) of 2.2 percent for 2025-2029 as reported by Blue Chip Financial Forecasts, (2) the compound 
annual growth rate for CPI for all urban consumers for 2029-2050 of 2.31 percent as projected by the Energy 
Information Administration in the Annual Energy Outlook 2018, and (3) the compound annual growth rate 
of the GDP Chain-type price index for 2029-2050 of 2.29 percent also reported in Annual Energy Outlook

of 9.04 percent from the Natural Gas Utility Proxy Group and 9.20 percent from the Combined

Ms. Bulkley employed two forms of the CAPM to derive her cost of equity, the traditional 
CAPM and the zero-beta CAPM, both of which have been used by the Commission in prior 
cases.85  Tr. 5021-22, 5025.  In her CAPM analyses, Ms. Bulkley utilized three estimates of the 
yield on 30-year Treasury bonds as her risk-free rate.  Tr. 5021-22.86  To determine the market-
risk premium, she used the expected return on the S&P 500 stock index calculated using a DCF 
model for all companies in the index based on market capitalization weighted growth rates and 
dividend yields less the 30-year Treasury bond yield.  Tr. 5022-23.  For the beta coefficient, Ms. 
Bulkley chose the average beta coefficient for proxy group companies as reported by Bloomberg 
and Value Line.87  Tr. 5023  Ms. Bulkley’s analyses produced a mean CAPM result for the Natural 
Gas Proxy Group of 10.95 percent and a mean CAPM result for the Combined Utility Proxy Group 
of 10.72 percent.  Tr. 5026-27. 
Ms. Bulkley asserted that the results of both the DCF and CAPM models have been affected 
by market conditions and, with traditional data inputs, have a tendency to underestimate the current 
cost of equity.  Tr. 5002.  Specifically, the DCF model is less reliable in current market conditions 
because dividend yields are at low levels that are not expected to continue.  Id.  Similarly, the 
results from the CAPM are also affected by current artificially low yields on Treasury bonds.  Id.

The SFP utilized a model that has been applied in other rate cases before the Commission 
to arrive at 8.2 percent ROE recommendation.  Notwithstanding the fact that the Companies are 
stand-alone gas distribution companies, to derive their ROE recommendation, the SFP did not 
consider any companies that are classified by Value Line as “Natural Gas Distribution 
Companies.”  Rather, the SFP used a proxy group of investment grade, Value Line listed electric 
and combination electric and gas utilities that had regulated revenues of at least 70 percent of total 
revenues, were currently paying common stock dividends, and were not currently included in any 
merger or acquisition activity.  Tr. 3134-35.  The SFP performed DCF and CAPM analyses and 
assigned two-thirds weight to the DCF result and one-third weight to the CAPM result.  Tr. 3152-
53.

price data for the period April 2019 to June 2019.  Tr. 3138.  In computing the growth factor to be 
used in the DCF, the SFP performed a two stage analysis.  Tr. 3139.  In the first stage applicable

beta CAPMs and derived an overall CAPM result of 8.04 percent.  Tr. 3149-51.  The SFP then 
weighted their DCF result 67 percent and their CAPM results 33 percent to arrive at a ROE 
recommendation of 8.16 percent that they rounded up to 8.2 percent.  Tr. 3152-53. 
As discussed more fully below, it is the Companies’ position that the ROE recommended 
by the SFP does not pass muster under longstanding applicable legal standards and would achieve 
an end result that is at odds with over forty-years of Commission practice of providing rate support 
to allow utilities to maintain low “A” rated long-term debt ratings.  The ROE recommended by the 
SFP is the product of its insistence on mechanically applying a methodology that has a number of 
inputs that produce unreasonably low returns. While the Companies recognize that many aspects

2. 
The Applicable Legal Standards 
Under the Public Service Law (“PSL”), the Commission is charged with setting rates that 
are “just and reasonable.”89  Over seventy-five years ago, the United States Supreme Court 
explained the meaning of this standard in Federal Power Commission v. Hope Natural Gas Co. 
(“Hope”).90  The Court stated: 
The rate-making process under the Act, i.e., the fixing of ‘just and 
reasonable’ rates, involves a balancing of the investor and the consumer 
interests.  Thus we stated in the Natural Gas Pipeline Co. case that 
‘regulation does not insure that the business shall produce net revenues.’ 
315 U.S. p. 590[.]  But such considerations aside, the investor interest has a 
legitimate concern with the financial integrity of the company whose rates 
are being regulated.  From the investor or company point of view it is 
important that there be enough revenue not only for operating expenses 
but also for the capital costs of the business.  These include service on the 
debt and dividends on the stock. Cf. Chicago & Grand Trunk Ry. Co. v. 
Wellman, 143 U.S. 339, 345-346[.]  By that standard the return to the 
equity owner should be commensurate with returns on investments in 
other enterprises having corresponding risks.  That return, moreover, 
should be sufficient to assure confidence in the financial integrity of the 
enterprise, so as to maintain its credit and to attract capital. See, State of 
Missouri ex rel. Southwestern Bell Tel. Co. v. Public Service Commission, 
262 U.S. 276, 291[] (Mr. Justice Brandeis concurring). 
 
Hope at 603 (Emphasis added).   
The notion of what constitutes a commensurate return “on investments in other enterprises 
having corresponding risks” was expanded upon by the Court in  Bluefield Waterworks & Imp. 
Co. v. Public Service Comm. Of W. Va., 262 U.S. 679, 692 (1923) where the Court stated: 
A public utility is entitled to such rates as will permit it to earn a return on 
the value of the property which it employs for the convenience of the public 
                     
89 Pub. Ser. Law § 65.(1) (McKinney’s 2011). 
90 320 U.S. 591, 64 S. Ct. 281, 88 L. Ed. 333 (1944). 


--- Page 148 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
143 of 310 
equal to that generally being made at the same time and in the same general 
part of the country on investments in other business undertakings which are 
attended by corresponding risks and uncertainties[.]   
 
In addition, in explaining how a regulatory commission should ensure that its results are 
just and reasonable, the Court in Hope emphasized that it was the end result that was of paramount 
importance.  The Court stated: 
We held in Federal Power Commission v. Natural Gas Pipeline Co., supra, 
that the Commission was not bound to the use of any single formula or 
combination of formulae in determining rates.  Its rate-making function, 
moreover, involves the making of “pragmatic adjustments[.]”  And when 
the Commission’s order is challenged in the courts, the question is whether 
that order “viewed in its entirety” meets the requirements of the Act[.]  
Under the statutory standard of “just and reasonable” it is the result 
reached not the method employed which is controlling.  Cf. Los Angeles 
Gas & Electric Corp. v. Railroad Commission, 289 U.S. 287, 304-305, 
314[;] West Ohio Gas co. v. Public Utilities Commission (No.1), 294 U.S. 
63, 70[;] West v. Chesapeake & Potomac Tel. Co., 295 U.S. 662, 692-693 
(dissenting opinion).  It is not theory but the impact of the rate order which 
counts. 
320 U.S. at 602.  (Emphasis added). 
On a number of occasions, New York’s courts have applied the Hope standards in 
proceedings involving review of Commission orders.91

4. 
The SFP’s ROE Recommendation Creates A Substantial Likelihood That 
Both Companies Will Be Downgraded And Is Therefore Contrary To The 
Legal Standards And Applicable Commission Policy 
 
In addition to failing to afford the Companies a reasonable opportunity to earn a return 
commensurate with the return being earned on investments in other enterprises having 
corresponding risks, the SFP’s 8.2 percent ROE recommendation creates a substantial likelihood 
that both KEDNY and KEDLI will be downgraded by Moody’s.  Each of the Companies currently 
maintains low “A” rated bond ratings from S&P and Moody’s.99  In its most recent credit opinion 
for each of the Companies, Moody’s stated that both Companies were facing a “negative outlook”

Companies’ long-term debt, and the consequences of such a downgrade, the Companies 
respectfully request that the ALJs and the Commission take notice of the Novel Coronavirus crisis 
and the impact that the crisis has had on financial markets.  While the SFP testified that it believed 
that its recommendation would at worst, lead to a one-notch downgrade with respect to KEDLI’s 
long-term debt, the fact is that the SFP does not control the rating of the Companies’ debt and has 
no special insight into what the ratings agencies may or may not do.  As the parties to the Generic 
Financing Proceeding recognized, the consequences for a utility falling below investment grade, 
or even to BBB, could be substantial.  See Exhibit 676 at 13.  Accordingly, in the current financial 
environment, the Companies urge the Commission to provide the Companies sufficient rate relief 
to preserve their current bond ratings. 
5. 
The Record Shows That The Commission Could Arrive At A Just And 
Reasonable ROE By Making A Number Of Changes To The SFP’s ROE 
Methodology That Are Supported By The Record 
 
As the Hope decision makes clear, it is the result reached, not the methodology employed, 
that determines whether a particular regulatory finding passes the test of being just and reasonable.  
In this case, overwhelming record evidence establishes that the end result of the SFP’s 8.2 percent 
ROE is not just and reasonable.  However, at the same time, the record shows that there are a 
number of adjustments that can and should be made to the SFP’s ROE methodology that would 
result in a ROE methodology that is logical, well-supported in both financial and academic theory 


--- Page 157 ---

of gas and electric distribution utilities.  In contrast, the SFP relied upon a single group of electric-

The SFP’s use of a proxy group comprised solely of electric utilities and combination gas 
and electric utilities to establish the ROEs for the Companies unreasonably fails to recognize that 
even though the Companies are ultimately owned by National Grid plc., they are not electric 
utilities, they are not considered electric utilities by the major financial ratings agencies110 and they 
do not face the same risks and opportunities as electric utilities.111  As the Commission recognized 
many years ago, utility companies may own assets or businesses that have differing degrees of 
risk, and it is appropriate, for example, in establishing the ROE for a regulated utility to make 
adjustments to eliminate the impact that riskier unregulated businesses may have on the regulated 
ROE.112  Similarly, where, as here, the record shows that the cost of equity for a gas distribution 
business may be greater than the cost of equity for an electric utility business, it is both reasonable 
and necessary to attempt to identify and properly recognize the difference by basing the ROE for 
gas distribution companies on a proxy group that reflects an appropriate number and weighting of 
gas distribution companies.

be accounted for in setting utility rates, so too should the Commission recognize and reflect in rates

b. The Companies’ Proposed Growth Rates Should Be Used In 
Determining The DCF Results 
The most significant difference between the Companies’ application of the DCF model and 
SFP’s approach is the growth rates used.  Tr. 5087.  Ms. Bulkley used a consensus of analysts’ 
earnings per share (“EPS”) growth rates for the proxy group companies as the near-term growth 
rate, and an estimate of growth in the overall economy for the long-term growth rate.  Id.  This 
mitigates the uncertainty associated with forecasting individual companies’ growth rates over very 
long time horizons.  Id.  By contrast, the SFP used more limited dividend growth projections from 
a single source (i.e., Value Line) for the near-term growth rate, and a “sustainable growth rate” for 
the long-term growth rate.  Tr. 5087-88. 
 
The SFP’s sole reliance on Value Line dividend growth projections for the near-term 
growth rate is less reliable than Ms. Bulkley’s approach because dividends are based on 
management decisions related to cash management and other factors, and therefore dividend 
growth rates are less likely to accurately reflect investors’ growth expectations than earnings 
growth rates.  Tr. 5087-88.  On the other hand, reliance on earnings growth is appropriate because 
earnings are the fundamental determinant of a company’s ability to pay dividends and because 
investment analysts primarily report and rely on earnings growth estimates.  Tr. 5089.  The use of 
earnings growth estimates to forecast the cost of equity is also extensively supported by academic 
research.116  Tr. 5008-11. 
                     
116 See also, Exhibit 655 at 8-10. 


--- Page 162 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
157 of 310 
With respect to long term growth rates, as discussed supra, Ms. Bulkley utilized an estimate 
of growth in the Gross Domestic Product (“GDP”) of 5.56 percent that was based on the historical 
average growth rate in real GDP for the period 1929 through 2018 -- 3.22 percent -- plus an 
expected average inflation rate of 2.27 percent.  Tr. 5012-13, Exhibit 161.  While the SFP criticized 
Ms. Bulkley’s forecast as being outside the range of certain current forecasts of GDP growth (Tr. 
3175-76), the record shows that economists believe that current forecasts of GDP growth are 
understated due to the impact of the financial crisis and great recession that began in 2007 on 
subsequent GDP growth.  Tr. 5094-95.  Under these circumstances, it is very reasonable to assume 
that GDP growth will return to levels approaching the historical average levels achieved since the 
great depression.117 
 
In contrast, the record shows that the SFP’s use of sustainable growth rate as the long-term 
growth rate in its two-stage DCF model is significantly flawed.  First, as explained by Ms. Bulkley, 
while the SFP asserts that its calculation of sustainable growth rate represents a measure of long-
term growth for the period 2024 and beyond, the inputs to its calculation are, for the most part, 
shorter-term estimates for the period 2019 and earlier.  Tr. 5092.  Therefore, the SFP’s long-term 
growth rate estimate reflects, at best, one analyst’s forecast of only the very early years of the 
second stage of the SFP’s DCF model, which theoretically extends 200 years.  Id., Tr. 3250.  In 
contrast, the Companies’ estimate of long-term GDP growth reflects inflation projections over 30 
years and considers overall measures of economic growth.118  Tr. 5092, Exhibit 161.

c. Both Basic Financial Theory And Common Sense Support The Use 
of Excel’s XIRR Function To Properly Measure Investor 
Expectations As To Cash Flows In The DCF Calculation 
As the SFP testifies, the DCF methodology assumes that the principles used to measure the 
cost of common equity are the same as those used in measuring the yield investors require on debt.  
Tr. 3137.  The foundation of the DCF is that investors will price common stock to equal the present 
value of future dividend payments.  Id. 
In this case, the DCF analyses conducted by both Ms. Bulkley and the SFP utilized proxy 
groups of publicly traded utilities that in all cases pay dividends quarterly.  Tr. 3338, 5099-5100.  
In calculating her DCF results, Ms. Bulkley utilized the Microsoft Excel XIRR function that allows 
the analyst to include future dividends in a manner that tracks quarterly dividends.  Tr. 5097.  In 
contrast, the SFP utilized the Microsoft Excel IRR function that assumes that all dividends are paid 
at the end of the year.  Tr. 5099.  This is simply not the case and thus the IRR function 
underestimates the cost of equity in the DCF analysis.  Tr. 5099-5100.  In this case, the 
understatement is between 11 and 21 basis points under Ms. Bulkley’s DCF analysis.  Tr. 5098. 
The SFP’s opposition to the use of the XIRR function was based in part on an analysis in 
which the SFP backcast the DCF results presented in Appendix B of Exhibit 652, the GFP 
Consensus Document, and concluded that the Recommended Decision in the Generic Financing 


--- Page 166 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
161 of 310 
Proceeding relied on the IRR rather than the XIRR function.124  Tr. 3180.  This is not surprising 
given that the XIRR was not fully integrated into Microsoft Excel until 2007, more than a decade 
after the issuance of the Recommended Decision.  Tr. 5100. 
What is more surprising however is that in reviewing the GFP Consensus Document, the 
SFP did not conclude that the Commission should use the XIRR function as a measure of 
attempting to update the ROE methodology to achieve the results originally intended by the 
signatories to the GFP Consensus Document.  As set forth therein, the signatory parties to the GFP 
Consensus Document – including Staff – recognized that the Commission’s methodology for 
establishing utility ROEs produced “volatile returns” that, were, among other things, highly 
interest-rate sensitive.  Exhibit 652 at 2-3.  Specifically, when viewed over a backcast period 
beginning in 1980, the Commission allowed returns that were generally higher than returns 
allowed by public utility commissions in other jurisdictions in the high interest rate environment 
of the early 1980s, lower than returns allowed by public utility commissions in other jurisdictions 
in the relatively low interest rate environment of the years immediately preceding the Generic 
Financing Proceeding, and lower than returns produced by other reasonable approaches, such as 
the CAPM.  Id.  Summing up the approach to the rate of return issue, the Consensus Document 
concluded: 
                     
124 On cross-examination, the SFP testified that the Commission’s decision in Case 08-E-0539, 
Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc. Order Setting Electric Rates (issued and effective April 
24, 2009) (“April 24 Order”) supported the SFP’s position concerning the use of the XIRR.  Tr. 3340.  In 
Case 08-E-0539, the Commission rejected requests that the DCF model reflect the payment of dividends 
quarterly based upon a finding about whether investors re-invest dividends and the impact of such decision.  
April 24 Order at 126-127.  Whatever the merits of that finding, it has no relevance here because the record 
support for the use of the XIRR model does not depend on how or whether investors re-invest dividends.  
As discussed above, the XIRR model properly captures the way the proxy group companies pay dividends 
today and thus accurately calculates the return required by investors under a DCF calculation.  The IRR 
model does not capture the payment of quarterly dividends in a manner consistent with the expectations of 
investors in the proxy group companies.

Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
162 of 310 
Appendix C summarizes the results of our approach to the rate of return 
issue.  The tables compare the allowed returns set by the Commission since 
1981 with the returns that would have been produced by our generic 
approach.  The averages and standard deviations of our proposed generic 
approach for the entire time horizon indicate that it produces returns that 
are, over time, comparable with, but less volatile than, the DCF-based 
returns set by the Commission over the 13-year backcast period.  Based on 
the results of the historical analysis, we conclude that if the past is a 
reasonable indicator of future economic conditions, then ratepayers will pay 
no more or less for return on equity and shareholders will not have the 
opportunity to earn more or less than they did on average during the last 13 
years.  However, because our approach reduces the volatility of the return 
on equity, it is likely that, in the future, ratepayers will experience less 
volatility in their bills and shareholders will experience less earnings 
volatility than they did during the past 13 years. 
 
In considering a backcast of the results of the Generic Financing Proceeding, the relevant 
question is not whether the signatory parties would have used the XIRR function in the Generic 
Financing Proceeding if that function had existed, but rather why is the SFP model producing 
returns that are so far removed from what was intended by the signatory parties to the GFP 
Consensus Document?  Had the SFP focused on this question, it would have been apparent that 
the Commission should approve the use of the XIRR function in performing DCF analyses because 
it more accurately captures the timing of actual cash flows received and required by investors 
consistent with a well-recognized financial principle that investors place value on the timing in 
which cash flows are received.  Tr. 5099. 
 
In this case, the SFP appears to recognize that all present value calculations discount future 
cash flow payments to account for the time value of money.  Exhibit 650.  At the same time, the 
SFP testified that a rational investor would be indifferent to whether the investor received four 
payments of $25 equally spaced throughout the year125 or one payment of $100 at the end of the 
year.  Tr. 3344.  Absent an assumption that the time value of money is 0 percent, the Companies 
                     
125 Such as on March 31, June 30, September 30 and December 31. 


--- Page 168 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
163 of 310 
do not believe that these statements can be reconciled.  It is a fundamental economic and financial 
principle that rational investors would clearly prefer to receive their payouts more quickly,126 and 
the XIRR function, unlike the IRR function, permits the actual timing of cash flows to be reflected 
in the results of DCF analysis.  Accordingly, the ROE established in this proceeding should reflect 
the use of the XIRR function to properly capture the timing of quarterly dividend payments. 
d. It Is Completely Reasonable And Appropriate To Use Bloomberg 
Betas In The CAPM Calculation 
In performing their CAPM analyses both Ms. Bulkley and the SFP utilize Value Line beta 
coefficients which are calculated over a five-year period.  Tr. 5107, 5110, 5023.  In addition to 
Value Line betas, Ms. Bulkley uses Bloomberg beta coefficients based on a ten-year period.  Tr.  
5023-25, 5106.  This is particularly important in this case because it mitigates the disparate impact 
of the passage of the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act (“TCJA”) on utility betas as compared to betas of 
other companies in the marketplace.  Tr. 5024, 5106.  As Ms. Bulkley explained, while other 
industries were able to retain the benefits of a reduced corporate tax rate from the TCJA, utilities 
were generally required to pass this benefit through to customers.  Tr. 5024.  As a consequence, 
the TCJA’s effect was to produce a significant change in the returns on the utility industry relative 
to the broader market; that is, utility returns temporarily under-performed the overall market.  Id.  
This change in relative returns results in a drop in utility betas even though the relative volatility 
of utility stocks compared to the performance of S&P 500 is essentially unchanged.  Tr. 5107. 
In this case, the record shows that in the Companies’ last base rate proceedings, the Value 
Line beta for the Staff proxy group was 0.75 compared to 0.61 in those proceedings.  Tr. 5108.  As 
the SFP agreed on cross-examination, the impact of TCJA could be a factor that affected the 
calculation of utility betas.  Tr. 3299-3300.  At the same time, it is unquestionably the case that the 
                     
126 See. Tr. 5099. 


--- Page 169 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
164 of 310 
major ratings agencies believed that the passage of the TCJA was something that increased the 
risk of utility debt.  Tr. 4984-88, 3126-27.  As the SFP acknowledged, the impact of the TCJA on 
utility cash flow may not be permanent but is something that has had a negative impact on utilities 
over the past few years.  Tr. 3302. 
The record shows that the SFP’s reliance solely on Value Line betas is responsible for a 
108 basis point decrease between Staff’s CAPM result in Cases 16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059 and 
the SFP’s CAPM result in this case.127  Tr. 5108.  To the extent that this is the product of a 
calculation of beta that has been overly influenced by the impact of the passage of the TCJA, it is 
reasonable to consider other beta measures such as the Bloomberg betas utilized by Ms. Bulkley.  
In making this suggestion, the Companies are not suggesting that Value Line betas should be 
ignored.  Rather, it is the Companies’ position that the Commission should consider and reflect in 
its ROE determination the impact of both Value Line and Bloomberg betas.  As the record shows, 
doing so would increase the SFP’s ROE recommendation by 7 basis points.  Tr. 5065. 
e. In Determining The CAPM Results, The Commission Should Not 
Limit Its Consideration To A Single Estimate Of The Market 
Return 
To calculate the required return on the market as whole to be used in the CAPM analysis 
Ms. Bulkley utilized the expected return on the S&P 500 index calculated using a DCF model for 
all companies in the index based on market-capitalization weighted growth rates and dividend 
yields.  Tr. 5022-23.  In contrast, the SFP relied upon an estimate provided by Merrill Lynch for 
the months of May through July 2019.  Exhibit 443. 
While the SFP suggests that use of a Constant Growth DCF model to determine the CAPM 
market return is not appropriate because the Companies in the S&P 500 cannot maintain their three 
                     
127 Staff’s CAPM result in Cases 16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059 was 9.24 percent as compared to 8.04 percent 
in this case. 


--- Page 170 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
165 of 310 
to five year growth rates in perpetuity, the SFP’s criticisms ignore the fact that the composition of 
the S&P 500 changes over time depending on individual company results.  Tr. 5112.  Therefore, 
investor expectations for the index as a whole may not change even though investor expectations 
for individual companies may vary.  Id.  Thus, it is reasonable to assume that the average growth 
of S&P 500 could be sustainable in the long run and it is therefore a reasonable estimate of the 
market return used in the CAPM analysis.  Id.  The record shows that the use of this estimate of 
the long run market return would increase the SFP’s recommended ROE by approximately 70 
basis points.  Tr. 5065. 
 
In addition, the record shows that the market-return that the SFP relies on is more than 100 
basis points below market return estimates provided by such reputable sources as S&P, Yahoo! 
Finance and Bloomberg Professional, which range from 12.23 percent to 14.42 percent.  Tr. 5112.  
As is the case with many of the aspects of the SFP’s ROE methodology, the estimate of the market 
return is based on a single input that the SFP deems to be reliable without providing any real 
explanation as to why this is the case.  While the Companies do not believe that the SFP’s Merrill 
Lynch estimate should not be considered, they see no reason why one input should be favored 
above all others.  Accordingly, the Companies request that the Commission consider the various 
estimates of the market return provided in the record rather than relying solely on the Merrill Lynch 
estimate. 
f. The Commission Should Reconsider Its Practice Of Using The 
Average Of Historic 10 and 30-Year Treasury Bond Yields In 
Determining The Risk Free Rate To Be Used In The CAPM 
Analysis 
Consistent with prior Commission decisions, the SFP relied on the average of 10-year and 
30-year historic Treasury bond yields in its CAPM analysis.  Tr. 3145, 3193-94.  The SFP justifies 
its position not only by reference to prior Commission decisions but also by its view that this 


--- Page 171 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
166 of 310 
practice captures the expectations of investors who have “intermediate-term as well as long-term 
investment horizons.”   Tr. 3194. 
In contrast to the SFP, Ms. Bulkley relied on both historical and projected yields on 30-
year Treasury securities to determine the risk-free rate.128  Tr. 5021-22.  Ms. Bulkley’s position is 
supported by the financial and academic literature and decisions in other jurisdictions.  Tr. 5022, 
5102-03.  With respect to the SFP’s claim that using an average of 10-year and 30-year Treasury 
yield best captures investor’s time horizon, the record shows that the relevant horizon is that 
associated with the investment, not the investor,129 and the use of 30-year bond yields is consistent 
with the long term investment horizon for gas and electric assets.  Tr. 5102. 
The Companies recognize that the Commission has adopted the SFP’s position concerning 
the CAPM risk-free rate in prior cases.  However, as discussed by Ms. Bulkley, artificially low 
interest rates have made the results of various ROE estimation methods more unreliable.  Tr. 5103.  
This is particularly true given the recent market turmoil. 
The Companies submit that the most recent three-month average of 10-year and 30-year Treasury 
bond yields is anomalously low and should not be used in the CAPM method to determine forward 
looking rates of return.  In the past, in response to anomalous or unusual circumstances in financial 
markets the Commission has made changes to its methodology for determining ROEs to address 
                     
128 Ms. Bulkley utilized (i) the current three-month average yield on 30-year Treasury bonds, (ii) the

With respect to the determination of the risk-free rate to be used in the CAPM, Dr. Morin states: 
“At the conceptual level, because common stock is a long term investment, and because 
the cash flow to investors in the flow of dividends last indefinitely, the yield on very long 
term government bonds, mainly the yield on 30-year Treasury bonds, is the best measure 
of the risk-free rate for use in the CAPM and Risk Premium methods.”

such conditions.130  In this case, the Companies believe that the Commission should consider using

market conditions that currently affect the interest rates on government securities. The current 
forward looking rates on 30-year Treasury securities are at the low end of the range of historical 
rates but would nonetheless provide a more reliable CAPM result than an average of current yields 
on 10-year and 30-year Treasury bonds. 
6. 
Conclusion 
The record shows that the Companies’ proposed ROE of 9.65 percent is appropriate, 
reasonable and commensurate with the returns being authorized for and projected on investments 
in comparable enterprises having corresponding risks.  In contrast, the SFP’s ROE 
recommendation does not meet the just and reasonable standard because it is not comparable to 
the returns available to investors in companies with corresponding risks and it will likely not permit 
the Companies to support their current credit ratings. 
B.

1. 
The Companies And The SFP Agree That A Capital Structure Containing 
A 48 Percent Equity Component Should Be Used For Ratemaking 
Purposes In This Proceeding 
While they may arrive at the decision in somewhat different ways, both the Companies and 
the SFP agree that the capital structure used for ratemaking purposes in this proceeding should 
reflect a 48 percent equity component.  Tr. 3112, 5206, 5230. 
                     
130 The record shows that, the Commission’s ROE methodology has evolved over time.  Tr. 5058-59.

model and the bond yields used in the CAPM from six months to three months.  Tr. 5062-63.  The 
Commission has also changed how it estimates the market risk premium.  Id.

C. 
Long Term Debt: Forecast Versus Existing Interest Rates 
1. 
The Commission Should Reconsider Its Policy Of Using Updated Interest 
Rates Instead Of Forecast Rates 
In KEDNY’s initial filing herein, it included a planned issuance of $400 million of new 
long term debt in the Rate Year at an interest rate of 3.83 percent.  Tr. 3127-28, 5216.  The SFP 
recommends an interest rate of 3.24 percent.  Tr. 3128-29.  The SFP does not agree with KEDNY’s 
proposed use of forecast Treasury yields to determine the interest rate for the planned Rate Year 
debt issuance.  Tr. 3128-29.    However, both the Companies and the SFP recommend updating 
the interest rate at the time of the Commission’s decision.  Tr. 3129, 5252-53. 
While the Companies recognize that the Commission has used actual interest rates at the 
time it issues its decision to establish long term debt interest rates for planned issuances in past 
cases,131 the Companies urge the Commission to reconsider its practice in light of current 
conditions in the financial market-place.  Specifically, the Federal Reserve has set short term 
interest rates at or close to 0 percent and the yields on some long term Treasury securities are 
hovering near or less than one percent.  Under these circumstances, KEDNY submits that the 
forecast rates available to the Companies at the time the Commission issues its decision in these 
cases are likely to be the best approximation of the rates that will be available to KEDNY for the 
issuance of debt later in the Rate Year.  The forward looking rates used by KEDNY in these 
proceedings are widely used in financial markets and fully consistent with the Commission’s 
policy of using a fully forecast test period to set rates.  Tr. 5253.  In addition, there is a reasonable 
and verifiable link between current interest rates and those forecast from the Rate Year and there 
is no reason for the Commission to ignore that link.  Id.  For all these reasons, it is appropriate for 
the Commission to approve the use of forecast interest rates in this case. 
                     
131 Tr. 5253.

D. 
Credit Metrics 
1. 
Under a Forecast Of Credit Metrics That Properly Reflects Staff’s 
Proposed Revenue Requirement Neither KEDNY Nor KEDLI Meets 
Moody’s Standards For An “A3” Long Term Debt Rating 
In assessing whether the end result of a particular authorized revenue requirement meets 
the legal standards established in the Hope and Bluefield decision, it is critical that the Commission 
assess whether the end result of its decision will meet the metrics established by the major ratings 
agencies, Moody’s and S&P, to allow the Companies to maintain their current low “A” credit 
ratings.132  With respect to these metrics, one area where the Companies and the SFP disagree is 
whether the Staff revenue requirement would permit the Companies to maintain their current “A3” 
long term debt rating under Moody’s core metric which is the CFO pre-WC/Debt rating.  As 
discussed supra, Moody’s has stated that both Companies’ long term debt ratings could be 
downgraded if the Companies are not able to achieve CFO-Pre-WC/debt results of “high teens in 
percentage terms on an underlying basis from fiscal 2021 onwards.”133  See Exs. 658, 659.   
In Exhibit 645, the SFP presented its calculation of the impact of Staff’s proposed revenue 
requirement on Moody’s CFO-metric.  The SFP indicated that (1) KEDNY’s results ranged from 
17.29 percent to 19.69 percent depending on whether certain “Pension & OPEB”134 deferral and 
accrual amount balances and uncollectible amounts reflected in an information request response 
provided by the Companies were included in the metric results, and (2) KEDLI’s results ranged 
from 13.87 percent to 15.64 percent depending on whether the pension and OPEB and 
uncollectible amounts were included in the results.  See Exhibit 645 at 2-3.  The SFP indicated 
that it believed that the pension and OPEB and uncollectible amounts should be considered in the

E. 
Earnings Sharing Mechanism 
1. 
The Staff’s Proposal To Impose An Earnings “Sharing” Mechanism On 
The Companies In Years Beyond The Rate Year Should Be Rejected 
 
In addition to establishing rates based on a 8.2 percent ROE that would be the lowest 
established by this or any other regulatory commission within the past forty years, the Staff Policy 
Panel (“SPP”) further proposes that the Commission impose an Earnings “Sharing” Mechanism 
(hereafter “Earnings Mechanism”) on the Companies that would apply in the years beyond the 
Rate Year – the twelve months ending March 31, 2021.  Tr. 3580-81.  Under the proposed Earnings 
Mechanism, to the extent that the Companies did not file revised base rates applicable to periods 
beyond the Rate Year, the Companies would be required to defer for future return to customers 
earnings in excess of the percentages returns set forth below:

174 of 310 
Tr. 3581-82.  The Companies submit that to the extent that the PSL authorizes the Commission to 
impose the proposed Earnings Mechanism on the Companies in a litigated one-year rate case,140 
the record evidence does not support a finding that the proposed Earnings Mechanism is just and 
reasonable. 
At the outset of this discussion, the Companies do not dispute that the adoption of earnings 
sharing mechanisms in the context of negotiated settlements/joint proposals that establish rates 
over a multi-year period is completely reasonable and appropriate.  The Companies have supported 
such mechanisms in a number of proceedings.141  As discussed more fully below, such mechanisms 
are particularly appropriate when they are tied to a settlement that reflects a multi-year stay out 
commitment.  However, such circumstances are not present here. 
In order to lawfully adopt the proposed Earnings Mechanism, the Commission must, at a 
minimum, find on the basis of substantial evidence, that the Earnings Mechanism will be just and 
reasonable142 during the period in which it will be in effect – in this case, the period beyond March 
31, 2021.  As discussed supra, in order to meet the just and reasonable standard, the Commission 
must determine that mechanisms, coupled with the Companies’ current rates will afford the 
Companies a reasonable opportunity to earn a return on equity that is commensurate with the 
returns earned by enterprises facing corresponding risks.  No such finding can be made in this case 
                     
140 The Companies acknowledge that the Commission imposed a similar earnings mechanism on one

judicially.  See National Fuel Gas Distribution Corp. v. Public Service Commission, 169 A.D.3d 1334 (3rd 
Dept. 2019), App. Denied, 33 N.Y.3d 1053 (2019) (“National Fuel”).  However, the Companies are not 
asserting, as was the case in National Fuel, that the proposed earnings mechanism violates the rule against 
retroactive ratemaking or applicable precedent that requires utilities to be treated equally to other parties.  
Rather, as discussed more fully, infra, the Companies are contending that the Earnings Mechanism 
proposed in this case has not been demonstrated to be just and reasonable based on the record of this case.

1. The Companies’ customers will have paid rates that reflect an 8.2 percent ROE; Con

The point here is not that the Companies should be entitled in a litigated one-year rate case 
to the same results as another utility that is able to negotiate a three-year settlement.  Rather, the 
point is that there is no plausible reason for the Commission to impose an Earnings Mechanism on 
companies whose customers are paying rates that reflect a significantly lower ROE and that 
somehow find a way to achieve incremental efficiencies that (1) cause no harm to their customers 
today, but (2) will provide substantial incremental benefits to customers when future rate cases are 
filed. 
                     
150 While Con Edison and the Companies are not completely comparable in terms of size of rate base, the

return for three years, KEDNY’s customers will derive a cost savings of approximately $58.4 million and 
KEDLI’s customers will derive savings of approximately $37.8 million. 
151 These savings are equal to sixty basis points of earnings based on Staff’s proposed revenue requirement.

From the Companies’ perspective, it is poor regulatory policy for the Commission to 
impose an Earnings Mechanism that effectively discourages the Companies from finding ways to 
manage their business in a manner that minimizes the need for time consuming and expensive rate 
proceedings.152  In addition, in proposing an Earnings Mechanism in this case, the SPP provided 
no evidence whatsoever that the Companies had in the past earned excessive or unlawful profits 
that might provide some support for an Earnings Mechanism.  Moreover, the Commission has 
ample authority to act on the Companies’ rates if it believes that the Companies’ earnings have 
reached an unjust and unreasonable level.153  This includes the authority to make rates temporary 
while conducting an investigation.154  Under these circumstances, there is no reason for the 
Commission to impose an additional Earnings Mechanism on the Companies.  The SPP’s proposal 
is an unjust and unreasonable solution to a non-existent problem.  It should be rejected.

B. 
ECOS Study 
1. 
The Commission Should Utilize The Companies’ Forward-Looking (Pro-
Forma) ECOSS For Revenue Allocation And Rate Design In These 
Proceedings 
The Companies’ embedded cost of service studies (“ECOSS”), which are contained in 
Exhibit 726 (RDP-3ATS) for KEDNY and Exhibit 726 (RDP-3BTS) for KEDLI, are forward-
looking (pro-forma) studies.  The Companies’ ECOSS presents, inter alia, the costs that the 
Companies expect to incur in the Rate Year, the allocation of costs to each service class, the 
calculation of the return provided by each service class and the comparison of those returns to the 
system average rate of return.  Tr. 2649, 2656-57, 2659.  The Companies used the same forward-
looking approach in prior rate cases, including the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.155  This 
is also the approach used by the Companies’ Upstate New York affiliate, Niagara Mohawk, most 
recently in Cases 17-E-0238 and 17-G-0239.156   
                     
155 See Cases 16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059, Direct Testimony of the Rate Design Panel, pages 11-19. 
156 See Cases 17-G-0239, et al., Direct Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel, pages 13-22. 
 


--- Page 185 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
180 of 310 
Staff proposes using an historic ECOSS instead of the Company’s pro-forma study.  Tr. 
2943, lines 11-13.  The historic ECOSS that Staff supports allocates the costs incurred during the 
Historic Test Year among the various service classifications.157  Staff argues that a pro-forma 
ECOSS can introduce additional errors in its results.  Tr. 2943, lines 13-15.  Staff claims that use 
of an historic cost of service study is more appropriate unless “very large changes in utility 
operations are projected.”  Id., lines 15-18.   
The Companies recommend the Commission utilize their pro-forma ECOSS that reflects 
the costs, rate base, and physical units that are projected for the Rate Year, and for which the 
revenue requirement, revenue allocation, and rate design will be set.  Tr. 2814, lines 18-20.  The 
Companies acknowledge that actual conditions in the Rate Year may differ from the projections 
factored into the ECOSS.  However, the same is true for an historic ECOSS.  Tr. 2814-15, lines 
21 and lines 1-5.  Specifically, historic allocator values may not reflect normal weather, and 
Historic Test Year costs will likely differ, in amount and type, from the costs included in the Rate 
Year.  Id.   
Staff explicitly acknowledged that one of the goals of the ratemaking process is to establish 
rates that move each rate class closer to the system average rate of return, so that the rates will 
reflect cost causation.  Tr. 3028, lines 13-17.  However, Staff admits that an historic ECOSS cannot 
measure that movement toward the system average rate of return until the next rate case.  Tr. 3029, 
lines 5-12.  Thus, a historic ECOSS fails to accomplish one of the fundamental goals of performing 
an ECOSS – to measure the rate classes’ movement toward the system average rate of return.  Staff 
also acknowledged that a historic ECOSS will not be normalized for weather, and that it may not 
reflect non-recurring costs that are normalized out of the Historic Test Year.  Tr. 3030, lines 3-13.
