2. 
Distribution Main Costs Should Be Classified As Partially Demand-
Related And Partially Customer-Related 
Distribution mains are installed (i) to connect customers to the Companies’ gas system, as 
well as (ii) to provide capacity to meet customer demand on a design day.  Tr. 2653, lines 9-11.  
Thus, distribution main costs are both customer-related and demand-related.  Id., at 11-12.  To 
determine how much of the main costs should be classified between the two categories, the 
Companies performed a minimum system study.  Id., at 13-14.  A minimum system study looks at 
what would be the cost to install mains to serve customers if there were minimal or zero demand 
or usage.  The premise is that this is the cost of mains infrastructure needed simply to connect 
customers.  Based on that minimum-system study, the Companies determined a ratio of costs 
appropriately classified as customer-related, with the remaining balance classified as demand-
related.  The Commission has accepted the Companies’ classification of main as both demand- 
and customer-related in prior rate cases, including the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.158  
Also, as cited by NYC (Tr. 5570-71), the Commission has accepted the classification of main as

G-0202, et al., and Case 17-G-0239, et al.

Revenue Allocation  
1. 
The Companies Proposed Revenue Allocation Using Tolerance Bands

The Companies’ proposed revenue allocation is contained in Exhibit 727 (RDP-4ATS),

proposed revenue allocations aim to bring each service class closer to unity (supporting cost 
causation principles) while mitigating extreme impacts to any one class.  Tr. 2817, lines 6-8.  The 
Companies proposed the use of tolerance bands to separate service classes as those that are over-, 
under-, or near/at the system average of return.  Id. at 8-15.  Using a tolerance band of 25 percent, 
those service classes that produced a rate of return 25 percent or greater than the system average 
rate of return (meaning that they are subsidizing other service classes) were assigned lower 
percentage revenue increases than those service classes that produced a rate of return 25 percent 
or less than the system average rate of return (meaning that they were being subsidized by other 
service classes).  Id.  Those service classes that were within the 25 percent tolerance band (between 
25 percent above and below the average return) were assigned revenue percentage increases close 
to the average increase.  Id.  The tables below summarize the revenue increases (by percentage) 
each rate class was given depending on whether it was within, above, or below the tolerance band.

As can be seen above, the Companies’ proposed revenue allocation is consistent – those 
rate classes with greater rate of return at present rates receive smaller percentage increases to the

Staff’s revenue allocation proposal, which is submitted as Exhibit 523, should be rejected.  
As an initial matter, Staff uses an historic ECOSS, which should be rejected for the reasons 
discussed above.  Because a historic ECOSS, by its definition, does not reflect the costs that will 
be incurred during the Rate Year, or the normalized revenue, it cannot be used to measure the rate 
classes’ movement toward unity. 
Further, Staff’s revenue allocation produces inconsistent results.  For example, for 
KEDNY, Staff shows that SC 2-1, SC 4A (HLF) and SC 4B produce similar returns at present 
rates of 4.47, 4.47, and 4.34 times the system average rate of return, respectively.  Exhibit 523, 
page 1.  However, Staff recommends revenue target increases for these classes of 2.89%, 5.15% 
and 1.23%, respectively.  Id.  Staff does not offer any explanation as to why three rate classes with 
very similar returns at present rates are given very different increases.  The inconsistencies of 
Staff’s revenue allocation are further illustrated in the graphs below.

D. 
Rate Design 
1. 
The Commission Should Adopt The Companies’ Rate Design Proposals 
For The Firm Service Classes 
 
Each firm service class has a fixed, minimum charge applicable to all customers, as well 
as a volumetric rate (or rates).  Tr. 2825, lines 10-12.  The Companies’ rate design proposals are 
intended to (i) collect the revenue target determined in the revenue allocation process; (ii) avoid 


--- Page 193 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
188 of 310 
severe, disproportionate impacts to customer subgroups within a service class; and (iii) provide 
price signals to impact usage patterns, including promoting energy conservation.  Tr. 2664-65, 
lines 17-21 and lines 1-2. 
The Companies’ latest rate design proposals are available on Exhibit 727 (RDP-4ATS), 
Schedule 3 for KEDNY and Exhibit 727 (RDP-4BTS), Schedule 3 for KEDLI.  For service classes 
with rate blocks (different rates based on usage level), the Companies’ proposed rate design 
reduces the decline of the slope (ratio of the penultimate block rate to the tail block rate).  This 
“flattening” of the rate blocks (meaning the tail block, applicable to the highest usage level, is now 
more expensive) is intended to incentivize consumers to reduce consumption.  While the rate 
design can only be finalized based on the revenue requirement that is ultimately approved by the 
Commission, the Companies support rate design that encourages energy conservation.  However, 
it must balance that interest with the goal of aligning rates with costs.  Thus, it is important to 
recover a part of the costs through the minimum charge and the rest through volumetric rates.  
Staff’s rate design proposals are contained in Exhibit 524.  Staff’s rate design diverged 
from that of the Companies primarily because of the significant difference in the proposed revenue 
requirement.  Because Staff’s proposed revenue requirement was lower, Staff’s rate design reduces 
the slope more than do the Companies’ proposals.  However, Staff acknowledged that a higher 
revenue requirement would require a more gradual reduction in slope.  Tr. 2958, lines 8-11.  The 
Companies submit that the Commission should utilize the Companies’ proposed rate design for 
the firm service classes, as set forth in Exhibit 727.

F. 
Non-Firm Service Classes 
1. 
The Commission Should Adopt the Companies’ Proposed Rate Design 
For The Non-Firm Demand Response Service Classifications 
 
Pursuant to the Commission’s Order Approving Tariff Revisions and Directing Further 
Tariff Filings (“TC/IT Order”) issued and effective February 7, 2019 in Cases 16-G-0058 and 16-
G-0059, the Companies established new non-firm demand response (“NFDR”) service 
classifications in place of the existing temperature controlled (“TC”) and interruptible (“IT”) 
service classifications.  Specifically, the NFDR service class is a two-tier structure differentiated 
by the customer’s fuel switching equipment and the designated interruption temperatures.  Tr. 
2693, lines 1-20.  Tier 1 customers must have fully automatic switchover equipment, whereas Tier 
2 customers are permitted to have fully automatic, semi-automatic, or manual switchover 
equipment.  Id.  Currently, the interruption temperature for Tier 1 customers is 16 degrees 
Fahrenheit, and 20 degrees Fahrenheit for Tier 2 customers. 
Staff disagrees with certain aspects of the Companies’ proposed non-firm rate design, as 
highlighted below. 
a. Staff’s Proposal On How To Set Rates For The Non-Firm Demand 
Response Service Classifications Produce Inconsistent Results 
Among Similarly Situated Customers 
 
Staff does not believe that the Companies’ proposed non-firm rate design provides 
sufficient incentives for customers to choose non-firm service over firm service.  Tr. 2964, lines 
7-16.  Instead, Staff proposes non-firm rates be set such that the total bill of a non-firm customer 
is 30 percent (for Tier 1) and 40 percent (for Tier 2) less than the total bill of the otherwise 


--- Page 195 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
190 of 310 
applicable firm service.  Tr. 2964-65, lines 19-24 and lines 1-2.  Staff’s proposal should be rejected 
because setting the discounts on a total bill basis could lead to different discounts between non-
firm sales customers and non-firm transportation customers.  A total bill for a firm sales customer 
includes the delivery charge and the cost of commodity supplied by KEDNY or KEDLI, whereas 
a total bill for a firm transportation customer would consist of the delivery charge and the cost of 
commodity charged by an energy service company (“ESCO”).  The commodity cost charged by 
the Companies may differ from that of an ESCO.  Under that scenario, if a firm sales customer 
and a firm transportation customer consume the same quantity of gas under the same service 
classification, the delivery charge would be the same for both customers, but the commodity cost 
would be different.  That also means the total bill of the firm sales customer and the total bill of 
the firm transportation customer would be different.  Consequently, to produce a total bill that is 
30 percent less than that of the applicable firm service (for Tier 1), the delivery rate of a non-firm 
sales customer would necessarily be different from that of a non-firm transportation customer; the 
only way to achieve the same discount on a total bill basis would be to back into the delivery rate, 
resulting in different delivery rates for non-firm sales and transportation customers.  This results 
in a different treatment of similarly situated customers who both agree to be interrupted at the same 
temperature.160  As such, Staff’s proposal violates Sections 65(2), 65(3) and 66(12)(d) of the 
PSL,161 because it proposes to treat similarly situated customers differently without any rational 
                     
160 Under the Joint Proposal adopted by the Commission in Cases 17-G-0202, et al., Niagara Mohawk was 
required to consider tariff modifications in its next rate filing to ensure that similarly-situated customers are 
subject to the same delivery rates regardless of their commodity supplier.  (See page 31 of the Joint 
Proposal).  The difference in the delivery rates for non-firm sales and transportation customers resulting 
from Staff’s proposal in the instant case would be directly contradictory to the objective of the requirement 
approved by the Commission in the Niagara Mohawk Joint Proposal. 
161 PSL §§ 65(2), 65(3) and 66(12)(d) (McKinney’s 2011). 
 


--- Page 196 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
191 of 310 
justification.162  Thus, Staff’s proposal to calculate the discount on a total bill basis should be 
rejected.  Instead, the Commission should adopt the Companies’ proposed rates as set forth in 
Exhibit 727, which the Companies submit sufficiently reflect the difference in the level of service 
and provide sufficient discounts from the otherwise applicable firm rates to properly incentivize 
customers to choose non-firm service. 
b. Staff’s Proposal That The Companies Use The Incremental Cost Of 
Gas To Calculate The Non-Firm Gas Cost Should be Rejected 
 
The Companies propose to include gas costs incurred for and recovered from the non-firm 
demand response service classification in their annual gas cost reconciliation.  Non-firm customers 
will share in the over/under recoveries of gas costs that currently only apply to firm sales 
customers.  Pursuant to the Order Authorizing Combined Gas Portfolios issued October 28, 2005 
in Case 05-G-0903, KEDNY and KEDLI manage their gas supply portfolio on a combined basis.  
Tr. 2834, lines 4-21.  The gas supply planning process accounts for gas to supply non-firm 
customers up to the lowest designated interruption temperature.  Id.  Therefore, all sales customers 
should contribute to the costs of the entire supply portfolio.  Id.  To that end, the Companies 
proposed to calculate the non-firm cost of gas using the same formula used to calculate the cost of 
gas for the former TC service class.  Tr. 2833, lines 12-19.  The former TC and IT service 
classifications both currently use a weighted average cost of gas (“WACOG”) to calculate the 
applicable gas costs, but the former includes a demand charge while the latter does not.163  Id.  In 
this rate filing, the Companies propose that all customers under the new NFDR service 
classification would receive a demand charge.   
                     
162 See, Matter of Lefkowitz v. Public Service Commission, 40 N.Y.2d 1047, 1048 (1976).  See also, Matter 
of New York State Electric & Gas Company v. Public Service Commission, 308 A.D.2d 108,115 (3rd Dept. 
2003). 
163 The demand charge reflects the per unit 100 percent load factor fixed cost associated with the pipeline 
transportation path that is delivering the forecasted highest cost of baseload gas for that month.  Tr. 2882. 


--- Page 197 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
192 of 310 
Staff proposes that the Companies use the Incremental Cost of Gas (“ICOG”) to calculate 
the non-firm sales service cost of gas, instead of the WACOG as proposed by the Companies.  Ex. 
644 at 14.  The Companies’ ICOG calculation reflects the highest cost supply including 
commodity, variable cost, and the 100 percent load factor per unit fixed costs of the pipeline 
transportation path.  Ex. 641 at 47-49.  Currently, the Companies only utilize this ICOG calculation 
when assessing imbalance cash outs for a handful of daily balanced non-firm customers. 
Staff’s proposal to use the ICOG should be rejected for two primary reasons.  First, it 
contradicts Staff’s goal of incentivizing customers to choose non-firm service over firm service.  
The ICOG, by definition, is almost always higher than the WACOG.  Tr. 2834, line 14.  See e.g., 
Ex. 641 at 41-45.  This would counter the impact of any incentive created by the fact that non-firm 
service classes reflect lower non-firm delivery rates compared to the otherwise applicable firm 
rate.  Second, Staff’s argument that using the WACOG results in firm customers subsidizing non-
firm customers is incorrect.  The times when the ICOG is likely to be set (e.g., the coldest days) 
are the times when non-firm customers can expect to be interrupted.  Tr. 2834, lines 16-18.  When 
non-firm customers are interrupted they do not cause the Companies to purchase gas that is priced 
at the ICOG.  Therefore, using the ICOG instead of WACOG would, in essence, charge non-firm 
customers the very costs that they are trying to avoid, further reducing their incentive to choose 
non-firm service.   
In addition, because, as discussed supra, the Companies propose that non-firm customers 
should be included in the annual gas cost reconciliation any subsidies between the firm and non-
firm service classifications would be eliminated.  Tr. 2835, lines 9-12.  For these reasons, the 
Commission should reject the Staff’s proposal to calculate the non-firm cost of gas using the 
ICOG. 


--- Page 198 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
193 of 310 
c. The Companies Must be Able To Adjust The Interruption 
Temperature Thresholds For Tier 1 And Tier 2 Customers 
 
NYC proposes that the Companies’ tariffs specify that Tier 1 customers will be interrupted 
at 15 degrees Fahrenheit and Tier 2 customers at 20 degrees Fahrenheit. Tr. 5539, lines 10-12.  The 
Companies disagree.  The Companies’ initial proposal to set interruption temperatures at 15 and 
20 degrees for Tier 1 and 2 customers, respectively, was intended to be a starting point, not a fixed 
temperature.  The key feature is the difference in the interruption temperature between the two 
tiers, not the specific temperatures at which interruptions will occur.  The Companies’ tariffs 
purposefully leave out the specific temperatures because they may change.  Every year, prior to 
the start of the winter season, the Companies’ gas system planning department determines the 
temperature at which interruptions must occur for non-firm customers based on the capacity 
requirements for that winter season.  Tr. 2835-36, lines 18-21 and lines 1-3; Tr. 2864, lines 3-7.  
This may mean that the 15- and 20-degree cuts off will fluctuate year over year.  For example, for 
the 2019/2020 winter season, the Companies set the designated interruption temperature at 16 
degrees Fahrenheit for Tier 1 customers.  Tr. 2863, lines 13-16.  Exhibit 639.  The difference in 
the rates for Tier 1 and 2 is based on the difference in discounts from the otherwise applicable firm 
rate, and is not tied to any specific temperatures.  Tr. 2907-08, lines 21-25 and lines 2-5.  Thus, 
the Companies maintain that their tariffs must retain flexibility to adjust the designated interruption 
temperature to allow proper system planning based on changing conditions year over year.

1. 
Calculation of the Make Whole Provision From April 1, 2020 Until Rates

Pursuant to the February 10 Make Whole Order, the Commission held the Companies

decoupling mechanisms.174  Financial true-up targets established in the Commission’s order, as 
well as depreciation and amortization expense, will be applied to the extension of the suspension

H. 
Lost And Unaccounted For Gas (“LAUF”) 
1. 
The Commission Should Adopt The Companies’ Proposed Modifications 
And Updates To Their Respective LAUF Mechanism 
 
The Companies proposed four modifications and/or updates to their existing recovery 
mechanism for lost and unaccounted for (“LAUF”) gas.  Specifically, KEDNY and KEDLI each 
proposed to (i) update the LAUF targets and associated deadbands; (ii) align the implementation 
of the new targets with the start of the next annual cycle for calculating the Companies’ Gas 
Adjustment Clause (“GAC”), which begins September 1, 2020; (iii) remove the adjustment to the 
LAUF mechanism for inactive accounts; and (iv) modify the calculation to gross up gas costs for 
LAUF gas.  Tr. 2841, lines 16-20.  
Staff disagrees with several aspects of the Companies’ proposals related to the LAUF 
mechanism.  Specifically, Staff disagrees with KEDLI’s proposed LAUF target.  Tr. 2973, line 24.  


--- Page 205 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
200 of 310 
Staff also disagreed with the Companies’ proposal to remove inactive account adjustments from 
the LAUF calculation. Tr. 2977, lines 17-19.   
Staff’s proposed calculation for KEDLI’s LAUF target excludes the actual LAUF from 
2014.  Tr. 2974, lines 5-15.  In accordance with the Staff White Paper on LAUF Gas,176 LAUF 
calculations are based on an average of the most recent five year datapoints.  Tr. 2756, lines 5-7.  
Thus, the Companies’ proposal for the new LAUF targets were calculated using actual LAUF 
performance from 2014 through 2018.  Id.  Staff argues that 2014 should be excluded because it 
believes the 2014 value is an outlier that deviates from the LAUF values in other years.  Tr. 2974, 
lines 5-15. 
Staff’s proposal to exclude a datapoint for being an “outlier” is an arbitrary measure to 
lower the Companies’ target.  Staff’s White Paper on LAUF Gas, which guides the Companies’ 
LAUF calculations, acknowledges that actual LAUF can vary from year to year, which is why a 
five-year average is recommended to establish a utility’s LAUF target.  Tr. 2842, lines 16-19.  A 
five-year average will therefore “smooth out” the highs and lows that may occur over the period 
and serve as a reasonable basis for the Companies to set their future targets.  There is no standard 
to determine what constitutes an outlier, and Staff has not proffered one.  Further, Staff did not 
indicate whether they would also agree to exclude values that are too low as outliers.  In short, 
there is no measurable way to determine the point at which a specific datapoint skews the five-
year average enough to justify excluding it from the Companies’ LAUF target calculation.  
Therefore, the Commission should deny Staff’s proposal to exclude the 2014 data and adopt 
KEDLI’s proposed target, which abides by the guidelines set forth in the Staff White Paper on 
LAUF Gas. 
                     
176 Exhibit 499. 


--- Page 206 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
201 of 310

3. 
The Commission Should Reject Staff’s Proposal For a Service 
Classification Study 
Staff proposes, among other things, that the Companies perform a study to ensure that 
customers are in the correct service classification.  Tr. 2988, lines 6-7.  The Commission should 
reject this proposal.  The Companies make every effort to place customers on the correct service 
classification at the time of application based on the information provided.  Tr. 2847, 11-21.  The 
Commission has consistently held that the utility is not responsible for monitoring customer 
accounts to ensure they are on the lowest rate at all times.  Instead, the customer is responsible for 
requesting the utility to review their account for potential reclassification.  A study that reviews 
every account would impose an undue burden on the Companies with respect to both time and 


--- Page 208 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
203 of 310 
money for an effort that may not produce a significant impact.   While a study may be appropriate 
in a multi-year settlement, it is wholly inappropriate in a one-year litigated case, especially where 
a July order establishing rates would mean the Companies would have less than five months to 
complete such a study.  Therefore, the Commission should deny Staff’s proposal. 
4. 
The Commission Should Accept The Companies’ Proposal On Non-
Labor O&M Cost Recovery 
Staff also proposes that non-labor O&M costs for demand response programs be recovered 
through the DRA mechanism, whereas the Companies recommend recovering such costs in base 
rates.  Tr. 694-95, lines 21-24 and lines 1-12; Tr. 2848, lines 5-6.  Requiring recovery of non-labor 
O&M costs through the DRA would create a lag in the recovery, which would add to the adverse 
cash flow impacts affecting the Companies’ credit metrics.  Therefore, the Commission should 
accept the Companies’ proposal to include forecast non-labor O&M costs in base rates. 
5. 
The Commission Should Adopt the Companies’ Proposed Paperless 
Billing Credit 
The Companies propose to update the current paperless billing credits, from $0.35 to $0.41 
per service period for KEDLI and from $0.49 to $0.44 per service period for KEDNY.  Tr. 2849-

waiting until KEDNY has a full year of actual billing data to determine the updated credit amount 
and that KEDNY provide revised calculations in Briefs on Exceptions.  Tr. 2952, lines 16-23.  The 
Companies submit that the cost estimates supporting the paperless billing credit included in the 
initial filing, adjusted for inflation, is a reasonable basis for its proposed paperless billing credit.  
Exhibit 134 (RDP-8, Schedule 2) for KEDNY and Exhibit 150 (RDP-8, Schedule 2) for KEDLI.  
Submitting a revised paperless bill credit calculation in Briefs on Exceptions could create a 
disconnect between the paper bill costs included in the Companies’ revenue requirement and the

costs included in the paperless bill credit calculation.  Therefore, the Commission should accept

IX. 
GAS POLICY AND ENERGY EFFICIENCY 
A. 
Energy Efficiency 
 
1. Downward-only reconciliation 
 
Staff recommends that the Companies’ energy efficiency programs be subject to a 
downward-only reconciliation at the end of the Rate Year.  Tr. 672, lines 6-8.  The energy 
efficiency programs proposed by the Companies in this case and the budget levels authorized by 
the NE:NY Order are intended to help meet very ambitious energy efficiency targets over a period 
of several years.  Sustained funding levels year-on-year are needed to provide the continuity 


--- Page 213 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
208 of 310 
needed to meet these long-term targets.  Therefore, the Companies believe that established funding 
levels should be retained for application towards these energy efficiency initiatives through 2025, 
regardless of the length of the rate period approved in this case.  Tr. 161-62, lines 20-21 and 1-5.   
2. 
Updating EE Budgets and Targets 
In the Supplemental Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel, the Companies incorporated 
the updated energy efficiency budgets and targets established in the NE:NY Order for the Rate 
Year.  Tr. 228, lines 16-19.  Similarly, the Companies adjusted their respective revenue 
requirements to move EM&V costs from base rates and into the energy efficiency program 
portfolio budgets as directed by the Commission in the NE:NY Order.  Tr. 229, lines 7-10.  
Therefore, there is no remaining dispute between Staff and the Companies on these issues.    
3. 
Reporting Requirements 
 
NYC recommends increased, detailed energy efficiency reporting.  Tr. 5311-12, lines 7-21 
and 1-11.  This recommendation should be rejected.  The Companies already are subject to 
comprehensive reporting requirements for their energy efficiency programs, many of which have 
been established by Staff or the Commission and are set forth in Commission orders or guidance 
documents the Companies follow.  These reporting requirements apply to all the State’s utilities 
and establish a common framework for assessing performance on energy efficiency programs and 
initiatives throughout the State.178  The Companies are open to collaborating with NYC to 
determine how to improve information sharing and communications consistent with applicable 
guidelines.  However, there is no basis for the Commission to impose new or additional energy

4. 
Use of Unspent Energy Efficiency Funds 
Staff proposes that unspent energy efficiency funds be amortized over five years to offset 
energy efficiency collections.  Tr. 675, lines 10-14. The Companies are not opposed to the principle 
reflected in Staff’s recommendation to mitigate energy efficiency collections using previously 
collected unspent or uncommitted energy efficiency funds.  The general expectation the 
Commission expressed in the NE:NY Order was that: “all available uncommitted and unspent 
utility energy efficiency funds will be used to mitigate the impacts of the portfolio budgets 
authorized here.”179  However, the Commission did not impose or identify a particular manner of 
application or amortization period for such funds, and there is no basis for the five year 
amortization proposed by Staff.

B. 
Green Gas Tariff  
The Companies proposed to implement a Green Gas Tariff (“GGT”) offering that would 
enable customers to voluntarily purchase renewable natural gas (“RNG”) and support the 
development of the RNG market. Tr. 56, lines 6-12.  The Companies initially planned to make the 
GGT available beginning in Data Year 1; however, given the context of a one-year rate case, and 
the fact that the Companies will likely be looking to file another rate case (seeking a multi-year 
plan) soon, the Companies have decided to withdraw the GGT proposal.  Given the costs to 
implement the GGT, the compressed period implementation timeframe, resource and workplace 
constraints due to the COVID-19 restrictions, among other factors, the Companies believe 
withdrawing the GGT proposal from this case is appropriate.  Accordingly, the Companies will 
take the necessary steps to withdraw the GGT proposal from this case.   
C. 
Utility Energy Services Contracts Program: Share Of Net Revenue, Full-Time 
Equivalent Employees, Inclusion In EE Portfolio, EE targets And Budgets 
 
The Utility Energy Services Contract (“UESC”) program is an energy services contracting 
program where the Companies and customers, typically government agencies and other large 
commercial entities, enter limited-source contracts for energy management services, such as 
energy and water improvements.  Tr. 59, lines 18-21.  With a UESC, the Companies help 


--- Page 216 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
211 of 310 
customers analyze their current energy usage, identify opportunities for improvement, and work 
with pre-selected contractors to achieve efficiencies, reduce costs, and adopt renewable energy 
systems, provided the efficiencies include some measure of gas savings. The customers, in turn, 
pay for the contracted services for the improvements delivered under the UESC.  Tr. 60, lines 1-
6.  Although Staff supports a platform service revenue (“PSR”) mechanism for the UESC program, 
it recommends a lower PSR percentage than the 33 percent proposed by the Companies, and also 
proposes to change how the PSR is calculated.  Tr. 598-600.  Staff also recommends eliminating 
the two incremental FTEs supported in the Companies’ case to support the UESC program.  The 
Company addresses Staff’s PSR recommendation in Section IX.L, below.   
Regarding Staff’s recommendation to eliminate the incremental FTEs, the Companies note 
that the Commission supports utilities identifying opportunities to generate PSRs through the 
provision of value-added services to customers and stakeholders.180  The UESC program presents 
an opportunity for the Companies to implement this Commission directive. To have a reasonable 
chance to succeed, however, the program requires a dedicated set of resources. Requiring that the 
Companies redeploy resources from their energy efficiency programs—which also are charged 
increased and accelerated penetration—would result in inadequate resources dedicated to both 
these important areas. 
The Companies also disagree that the UESC program should be incorporated into the 
energy efficiency portfolios or that specific budgets and targets should be established.  The UESC 
program is designed as an energy services contracting program rather than a direct customer 
incentive program. Energy savings delivered through UESC programs are tracked and measured

through the existing utility energy efficiency programs as customers participating in the UESC

F. 
Geothermal 
1. 
Proposed Expansion Of Demonstration Project 
 
The Companies propose to expand the Geothermal Gas REV Demonstration Project 
(“Riverhead Demonstration”) authorized in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.  The 
Riverhead Demonstration has allowed KEDLI to provide non-traditional heating and cooling 
services to participating customers since November 2017, and demonstrate that the ground source 
heat pump (“GSHP”) technology works and can deliver customer satisfaction and savings.  Tr. 77, 
lines 1-12.  “[H]eat pumps create meaningful improvements in overall efficiency and reduce 
carbon emissions,”181 and increased GSHP deployment is important to helping achieve the State’s 
carbon reduction goals.   
 
The proposed expansion of the Riverhead Demonstration would test whether utility 
ownership of underground loops, the most expensive part of the system, can accelerate greater 
market adoption.  The Companies would work in partnership with geothermal installers and other 
stakeholders to animate the geothermal market and develop learnings, including how best to install 
and operate GSHP systems in areas that may have significant natural gas infrastructure.  The 
program would look to test the utility loop ownership model primarily in supply-constrained areas 
and would prioritize low-to-moderate income customers, as well as commercial and industrial 
customers for whom the underground loop portion of GSHP systems presents a particularly high-
cost barrier to entry.  Tr. 79-20, lines 6-21 and 1-6.  An expanded program that increases the 
                     
181 NE:NY Order, page 53.   


--- Page 220 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
215 of 310 
number of GSHP installations will provide customer benefits by reducing peak demand, energy 
intensity, and carbon emissions while increasing energy efficiency and distributed energy 
penetration.    
 
Staff’s opposition to the expanded demonstration project is not shared by other parties in 
this case.  Jared Rodriguez for Estates NY Real Estate Services LLC notes that “[g]eothermal

technology, particularly shared geothermal loop energy systems, will likely play a key role in

of the Companies’ involvement in geothermal technologies to “[p]ump heat, not gas.”  Tr. 5721,

affect the GSHP market (Tr. 698, lines 6-10), is contrary to the position of at least one major New 
York geothermal trade organization, whose members include the very types of entities Staff 
believes could be adversely affected.  Tr. 174, lines 11-18.182  
 
Staff states that legal or statutory issues also may affect the ability of the Companies to 
provide geothermal market services.  Tr. 719, lines 6-14.  However, The Companies’ proposal in 
this case is for an expanded demonstration project, not a full ownership program.  To meet New 
York’s GHG emissions reduction goals, it is critical for the GSHP market to grow significantly.

G. 
Power to Gas Demonstration Project (KEDLI) 
KEDLI’s Power to Gas (“P2G”) demonstration project proposal is intended to support 
exploration of the potential for using renewable electricity to produce hydrogen or pipeline quality 
RNG that can be delivered with the existing gas network.  Tr. 108, lines 18-21.  Staff opposes the 
proposed P2G project on the basis that its proposed use of renewable electricity is not among “the 
envisioned solutions and goals for excess renewable electricity” set for in the Commission’s 
December 13, 2018 Storage Order.185  Tr. 704, lines 12-21; Tr. 739, lines 12-15.   
Staff’s basis for rejecting the proposal is contrary to the spirit of innovation necessary for 
the state to achieve its emissions reductions objectives and contrary to the intent of the Storage 
Order.  To achieve the State’s bold clean energy targets, the Storage Order does not preclude 
technology types, and neither does PSL.  PSL Section 74, which compels the Commission to 
develop targets and policies, expressly identifies a range of storage types for various purposes, 
including the use of chemical reactions to store energy in usable forms by defining a “qualified 
energy storage system” as one which is “commercially available technology that is capable of 
absorbing energy, storing it for a period of time, and thereafter dispatching the energy using 
mechanical, chemical, or thermal processes to store energy that was generated at one time for use 
at a later time.”186  Further, as the Commission has articulated, the Energy Storage Roadmap “is 
technology-neutral and acknowledges that a range of energy storage solutions will be deployed to 
best meet customer and system needs.”187    
                     
185 Case 18-E-0130, In the Matter of Energy Storage Deployment Program, Order Establishing Energy 
Storage Goal and Deployment Policy (issued and effective Dec. 13, 2018) (“Storage Order”). 
186 PSL §74 (emphasis added). 
187 Storage Order, page 5.   


--- Page 223 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
218 of 310 
 
Staff’s opposition to the P2G project on the basis that New York’s existing electric 
generation mix does not have excess renewable electricity also is shortsighted.  The State is 
committed to developing significant off-shore wind capacity (Tr. 107, lines 2-4; Tr. 110, lines 7-
10), and the CLCPA mandates the move to 70 percent renewable electricity by 2030 and 100 
percent emission free electricity by 2040.188  Other parts of the country with significant installed 
renewable electricity capacity already experience curtailment occurrences that New York may see 
in the foreseeable future if the State’s renewable electricity ambitions are realized.  Tr. 265-66, 
lines 9-24 and 2-11.  However, rather than consider the P2G project on the basis that it was 
proposed—i.e., to evaluate the potential for converting excess renewable electricity to renewable 
natural gas or hydrogen—Staff considered the merits of the P2G project as if it were proposed to 
explore the merits of using bulk electric system power to produce RNG.  Tr. 744-46.  This was not 
the basis for KEDLI’s proposal and opposition to the project on that basis is simply misplaced.  If 
commercialized at scale, P2G has the potential to significantly decarbonize the gas network.  This 
project will provide insight into how to integrate such resources into the gas network and 
streamline the interconnection process for this new gas source.  Staff’s opposition to the P2G 
project should be rejected.

I. 
One-Year Deferral of RNG Interconnection Program, Full-Time Equivalent 
Employees for RNG Interconnection Program 
 
To encourage increased renewable energy development, the Companies proposed an RNG 
interconnection program whereby they would engineer, install and own certain portions of RNG 
interconnection equipment (e.g., meters, odorization equipment, and spectrometers) at project 
sites.  Tr. 114, lines 9-20; Tr. 4282, lines 10-16.  The Companies also propose adding five 
incremental FTEs to comprise a new Future of Heat engineering group.  Tr. 115, lines 12-19, Tr. 
4344, lines 6-10.  This new group would focus on, among other things, shortening the time needed 
to review and approve RNG interconnection projects, as well as other aspects of RNG projects, 
hydrogen blending, and non-pipeline solutions.  Tr. 115, lines 1-9; Tr. 116, lines 3-12.   
 
Staff supports the RNG interconnection program, but opposes the proposed KEDNY 
capital expenditures related to the program, opposes the proposed O&M for the KEDNY projects 
under the program, and opposes adding incremental FTEs to implement it.  Tr. 703, lines 2-11.  
The Companies appreciate Staff’s conceptual support for the proposed program; however, Staff’s 
opposition to funding the incremental labor needed to run the program, as well as removing 


--- Page 225 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
220 of 310 
KEDNY’s associated Rate Year operating and capital expenditures under the program, amounts 
to no actual support at all, and effectively precludes implementation of the program.  
 
The Companies’ RNG interconnection queue has grown considerably in recent years and 
additional resources are needed to support and accelerate development of this local and renewable 
gas supply.  Tr. 189, lines 18-21, Tr. 4283, lines 1-7.  The Companies’ proposal would mitigate 
two significant hurdles to RNG interconnection: the long time it takes for a developer to get 
interconnection approval, and the high capital cost of the interconnection.   
 
Staff’s recommendation that the Companies rely on existing labor resources to implement 
the program also is not feasible. The Companies currently rely on existing gas engineering 
personnel to support RNG interconnection proposals. These existing FTEs are dedicated to 
supporting the Companies’ traditional gas engineering activities and RNG interconnections 
incremental to their existing workload.  Tr. 190, lines 9-14.  As a result, interconnection processing 
has been delayed.  Because RNG developers rely on receiving timely interconnection approval to 
solidify project financing, such delays inhibit RNG development and the corresponding 
environmental and emissions benefits associated with such RNG projects.  Thus, the incremental 
FTEs proposed for the FOH Engineering Group are needed even if implementation of the RNG 
interconnection program is deferred one year as Staff proposes.

K. 
Earnings Adjustment Mechanisms: Metrics and targets, total available basis 
points 
 
The Companies proposed a series of Earnings Adjustment Mechanisms (“EAM”) designed 
to support the Commission’s REV objectives and the State’s clean energy goals.  The EAMs were 
focused on areas: System Efficiency, Energy Efficiency, and Carbon Reduction.  Tr. 120, lines 4-
9.  To align and incentivize the Companies to meet the EAM targets, maximum earnings levels 
were set at 78 basis points for KEDNY and 77 basis points for KEDLI across the three EAM areas, 
with each of the three EAMs comprised of two metrics (i.e., System Efficiency: (a) Peak 
Reduction, and (b) Supply Diversification; Energy Efficiency: (a) Incremental Energy Efficiency, 
and (b) Affordability; and Carbon Reduction: (a) CO2e Reduction – Full service, and (b) CO2e 
Reduction – Transportation).  Tr. 121-22, lines 6-14 and 1-2.   
 
Staff makes four overall recommendations regarding the Companies’ EAM proposals:  
(1) reject KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s proposals to earn up to 78 and 77 basis points, 
respectively, in the Rate Year, and replace those with a program allowing each 
company to earn up to 10 basis points maximum, plus earnings from a Share the 
Savings energy efficiency metric;  
(2) impose more aggressive performance measures on the Companies; 


--- Page 227 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
222 of 310 
(3) express eligible EAM amounts in terms of absolute dollars rather than as basis 
points; and 
(4) establish EAM metrics, targets and financial incentive levels for the full term of the 
rate plan if a multi-year rate plan is adopted.   
Tr. 553-57.  The Companies generally agree with Staff’s recommendations 3-4, above, but 
disagree with Staff’s proposal to substantially modify or eliminate the Companies’ proposed 
EAMs and drastically reduce the potential incentive levels.   
 
As discussed below, the Companies agree with Staff that the proposed target under the 
Affordability metric should be increased.  The Companies also note that the energy efficiency 
EAM targets must be increased to correspond to the increased targets established in the January 
16, 2020 NE:NY Order.  Tr. 232-33, lines 18-20 and 1-2.  However, the Companies disagree with 
Staff’s recommendations that the metrics be significantly altered from what the Companies 
proposed.    
 
The Commission has previously said that incentive mechanisms should provide utilities 
with stretch targets that are reasonably and realistically achievable, and with financially 
meaningful incentives.189  Thus, the Commission selected 100 basis points as the reasonable and 
appropriate ceiling for EAMs.190  The potentially achievable maximum incentive levels proposed 
by Staff are only one-quarter this level and less that one-third what the Companies proposed.  That 
level of incentive would not serve as the mechanism the Commission envisioned. 
                     
189 Track Two Order, page 68 (“Incentive opportunities should be financially meaningful and structured 
such that they encourage enterprise-wide attention as the utility and encourage strategic, portfolio-level 
approaches beyond narrow programs”).   
190 Id. (“As initial bounds on the first round of REV initiated EAMs, the maximum amount of earnings

Further, although there is merit to a system-level peak reduction metric as Staff proposes, 
the Companies question whether the capabilities are in place to effectively implement and 
administer such a metric by the Rate Year.  Current capabilities are insufficient to adequately 
measure peak reduction on a system-level and there are limitations on the ability to adequately 
measure peak reduction at a system-level or isolate it from other large macro-economic variables.  
Tr. 195-96, lines 19-21 and 1-7.  The peak-reduction metric proposed by the Companies’ would 
push KEDNY and KEDLI to continue to drive peak reduction, and should not be dismissed in 
favor of a metric which at this point cannot be effectively implemented.    
Staff also opposes the Companies’ proposed Supply Diversification metric and contends 
that EAMs for fuel and resource diversity should be considered in the context of diversifying the 
electric grid away from reliance on gas, rather than identifying diverse fuel sources for a gas 
utility’s supply portfolio.  Tr. 567, lines 13-24.  The Companies believe Staff’s view is too 
constrained.  A major goal of EAMs is to align utility shareholders’ financial interests with 
customers’ interests.  The Supply Diversification EAM would incentivize the Companies to 
prioritize RNG interconnection over the interconnection of traditional natural gas supply 
infrastructure or non-traditional natural gas supply infrastructure. Tr. 197, lines 14-21.  The 
Companies currently generate no profit from interconnecting third-party RNG projects, and they 
should be incentivized to prioritize RNG interconnections due their unique benefits, including 
emissions reductions and improved local waste management.  
 
The Companies disagree with Staff’s recommendation that the Affordability metric be 
rejected.  The outcome-oriented design of the Affordability metric means that it encompasses not 
only programmatic efforts of the utility to directly influence low-income customer energy usage, 
but also that it reflects utility partnerships and platforms meant to encourage markets to reduce

19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
224 of 310 
low-income customer bills.  Tr. 201, lines 1-10.  Affordability of energy bills is a top priority for

contends that the Track Two Order limits GHG reduction EAMs to decarbonization of end-uses 
(Tr. 591, lines 17-21), and therefore there is no room for any EAM that would achieve GHG 
reductions from decarbonizing inputs.  Such a construct is the epitome of form-before-substance, 
and is contrary to the spirit of the Track Two Order and EAMs in general, which is for utilities to 
innovate and design incentives that align the interests of customers and the utilities.191  Reducing 
GHG emissions is a clear goal identified in the Track Two Order and enshrined in state policy and 
legislation.   The Companies agree with Staff that decarbonization of end uses (such as heat pumps 
                     
191 See, e.g., Track Two Order, page 32 (“Staff accurately described how cost-of-service ratemaking

or electric vehicles) can be more readily implemented, tracked, and measured, some end uses are

L. 
Platform Service Revenues: Availability for E-Commerce Platform; Share of 
Revenues Related to UESC Program 
 
The Companies propose two platform service revenue (“PSR”) opportunities: (i) the E-
Commerce Platform; and (ii) the UESC program.  For the E-Commerce PSR, the Companies 
propose to retain 20 percent of the fees collected from vendors that participate in the E-Commerce 
Platform, and apply the remaining 80 percent for the benefit of customers.  Regarding the UESC 
PSR, the Companies propose to retain one-third of the fees received from UESC participants, and 
apply the remaining two-thirds for the benefit of customers. 
 
Staff opposes the E-Commerce PSR, claiming that the Companies did not satisfy the 
criteria in the Track Two Order to justify a PSR.  Tr. 596-97, lines 22-24 and 1-5.  The Companies 
disagree.  In discussing the criteria for whether a PSR should be approved, the Commission 
specifically noted that the “criteria are intended to support balanced judgment, as opposed to a 
set of binary determinations that must all be made in the affirmative.”192  The Commission also 
specifically recognized customer origination services as the type of service that would be suitable 
for a PSR, explicitly stating that “utilities may be best situated to provide origination in a cost-
                     
192Track Two Order, p. 49, n. 57.

effective manner that facilitates market penetration, based on analysis of customer data and/or 
connecting interested customers with DER providers via a marketplace portal.”193 The 
Commission also has approved E-Commerce PSR mechanisms for other utilities.194  There is no 
reason for finding the Companies did not satisfy the Commission’s PSR criteria, and no rational 
basis for denying a PSR mechanism for the Companies’ proposed E-Commerce platform.

vehicle needs, the Companies cannot agree with the City’s proposal.

X. 
 GAS SAFETY 
The natural gas industry has increased its focus on gas pipeline safety over the last decade.  
Tr. 4126.  This has resulted in a proliferation of regulations at both the federal and state level 
around gas safety.  Id.  For their part, the Companies have worked diligently to develop a culture 
where safety and compliance are paramount.  Tr. 3779-80, lines 13-21 and 1-12.  The Companies 
have focused on people, processes, systems, and technology to reinforce their commitment to 
safety and compliance.  Id.  As is stated in the Companies’ direct testimony, these commitments 
are reflected in improvements the Companies have made, including: additional field inspections; 
damage prevention enhancements; investments in technology solutions; enhanced training and 


--- Page 235 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
230 of 310 
simplified procedures and practices in the field; and efforts to implement the American Petroleum 
Institute’s recommended comprehensive risk identification and management framework 
(Recommended Practice 1173).197  Id.  The Companies’ efforts have resulted in measurable 
improvements in safety performance, and the Companies are committed to continuing this progress 
through the proposed safety investments and programs reflected in their filings.  Id.  The 
Commission should support these proposals in the interest of promoting gas safety.   
At the same time, the Companies do not believe it is reasonable for the Commission to 
impose in these cases a set of increasingly stringent gas safety performance measures where (i) the 
Commission lacks clear legal authority to do so in a litigated case, (ii) the work needed to achieve 
the Staff Pipeline Safety Panel’s (“SPSP”) proposed targets is not being funded in rates, (iii) the 
Companies would be subject to tens of millions of dollars of potential penalties without the benefit 
of a comprehensive rate plan, and (iv) the SPSP is proposing to implement new, more stringent 
targets in the middle of the performance year, by which point the Companies’ ability to achieve 
those targets would be severely compromised, at best.  Consider also that the current COVID-19 
situation will require the Companies’ to focus their full attention on providing safe and reliable 
service under extraordinary circumstances, and to prioritize essential work given work restrictions 
and resource constraints, which will certainly impact the Companies’ ability to meet performance 
targets.  For all these reasons, the SPSP’s proposed gas safety measures are not appropriate.       
A. 
Performance Metrics  
1. 
Performance Metrics In A One-Year Case – Especially One In Which 
Targets Would Be Set Six To Seven Months Into The Calendar Year – 
Are Wholly Inappropriate 
                     
197 API, Pipeline Safety Management Systems Requirements, API Recommended Practice 1173 (issued 
July 2015). 


--- Page 236 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
231 of 310 
The Companies are currently subject to a set of gas safety metrics that were negotiated as 
part of a comprehensive rate settlement in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.  Tr. 4129-
30.  The Companies agreed to be subject to these performance standards – and millions of dollars 
of associated performance penalties – as part of a rate settlement that provided for an agreed level 
of capital investments and O&M spending, in addition to resolving other contentious issues among 
the parties.  Indeed, as part of the comprehensive settlement, the Companies were able to recover 
an agreed level of costs to achieve the performance targets, including a rate allowance that reflected 
the costs of replacing leak prone main, responding to gas emergencies, repairing leaks, and 
mitigating instances of third-party damage.  The Companies were able to gauge the reasonableness 
of the performance targets (and associated penalties) in light of the rate allowance – and assume a 
level of risk based on the overall value of the settlement.   
In this case, the SPSP again proposes a set of gas safety performance measures with the 
potential for 150 basis points of penalties (also referred to as negative revenues adjustments) in a 
one-year litigated case.  Tr. 4038, lines 12-21.  In round numbers, 150 basis points equates to 
approximately $49.5 million of performance penalties at KEDNY and $32 million at KEDLI 
(based on the Companies’ proposed revenue requirements).  Tr. 4129, lines 8-10.  Unlike the 
comprehensive settlement in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, a litigated, one-year case 
does not enable the Companies to agree on a reasonable set of performance targets or, importantly, 
ensure an adequate level of capital and O&M funding to achieve those performance targets.   In 
fact, the SPSP testified, in setting more stringent performance targets, it was “focused on the 
compliance requirements based on regulations and established performance metrics in the past,” 
not the costs necessary to achieve the performance targets: 


--- Page 237 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
232 of 310 
Q.  
So just to be clear, is – is that a no that you didn’t consider the -- the

A.

The SPSP also concedes that it is not aware of an occasion where the Commission ordered 
gas safety performance measures with associated negative or positive revenue adjustments outside 
of a rate settlement.  Tr. 4136-37, lines 22-25 and 2-4.  There are many good reasons why the 
Commission has not set gas safety performance measures in a litigated case, including that it lacks 
the legal authority to do so.       
a. Gas Safety Metrics And Negative Revenue Adjustments Are 
Unlawful Penalties 
 
The proposed gas safety measures lack support under the PSL and the Commission’s 
regulations.  Indeed, nothing in the PSL or the regulations permits the Commission to establish 
gas safety performance targets in a rate proceeding.  Thus, imposing performance metrics with 
significant penalties is unlawful because the Commission lacks the authority to impose these 
penalties.   
None of the safety metrics the SPSP seeks to impose are contained anywhere in the 
Commission’s regulations.  Moreover, given the fact the Companies’ have consistently met or 
exceeded the performance standards in the areas of the proposed metrics – leak prone pipe, leak 
repair, et cetera – no party can be heard to argue the Companies are failing to meet their statutory 
obligation to provide “safe and adequate” service.198    
b. Performance Measures In A One-Year Case  
                     
198 The PSL provides that every gas corporation “shall furnish and provide such service, instrumentalities 
and facilities as shall be safe and adequate.” PSL § 65(1). 


--- Page 238 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
233 of 310 
Even if the Commission could order gas safety metric with performance penalties, the 
Companies believe that gas safety metrics are inequitable and unnecessary in a one-year, litigated 
case for several reasons.  The gas safety performance metrics are designed to drive performance 
improvements over time.  To that end, the Companies’ proposals for performance metrics 
contemplate a multi-year rate plan, which would allow for investments in safety programs and 
work practices to drive improvements over time.  The Companies do not agree with the inclusion 
of performance metrics in a one-year litigated case.  Indeed, the Companies submit that their 
consistently high level of performance, coupled with the robust reporting mechanism and the 
Commission’s oversight and enforcement powers, should provide the Commission with sufficient 
comfort that service will not be diminished in a one-year rate plan.  In the recent National Fuel 
rate order, the Commission specifically declined to impose gas safety or customer service 
performance metrics in a one-year litigated case.199 
c. Companies’ Safety Performance

and KEDLI’s performance, as reported by Staff’s reports through 2017 include: (i) an overall 
reduction in the damages due to excavator error since 2015; (ii) a reduction in emergency response 
times to gas leak and odor calls within 30 minutes every year since 2015; and (iii) a reduction in 
the backlog of total leaks every year since 2013.  Tr. 3711, lines 2-7.   
With regard to the gas safety regulations performance metric, the Companies have 
demonstrated continuous improvement through a steady decline of audit violations over the past 
several years.  Tr. 3711-12.  Indeed, the 2017 field audit and records audit results set forth in

e. Setting New Targets Mid-Year  
In New York, gas safety performance is measured on a calendar year basis – and the SPSP 
is proposing that its performance measures would apply in 2020.  Tr. 4142, lines 12-22.  However, 
as acknowledged by the SPSP, if the Commission were to adopt the performance targets applicable 
to the Rate Year, the targets would be finalized when the Commission issues a final order in this 
proceeding (Tr. 4144, lines 3-8), which could be six or seven months into the performance period.  
This means the Companies would already need to be managing their business to achieve these 
more stringent performance targets without knowing the outcome of this proceeding and, 
importantly, the rate allowance for the work needed to achieve these results.  As a general matter, 
the SPSP concedes that the Companies need to plan – either through capital investments and their 
operating workplans – to achieve more stringent performance targets in the Pipeline Safety 
Performance Measures.  Tr. 4143-44, lines 17-24 and 1-2.   This type of planning is not possible 
under the SPSP’s proposal.   
When challenged on the obvious inequity of setting performance targets six months or 
more into the performance period, the SPSP takes the presumptuous position that the Companies 
should have begun working against the its proposed targets when it saw the SPSP’s testimony last 
fall: 
Q.  
So is it the panel’s position that the companies should start –- should 
be planning to meet your proposed performance measures before 
they are finalized in an order, if they are finalized in an order?

Knowing what was proposed in our testimony, yes.  (Tr. 4145, lines

The SPSP’s position on this issue is clearly unreasonable.  The SPSP cannot argue that the 
Companies should be subject to a retroactive performance measures, with tens of millions of 
dollars at risk, that are implemented six months or more into the performance period – especially 
where the Companies could not know, until rates are set in a final order, the levels of funding they 
will have to achieve the performance targets.  This would be unreasonable on a blue-sky day, let 
alone under the current circumstances where the Companies’ operational resources are focused on 
maintaining safe and reliable service in the midst of a pandemic. 
2. 
Leak Prone Pipe Retirement Metric And Prioritization (FEMA 
Floodplains) 
The SPSP supports KEDNY’s proposal to continue its five-mile per year acceleration of 
LPP retirement and KEDLI’s proposal to maintain its CY 2019 LPP retirement level of 155 miles 
per year.  Tr. 4048, line 8-13.  However, as discussed above, the Companies do not support 
performance measures in a one-year case and, therefore, do not believe it is appropriate to include 
this metric in the Rate Year.  There are several reasons why an LPP metric is inequitable in a one-
year case.   
First, the Companies will not know the target mileage until, at best, the middle of the 
calendar year.  At the same time, the Companies will not know the rate allowance for its proactive 
main replacement program.  Without knowing whether the Companies will be adequately funded 
to perform this incremental work, the Companies cannot execute a workplan that will enable it to 
achieve the mileage – especially the additional five miles for KEDNY.     
Second, as discussed above, the SGIOP is proposing significant cuts to the Companies 
proactive main replacement programs – approximately $6 million for KEDNY and $10 million for 
KEDLI.  Tr. 4535, lines 11-17.  Significantly reducing the funding for the Companies’ main 
replacement programs, while holding them to aggressive performance targets with multi-million 


--- Page 242 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
237 of 310 
dollars penalties, virtually assures the Companies will incur financial harm.  Unlike the current 
multi-year rate plan, where the parties agreed on a level of capital funding for the main replacement 
program together with the LPP performance targets as part of a comprehensive settlement, the 
SGIOP and SPSP have separated the funding for these programs from the performance targets.  
The results are unquestionably unreasonable.   
Finally, recognizing the Companies’ ability to achieve a performance target in a given year 
could be impacted by any number of issues (e.g., storms, cold weather), the current LPP metric 
provides for cumulative targets over the term of the multi-year rate plan.  The current COVID-19 
pandemic events shine a bright light on why these types of provisions are necessary to provide 
flexibility in prioritizing work and enabling efficient use of resources.  Because a cumulative target 
is not an option in a one-year case, this places even more risk on the Companies’ ability to achieve 
these extremely challenging targets in the Rate Year. 
a.  NYC’s LPP Floodplain Proposal  
NYC recommends that the Companies be required to replace all LPP located in FEMA 500 
and 100-year floodplains within 10 years.  Tr. 5434, lines 6-19.  While NYC expresses opposition 
to incentives for all other gas safety metrics, including incentives for the general retirement of 
additional LPP, NYC proposes an incentive to encourage accelerated LPP retirement in 
floodplains. Tr. 4535, lines 1-13.  The Companies have already made a modification to their main 
prioritization algorithm, procedure ENG04030 “Identification, Evaluation and Prioritization of 
Distribution Main Segments for Replacement” that assigns a higher weight to LPP mains in 
floodplains.  Tr. 3796-97, lines 18-21 and 1-9.  This will help replace the LPP mains in floodplains 
earlier but will not guarantee completion of these replacements in ten years.  Tr. 3797, lines 1-3.  
In addition, the Companies have identified several pockets of low-pressure LPP in floodplains that 
the Companies are already planning to replace over the next five years.  Id., lines 3-5.  The 


--- Page 243 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
238 of 310 
Companies also note that all of the LPP and low-pressure main has been replaced west of Bay 
32nd Street in the Rockaways, which was an area hit particularly hard during Superstorm Sandy.  
Id., lines 5-8.  Therefore, the Companies maintain that increased acceleration to replace LPP in 
floodplains is unnecessary.  In short, the Companies believe that their existing risk-based 
prioritization model is the best approach for meeting the goal of retiring all LPP within floodplains.  
Additionally, the Companies would lose efficiencies of coordinating LPP replacements with 
municipal projects and other company work across the Companies’ territory if they were required 
to target floodplain work over other work, which could increase costs.   
b.  Positive Revenue Adjustment (“PRA”) For LPP 
The SPSP recommends enhancing the NRAs and removing the PRAs for LPP removal.  
Tr. 4048-49, lines 14-24 and 1-2.  The Staff Policy Panel cites the CLCPA to support its 
recommendation, noting that the incentives provided to natural gas utilities in the areas of gas 
safety and reliability should be “appropriate and produce desired outcomes.”  Tr 3603, lines 6-22.  
The Staff Policy Panel also cites the increased costs in the Companies’ capital programs associated 
with LPP removal in support of its recommendation.  Tr. 3604, lines 8-22.  NYC opposes the LPP 
removal PRA, claiming the Companies have not provided justification for rewarding additional 
main replacement or established “a compelling need” to increase the costs to customers to fund 
the PRA in this area.  Tr. 5462, lines 1-5, 10-13. 
If the Commission sees fit to include a performance metric in this area, which the 
Companies do not support for the reasons discussed above, the LPP metric must maintain the PRA 
component.  The recommendations to remove the LPP removal PRA202 is entirely counterintuitive

2. 
Leak Backlog Reduction Targets And Leak Survey Practices 
Under the terms of the Companies’ current rate settlement, the parties agreed to both 
negative and positive revenue adjustments associated with leak management.  Tr. 4152, lines 13-
18.  There are two components: (i) a “total” leak metric covering all leaks in the Companies’ leak 
backlog (Types 1, 2A, 2, or 3) and (ii) a “workable” or “repairable” leak metric measuring the 
backlog of leaks designated as potentially hazardous under the Commission’s regulations (Types 
1, 2A, and 2).  Id., lines 19-23.  The SPSP recommends the following changes to the Companies’ 
proposed Leak Backlog Metric: 
• Total Leaks: Reduce the baseline leak backlog targets for CY 2020 based on 
averages of leak repairs for the past five years.  Retaining flat target reductions each 
year of 150 leaks for KEDNY and 750 leaks for KEDLI, and remove additional 
target reductions based on repairs of high-emission Type-3 leaks (Tr. 4056, lines 4-
23; 
• Workable leaks: Reduce annual target from 25 to 20 each year (Tr. 4057, lines 2-
4); 
• Year-end target range: Set the year-end target range to December 26th – December 
31st (in lieu of Companies’ proposal of December 21st – December 31st) (Tr. 4058, 
lines 7-10); and 
• Incentives: Ability to earn PRAs is based on total leak repairs, removing existing 
incentive for repairing high-emitter Type-3 leaks. Tr. 4061, lines 9-21. 


--- Page 246 ---

Significantly, the SPSP’s proposal would lower the baseline, or the starting point, for the

15-25 and 2-4.  The SPSP also supports increasing the negative revenue adjustment from a total

If the Commission sees fit to impose a leak reduction metric, the Companies believe the 
Leak Backlog Metrics they have proposed are challenging and strike the correct balance to drive 
reductions of the overall leak backlog while strategically targeting the highest emission Type-3 
leaks to maximize methane emissions reductions from this program.  The Companies respond 
specifically as follows: 
• Baseline targets: The current Leak Backlog metric reduces the targets by 150 and 750 
leaks each year for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively.  It is reasonable, therefore, to 
set CY 2020 targets based on a 150 and 750 reduction in total leaks from 2019.   
• Annual reduction: The Companies support a fixed annual reduction.  The proposal to 
further reduce the targets each year based on high emitter Type-3 leak repairs was 


--- Page 247 ---

19-G-0310 
 
 
242 of 310 
intended to work in conjunction with the Companies’ proposed incentive structure, so 
that as the Companies earned incentives for repairing high-emitter Type-3 leaks, the 
metric would become more challenging the following year. 
• Workable leaks: The existing target of 25 leaks per year is challenging and reasonable. 
• Year-end target range: The SPSP gives no justification for reducing the Companies’ 
proposed 11-day target window to a 6-day target window.  Again, new leaks can arise 
at any time and increase as the weather becomes colder.  The Companies’ leak repair 
performance could be excellent for nearly a year, yet the Companies would be at risk 
of missing their targets if too many leaks arise in the last month of the year without 
sufficient time to make repairs.  
• Incentives: The Companies strongly believe that the Leak Backlog Metric should 
continue to incentivize and provide cost recovery for repair of high-emitter Type-3 
leaks.  High-emission Type-3 leaks are typically harder to access and more expensive 
to repair but can have a more significant impact toward reducing overall methane 
emissions than repair of lower emitting non-hazardous leaks.  The Companies also 
believe that incentives for high-emitter Type-3 leaks as part of the Leak Backlog Metric 
help to address EDF’s concerns about the structure of a backlog reduction metric. 
a. SPSP’s Proposed Re-Check Requirement is Inappropriate  
The SPSP proposes, to successfully eliminate a Type 3 or non-hazardous leak for purposes 
of the total leak metric, the Companies should be required to perform an inspection or a recheck 
of that leak, which would require the Companies to conduct a follow-up inspection between 14 
and 30 days following the repair date to validate said repair.  Tr. 4158, lines 9-15.  As the SPSP 
notes in its testimony, although 16 NYCRR §255.819(a) requires a re-check inspection for Types

1, 2, or 2A leaks, there is no follow-up inspection requirement for Type-3 leaks in the 
Commission’s regulations.  Tr. 4158-59, lines 24-25 and 2-4.  The SPSP concedes this requirement

is clearly unjust and unreasonable.   
b. NYC Leak Repair Proposal  
NYC proposes that the Companies should eliminate the entire leak backlog in five years.  
Tr. 5472, lines 9-12.  Again, the vast majority of leaks in the Companies’ backlogs are Type-3 
leaks, which are defined by regulation as non-hazardous and are reasonably expected to remain 
that way.  Tr. 4054, lines 18-20.  There are not sufficient benefits for removing all non-hazardous 


--- Page 249 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
244 of 310 
NYC leaks to justify the significant additional resources that would be required.  Thus, NYC 
proposed acceleration of leak repair would raise costs for customers without providing the benefits. 
c.  Environmental Defense Fund’s Proposals 
EDF’s proposal is to change fundamentally the structure of the leak management from a 
leak backlog measure to an overall methane reduction measure.  EDF recommends that the 
Companies use advanced leak detection technology (essentially the cavity ring-down spectroscopy 
(“CRDS”) technology mounted on a Google car) to conduct a baseline survey of the Companies’ 
entire service territory to establish a baseline methane emissions level and then to establish a target, 
such as a 50 percent reduction of emissions, to be achieved over a fixed period, such as five years. 
Tr. 5608-13.  EDF acknowledges that a baseline survey could take time and recommends that the 
existing metric structure stay in place until such time as the new metric could be developed. Tr. 
5614, lines 1-9.   
The Companies maintain that the advanced leak detection technology is not yet sufficiently 
reliable to form the basis for a new metric.  The Companies agree that they should begin to 
integrate CRDS technology into their arsenal of tools to combat methane emission, via their 
proposed Enhanced High Emitter Methane Detection Program.  This program provides for 
deployment of Google car-mounted CRDS technology operated by contractors in each of 
KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s service territories to prioritize high emission leaks for repair in limited 
areas already know to have a high leak concentration.  Even if the Companies believed that the 
technology could produce a reliable baseline measure, to achieve a full baseline survey of the 
Companies entire service areas that EDF proposes would require significantly more contractor 
resources to implement the program.  The Companies’ proposed Enhanced High Emitter Methane 
Detection Program supports use of the Google Car/CRDS technology in a more reasonable way 
and can provide an opportunity to further evaluate this technology. 


--- Page 250 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
245 of 310 
Importantly, the Commission has not approved CRDS or any other advanced leak detection 
technology for use to conduct required leak surveys.  At best, from a regulatory standpoint, the 
Companies can begin to deploy these technologies to supplement and enhance their traditional 
programs, as the Companies have proposed via their Enhanced High Emitter Methane Detection 
Program.   
d. NYC Leak Survey Proposal 
NYC recommends that the Companies adopt the same leak survey techniques and 
frequencies in their NYC service areas as Con Edison.  Tr. 5478, lines 1-3.  Con Edison has chosen 
to deploy more frequent walking surveys and monthly low-speed surveys that exceed regulatory 
leak survey requirements.  Tr. 3810, lines 8-10.  KEDNY and KEDLI have also deployed strategic 
leak survey techniques that exceed regulatory requirements to enhance customer safety, but in a 
different manner than Con Edison.  Id., lines 10-12.  For example, the Companies have an 
enhanced leak survey procedure for the winter months when leaks are more likely to occur.  Id., 
lines 12-14.  The procedure includes: more frequent patrols of all cast iron distribution mains (an 
average of 14 days and not to exceed 20 days) beginning in either late December or early January 
regionally depending on weather patterns and cast-iron break history; and additional surveillance 
when the seven-day frost degree days (daily average temp over seven consecutive days) is below 
27 degrees Fahrenheit.  Id., lines 15-21.  Con Edison, KEDNY, KEDLI, and other utilities across 
the stated deploy various and differing safety enhancement programs.  To require uniformity 
across neighboring service territories for programs that exceed regulatory requirements would be 
burdensome and costly and it would disincentivize innovation and chill the pursuit of discretionary 
safety enhancements.

The damage prevention metric measures the Companies’ ability to minimize instances of 
damage to its system caused by excavation activities.  The performance measures are based on 
instances of damage in different categories per 100 one call tickets, which notify the utility of 
excavation work in its territory.  Tr. 4165, lines 9-15.

damages rather than splitting the metric into three categories.  Tr. 4069-70, lines 10-24 and 1-14.

emergency reports in accordance with accepted company procedures and 16 N.Y.C.R.R. §255.604;

5. 
Regulatory Compliance/Gas Regulation Metrics 
KEDNY and KEDLI are currently subject to a Gas Safety Regulations Performance Metric 
that exposes the Companies to significant NRAs (up to 100 pre-tax basis points) based on 
violations of certain Commission’s rules and regulations contained in 16 NYCRR Parts 255 
(Transmission and Distribution of Gas) and 261 (Piping Beyond the Meter).  Tr. 3764.  Based on 
the Companies record of improvement in compliance since 2015, the Companies propose that the 
NRAs be spread between larger tiers, in line with the structure that was approved for Niagara 
Mohawk. The Companies also propose that the total NRA per year be capped at 75 basis points.   
Tr. 3765. 
The SPSP does not support this proposal and instead recommends that the metric’s tiers 
remain as they are currently established. Tr. 4091-92.  Based on the Companies’ record of 
improved performance, and because the broader tiers the Companies are proposing were recently 
adopted for Niagara Mohawk’s Gas Regulations Performance Metric, the Companies’ proposal 
should be accepted.   
The Companies also disagree with the SPSP’s recommendation to remove the existing 10-
violation cap for violations of a single code section identified during field audits.  The Companies’ 
Gas Safety Regulations Performance Metric includes a cap of 10 violations of a single code section 
for both records audit violations and field audit violations.  The 10-violation cap was agreed-to in 


--- Page 258 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
253 of 310 
the Companies’ Joint Proposal in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases to ensure that the 
metric did not create a scenario where violations with a high number of occurrences could result 
in an overly punitive penalty without focusing attention properly on the underlying compliance 
issue.  This mechanism allows for the fair evaluation of the root cause of field violations and a 
mechanism to mitigate root causes yet still provides a strong incentive to achieve full compliance 
with gas safety regulations.  Removal of the cap for field audits is overly punitive, not justified 
based on the Companies’ record of performance against this metric, and unnecessary in light of 
the implementation plan requirements for violations that exceed the cap.   
B.
