lines Program, Enhanced High Emitter Methane Detection Program (funding 
in rates or as pilot) 
1. 
Forecasts for Safety Programs - Plastics Fusion QA/QC 
 
The SGIOP recommends a downward adjustment to the capital forecast for this program 
in the amount of approximately $0.25 million for KEDNY and $0.31 million for KEDLI.  Tr. 
4666-67, lines 20-24 and 1-5.  Staff notes that the Companies’ response to IR DPS-711 indicates 
that the Companies’ forecast is based on the estimated number of incremental inspections times 
the unit price per inspection.  Tr. 4666.  The SGIOP does not question the Companies’ estimated 
number of inspections or unit prices, but states that the total forecast includes additional costs that 
were not justified, which Staff proposes to remove.  Tr. 4667, lines 5-8.  As explained in the 
rebuttal testimony of the Companies’ Gas Safety Panel, the Companies inadvertently neglected to 
include the basis for the entire forecast in their response to IR DPS-711.  Tr. 3821, lines 10-12.  
The forecast is based on the number of inspections times the unit price, plus the capital labor 
required to conduct the inspections that is not included in the unit price.  Id., lines 12-14.  The 
capital labor portion of the forecast is what would be removed by the SGIOP’s adjustment.  Id., 


--- Page 259 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
254 of 310 
lines 14-15.  The labor portion of the forecast is required to enable the Companies to meet their 
regulatory obligations to conduct additional inspections of plastic fusions.  Id., lines 15-19.  
Significantly, the SGIOP agrees the capital labor to perform the inspections is an appropriate 
component of the costs.  Tr. 4780, lines 14-17.  Thus, there is no basis for the SGIOP’s adjustment.   
2. 
 Enhanced Contractor Inspection Program (FTE) 
 
The Enhanced Contractor Inspection Program adds inspectors and a small amount of 
associated capital labor so that the ratio of inspectors to contractor crews will be one-to-one.  The 
SPSP supports the program but recommends a reduction to the Companies’ proposed number of 
FTEs to reflect the estimated workload.  Tr. 4119, lines 3-9.  The Companies do not agree with the 
SPSP’s five percent reduction in the FTEs associated with the program and believe the number of 
FTEs included in its rebuttal filing are necessary to maintain the one-to-one ratio of inspectors to 
contractor crews in the Rate Year and Data Years.  Tr. 3823-24, lines 1-21 and 1-6. 
3. 
Low Pressure Main – Valve Program 
 
The SGIOP proposes the Low-Pressure Main Valves Installation Program should be 
funded through NRAs rather than through rates.  Tr. 4665, lines 2-7.  The Companies do not agree.  
This program is an incremental safety program that will be ongoing because the valves will be 
installed on new services as well as on existing main.  Tr. 3827, lines 9-20.  This is an ongoing 
program with long-term benefits to customers that justify inclusion in rates.  Id.  There will also 
be future investment required to ensure the valves remain operational over time.  Id.  The costs of 
this program and the nature of this program render it inappropriate to fund through the limited pool 
of NRA dollars that are available.  Id.  This program should be funded through rates, and NRAs 
should be reserved for shorter-term programs that do not require ongoing spending.

C. 
NYC Local Law 152 (“LL152”) 
 
LL152 is a NYC Council law that requires building owners in NYC to conduct inspections 
including atmospheric corrosion and leak surveys of the natural gas piping inside buildings, 
including the same segments that the Companies are required to inspect pursuant the inside service 
line definition change.  Tr. 4104, lines 9-23.  The Companies, Con Edison, and the NGA worked 
with the DOB for nearly a year prior to issuance of the proposed rules implementing the law to 
provide guidance and information on how coordination could be achieved.  When the DOB issued 
its proposed rules on November 30, 2018, the Companies were disappointed that the rules were

E.

Pursuant to the Joint Proposal adopted in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Case, 
beginning in CY 2018, the Companies were to begin to relocate gas meters located inside premises 
to outside the premises when replacing or relocating gas service lines as part of the LPP retirement 
program, except where (i) the customer refuses permission to relocate the meter outside; (ii) local 


--- Page 264 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
259 of 310 
building codes or similar regulations prohibit outside meters; (iii) exterior or interior obstacles 
prevent meter relocation; or (iv) the gas meters should not be moved outside for safety reasons.  
The SPSP recommends that the Companies continue to relocate inside meters to outside and 
expand their efforts to perform this work not only in coordination with the LPP replacement 
programs, but also whenever the Companies perform service transfers or move an existing service 
line to a newly installed pipeline.  Tr. 4110, lines 5-15. 
The Companies agree with the safety benefits of relocating meters from inside to outside.  
Meter relocations would also eventually reduce costs of performing required inspections of inside 
service lines.  Tr. 3836-39.  At this time, however, KEDNY is not able to make specific 
commitments regarding relocating meters from inside to outside for reasons because of challenges 
coordinating with the Department of Buildings (“DOB”).  Id.  The DOB’s requirements are varied 
and complex; some examples include: three-pound pressure testing beyond the installation to the 
entire premises that could compromise internal piping that otherwise operates safely at lower usage 
pressures, particularly on older buildings common in NYC; multiple inspections by DOB including 
a close out inspection such that the work could not be completed in one day and would require 
customers to be out of service multiple days or longer; and work could not be performed by 
KEDNY’s crews, requiring additional resources to contract for master plumber services and 
coordination of KEDNY’s service line replacement crews with additional plumber crews.  Tr.

3837-38.  Taken together, the Code requirements prevent KEDNY from performing the meter

With regard to inactive accounts, the SPSP notes that inactive accounts should be addressed 
immediately and that the Companies should also address meters found not to be readily accessible.  
As stated in the Panel’s direct testimony, under the Companies’ current inactive accounts process, 
the Companies act immediately to perform a site analysis on every site to determine whether 
conditions, such as a vacant premises or other observable unsafe condition, warrant immediately 
securing the line.  Tr. 3824, lines 13-17.  In such instances, the service is cut within 24 hours.  Id.  
Assuming there is not an urgent circumstance, the process is designed to resolve inactive accounts 
within 60 days. Tr. 3824, lines 17-19.  In practice, however, 47 percent of KEDNY’s inactive 
accounts and 89 percent of KEDLI’s inactive accounts are resolved within the first 15 days, and 
only approximately 5 percent of KEDNY’s and 1.6 percent of KEDLI’s accounts remain inactive 
between days 45 and 60 of the current process. Tr. 3824-25.  The Companies’ proposal to achieve 
a 30-day process over a two-year period will expedite the entire process.  Tr. 3825, lines 1-3.  
Regarding meter locations, at the time of original installation, the Companies install meters in 
readily accessible locations (i.e., locations not obstructed and able to be accessed to perform 
inspections and maintenance), regardless of whether the meter is physically located on the outside 
or inside of a customer’s building.  Id., lines 5-9.  Over the past several years, the Companies have

G.   
Use of NRA Balances  
The Companies have historically used NRA balances to fund designated safety programs 
for the benefit of customers.  Such treatment is consistent with how other utilities across New York 
State, including Con Edison and Niagara Mohawk, have used such deferral balances.  Tr. 3792, 
lines 15-17.  In the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the Joint Proposal approved by the 
Commission specifically provides that “[a]ny Gas Safety Performance Metrics negative revenue 
adjustments incurred by KEDNY [and KEDLI] will be deferred for future use in incremental safety 
programs.”208  Id., lines 17-21.  For example, Niagara Mohawk is currently using NRA deferral 
balances to fund its Residential Methane Detection program and for GPS mapping of gas facilities.  
Tr. 3793, lines 1-2.  The SPSP recommends that the Companies use the NRA deferral balances to 
fund the Companies’ proposed Expanded Residential Methane Detector, I&R Control Line Survey 
and GPS Mapping, and Low Pressure Valve Installation Programs and, in consultation with Staff, 
that the Companies submit a plan for using NRA balances to fund additional incremental safety 
programs.  Tr. 4088-89, lines 21-24 and lines 1-3. 
The Companies support using portions of the deferral balances to fund the Residential 
Methane Detector Program and the Incremental I&R Control Line Survey and GPS Program.  The 
Companies maintain that, for a one-year rate plan, the other safety programs should remain in 
rates.  The Companies believe the practice of deferring gas safety related NRAs for future 
investment in gas safety programs should continue.  Use of NRA deferrals for gas safety programs 
                     
208 Joint Proposal § V.8.5(f).

allows the Companies to fund important safety programs for the benefit of the customers and 
communities served, while also mitigating increased rate requests that would normally accompany 
such incremental programs.  This allows the Companies to enhance the benefits provided to 
customers without increasing customer bill impacts.  Immediate return of the full balance of the 
NRA deferrals, as the NYC Gas Infrastructure and Safety Panel (“GISP”) suggests (Tr. 5486, lines 
20-22), would merely provide a one-time credit on customer bills without providing the funding 
required for, or benefits of, the incremental gas safety programs proposed.

Companies are not proposing to utilize the full NRA balances at this time.  Tr. 5418, lines 19-22.  
Immediate use of the full balances, however, would be short-sighted as the gas industry, and the 
safety regulations that govern it, are constantly evolving.  The Companies need the ability to react 
quickly and implement programs in response to these evolving regulatory and customer needs.  By 
leaving a portion of NRA balances in deferrals, the Companies are positioning themselves to 
respond timely to future regulatory mandates and implement corresponding programming that will 
directly benefit customers.  Therefore, the Companies propose to continue the accounting 
treatment and use of NRA funds in the manner established in prior rate cases. 
XI.  CUSTOMER SERVICE 
A. 
Customer Service Performance Metrics; Performance Measures In A One-
Year Case 
In addition to gas safety performance metrics, Staff is proposing customer service quality 
measures and penalties for missing service targets, notwithstanding that the Companies’ have 
delivered consistently high levels of performance over multiple years.  Tr. 1203, lines 11-13.  Staff 


--- Page 269 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
264 of 310 
and UIU recommend continuing the same four metrics209 set forth in the Companies’ current Joint 
Proposal – but significantly ratcheting up the performance targets.  Tr. 1465, line 10; Tr. 1467, 
lines 1-5.   These metrics, if missed, would result in annual penalties of $11.700 million and $9.900 
million for KEDNY and KEDLI.  Id.; Tr. 1473, lines 7-8; Exhibit 414 (SCSP-2), Page 2 of 2.  
In prior cases, KEDNY and KEDLI have voluntarily agreed to such mechanisms in 
negotiated rate case settlements, including specifically, the existing customer service quality 
program (“CSQP”).210  In discussing the history of performance incentives like the CSQP, the 
Commission has asserted that such programs “arose as a necessary component of multi-year rate 
plans to counter potential utility actions to increase earnings at the expense of reliability, safety 
and customer service quality.”211  As such, service quality metrics and targets have historically 
been negotiated elements of multi-year rate settlements, balancing agreed upon costs for customer 
service-related expenditures with desired levels of customer service quality.  
 However, in this case, KEDNY and KEDLI have demonstrated good performance on the 
service quality metrics, meeting or exceeding their respective service quality targets since 2015, 
with KEDNY consistently meeting all of its metrics since 2008.  Tr. 1203, lines 11-13.  Staff has 
stated that the Companies have been providing “proper performance.”  Tr. 1562, lines 14-15.  
Further, when asked if there was any indication of a decline in performance on any of the customer 
service quality metrics that would make the Companies miss its targets during the Rate Year, Staff 
responded, “[w]e don't have evidence in front of us today that would point to how the Company 
would miss a target.”  Tr. 1567, lines 2-4.  Similarly, when asked if he could “see anything to

For the same reasons discussed above relative to the gas safety performance metrics, 
customer service quality metrics are inappropriate in a one-year litigated case.212  Moreover, 
Staff’s assertion that a CSQP is needed in this case is inconsistent with the Commission’s order in

metrics.213  Tr. 1462, lines 16-19.   
Further, the metrics and targets recommended by Staff are arbitrary and without any basis 
in the existing statutory and regulatory framework.  The PSL and the Commission’s regulations 
are devoid of any mention of uniform statewide customer service quality standards or targets for 
electric and gas corporations like those in the proposed CSQP.214  As discussed, supra, the standard 
specified in the PSL for the provision of electric and gas service is that gas and electric corporations 
must provide “safe and adequate service.”215  Staff’s proposed CSQP essentially measures the 
Companies’ performance against itself, rather than any objective measure of customer service 
quality.  Tr. 1466, lines 5-18.  For example, with regard to KEDNY and KEDLI Complaint Rates,

the CSQP target proposed by Staff would set at 1.0, which Staff notes is, “[t]the lowest target

further, proposing a rate of 0.7 complaints per 100,000 customers for the KEDNY Complaint Rate,

Under Staff’s scheme, the Companies do not receive any reward or incentive for meeting 
or surpassing the customer service standards, other than retaining Staff’s proposed rate of return, 
which if approved, as discussed supra, would be the lowest in the state.  Staff describes the 
monetary consequences in the CSQP as “NRAs” rather than penalties, suggesting they provide 
“earnings consequences” related to the quality of service provided.  Tr. 1461-1462, lines 22-24 
and line 1.  As noted in City of Buffalo v. Neubeck, “a penalty is a sum of money for which the law 
exacts payment by way of punishment for doing some act which is prohibited, or omitting to do 
some act which is required to be done.”216  Regardless of the label chosen by Staff, there is no 
doubt the CSQP is designed to exact payment from the Companies as punishment for omitting to 
do some act, i.e., meeting customer service targets, that the CSQP requires be met.  Characterizing 
these monetary penalties as “earnings consequences” or NRAs does not change the fact that if the 
Companies fail to meet the targets imposed by the CSQP, the Companies would be penalized by 
as much as $11.700 million and $9.900 million, for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively.  Exhibit 
414 (SCSP-2), Page 2 of 2.  In short, the one-sided nature of the CSQP NRAs makes clear that 
                     
216 City of Buffalo v Neubeck, 209 AD 386, 388 (4th Dept 1924). 


--- Page 272 ---

267 of 310 
they are nothing more than monetary penalties, used to punish the Companies for failing to meet 
the arbitrary customer service targets sought by Staff under the CSQP. 
Staff claims that the CSQP should be continued because it is necessary to provide assurance 
that the Companies will maintain satisfactory customer service throughout the Rate Year.  Tr. 
1465, lines 14-15.  However, implementation of a CSQP as Staff recommends would impose 
arbitrary targets specific only to the Companies and establish a penalty scheme for not meeting 
said targets, regardless of whether the Company is in fact meeting the statutory standard of “safe 
and adequate” service.217  Imposing such standards and targets would result in unlawful disparate 
treatment for KEDNY and KEDLI – and ignores whether the level of service provided by KEDNY 
and KEDLI is consistent with levels of service provided by other electric or gas corporations.  
Indeed, as discussed supra, Staff and Mr. Collar seek to impose standards that in some cases are 
either at the highest levels imposed upon any New York utility, and in one case significantly more 
stringent than the highest standard currently used by a New York utility. Tr. 1472, lines 12-15; Tr. 
1799, line 13.  Absent a compelling reason to do otherwise, the statutory standards of safe and 
adequate service must be the same and universally applied to all electric and gas utilities.  There 
is no underlying statutory or regulatory basis or industry-wide standard for setting the service 
quality targets proposed by Staff or Mr. Collar.  The process recommended by Staff to adjust 
existing targets has never been adopted through SAPA and does not appear anywhere in the PSL 
or the Commission’s regulations.   
The Companies acknowledge that ensuring safety and reliability in the delivery of gas and 
in providing customer service is a primary mandate of the Commission.218  The Companies do not

their targets for service quality standards during the Rate Year.  Tr. 1567, lines 2-4; Tr. 1821, lines

Given the Companies’ history of performance on the customer service quality metrics, 
there is no justification to impose a CSQP or penalties to provide further incentive for the 
Companies to provide safe and adequate service as required under the PSL.220  No party to this 
proceeding has produced any evidence to suggest the Companies do not comport to this standard, 
and will not continue to do so during the Rate Year.  KEDNY and KEDLI have consistently 
demonstrated good performance over multiple, consecutive years meeting all respective service 
quality targets since 2015, including meeting all CSQP targets established in the Joint Proposal in 
the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.221  Tr. 1203, lines 11-13. 
Further, because the metrics are measured on a calendar year basis, and this case will not 
result in a Commission order until six or seven months into the calendar year, imposing metrics 
with new targets are particularly inappropriate.  Indeed, requiring the Companies to retroactively 
meet new, more stringent targets without the benefit of the full year to manage resources

1. 
Staff And UIU’s Proposals To Increase Service Quality Targets In 
Response To Meeting Or Exceeding Performance Levels Is Punitive And 
Based On Flawed Regulatory Policy 
Staff’s proposed targets for PSC Complaint Rate, Customer Satisfaction, Telephone 
Answer Rate, and Adjusted Bills, were adjusted in this case to make each of the current targets 
either more “stringent” or more difficult to achieve than the current target.  Tr. 1467, lines 1-5.  
Similarly, UIU Witness Gregg Collar recommends more stringent targets for KEDNY’s PSC 
Complaint Rate and Residential Customer Transaction Satisfaction,  KEDNY and KEDLI’s 
Adjusted Customer Bills metrics, and KEDLI’s Telephone Answer Rate.  Tr. 1798, lines 2-6.   
As discussed by the Staff, modifications to the targets were proposed in order to maintain 
“satisfactory service.”  Tr. 1465, lines 14-15.  As in past negotiated multi-year rate case 
settlements,222 Staff reviewed the Companies’ historic performance and adjusted the proposed 
targets to make them more stringent or difficult to achieve.  Tr. 1467, lines 1-5; Exhibit 414 (SCSP-
2), pages 1-2.     
Similarly, Mr. Collar proposes to set targets at a “higher level of service,” and at a level 
that would achieve “enhanced customer service.”  Tr. 1817, lines 13-14; Tr. 1798, line 1.  As 
discussed, supra, the statutory standard is “safe and adequate” service, and there is no statutory or 
regulatory basis that justifies imposition of standards or targets for “enhanced customer service” 
                     
222 See e.g. 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Case Order. 


--- Page 275 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
270 of 310 
or targets that are simply “harder to achieve”, upon the Companies in a litigated one-year case.  Tr. 
1798, lines 6-10. 
In cases where the typical methodology would result in a target that is lower than the 
current threshold, Staff declines to adjust the target.  Tr. 1468-1469, lines 23-24 and 1-6 (wherein 
Staff discusses the need to maintain the KEDNY’s call answered target at a level more stringent 
than was calculated under Staff’s “typical methodology”).  Staff’s proposal has the effect of either 
recommending more stringent targets, or where the data does not support more stringent targets, 
keeping the target at current levels, thus creating a “ratchet” where standards can only be increased 
and never be allowed to become less stringent.  Tr. 1469, lines 1-6.    However, ratcheting up 
performance measures solely based on past good performance is unreasonable and contrary to 
sound regulatory policy.  Tr. 1208-09, lines 20-21 and line 1.  Creating a ratchet, whereby by good 
performance is rewarded with steadily increasing performance targets creates a disincentive to 
achieve performance levels that are anything above existing targets.  Tr. 1209, lines 1-4.  Indeed, 
the application of Staff’s typical methodology penalizes a utility for good or excellent 
performance, by imposing more stringent targets, and rewards mediocre or poor performance by 
freezing targets at existing levels.  Further, ratcheting performance standards will eventually reach 
a level that cannot be reasonably achieved or sustained with current budgets – leading to a need to 
increase rates to achieve continuously increasing performance levels.  Tr. 1209, lines 4-7.  For 
these reasons, it is contrary to sound regulatory policy to make targets more stringent merely 
because a utility has consistently been meeting the existing target.  Id.; Tr. 1209, lines 7-9.

B. 
Terminations/Uncollectible Incentive Mechanism 
1. 
The Metrics and Targets Proposed by Staff, PULP, and UIU Should be 
Rejected as they Rely on Erroneous Assumptions or are Otherwise 
Inappropriate 
The Companies propose to continue the positive-only residential terminations and 
uncollectible performance measures established in their currently effective rate plans in the Rate 
Year.  Tr. 1112, lines 16-21.  The Companies propose to use the same methodology established in 
the previous case, with a modified approach for KEDLI.  Tr. 1114, lines 15-18.  KEDNY proposes 
to use a five-year normalized average using seven years of data omitting the high and low outliers 
and set a threshold reduction of one standard deviation from the average.  Tr. 1114-15.  KEDLI 
proposes to use the four years of data after the LIPA separation to develop its average, and establish 
a threshold reduction of one standard deviation from the average.  Tr. 1114, lines 18-20; Tr. 1116, 
lines 1-3.  The Companies provided exhibits outlining the proposed average and threshold 


--- Page 282 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
277 of 310 
termination and uncollectible targets for KEDNY and KEDLI.  See Tr. 1115, lines 6-14; Exhibit 
192, Schedule 4; Exhibit 193, Schedule 4. 
Staff supports the Companies’ proposal to continue the positive-only incentives, but argues 
that the three-year historical average from calendar years 2016-2018 without removing any outliers 
should be used to develop the targets.  Tr. 1479, lines 10-16; Tr. 1480, lines 7-10.  Staff asserts 
that use of the same time period for all metrics would yield a target “more relevant to Companies 
current operating environment[,]” standardize how the Companies’ historical performance in this 
area is viewed, and establish targets based on years the current performance measures were in 
place.  Tr. 1479, lines 10-20.  The overall effect of Staff’s adjustment to the average period used 
to calculate these performance measures is a reduction of the thresholds and targets for residential 
termination and uncollectible expense for both KEDNY and KEDLI.  Tr. 1480, lines 7-23; Tr. 
1177, lines 3-12.  Staff also proposes to add an additional metric to measure the amount of 
residential arrears to “balance [collection] measures more effectively for the benefit of utilities and 
customers.”  Tr. 1482, lines 7-12; Tr. 1485, lines 1-8.  Under Staff’s proposal, the Companies 
would only achieve the maximum positive revenue adjustment for these performance measures if 
threshold targets are achieved in all three metrics.  Tr. 1485-86.  Staff did not propose any changes 
to the positive revenue adjustment amounts tied to these performance measures.  Tr. 1486-87.   
The Companies do not believe that the three-year period proposed by Staff provides a 
representative data sample of the Companies’ uncollectible expense.  Uncollectible expense is 
largely driven by external factors – weather volatility, commodity pricing volatility, and changes 
in market and general economic conditions – which express over cycles longer than three years.  
Tr. 1178, lines 2-5.  A smaller data sample, like the three years proposed by Staff, is less likely to 
consider and normalize these factors, whereas a larger sample size, like the timeframes proposed 


--- Page 283 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
278 of 310 
by the Companies, typically flattens the impacts of these external variables.  Id., lines 5-10.  
Further, the timeframes proposed by the Companies already encompass the years in which the 
current metrics were effective, thereby mitigating Staff’s concerns that the data include, and the 
prospective targets be based on, the Companies’ historical performance in this area.  The 
Companies therefore believe that the Commission should use the normalized period proposed in 
their testimony as the basis for the residential termination and uncollectible averages and 
thresholds.   
Additionally, the Companies do not agree with Staff’s proposal to include residential 
arrears as part of the Companies’ terminations and uncollectible expense performance measures.  
As with uncollectible expense and terminations, the amount of residential arrears is heavily 
dependent on external factors beyond the Companies’ control, most notably customer behavior.  
Staff’s proposal completely disregards these external factors, which significantly impact the 
Companies’ levels of residential arrears, and assumes that the Companies will be able to manage 
its performance under these metrics independent of these exogenous factors, which is entirely 
unrealistic and unsupported by the Companies’ historical experience in this area.  Tr. 1179-80.  
Further, even if Staff’s methodology appropriately accounted for applicable external factors, 
addition of this metric to the residential terminations and uncollectible expense performance 
measures fails to account for the fact that terminations are inversely correlated to arrears.  Indeed, 
as terminations decrease, the amount of residential arrears would likely increase assuming the 
same external factors remain constant.  Staff has not provided any documentation indicating that 
the external factors affecting the Companies will change in the Rate Year and it is impractical to 
impose an additional metric to these performance measures that directly conflicts with the existing 
metrics.  Finally, Staff’s proposal infers that the 60-day arrears provided in the Companies’

monthly Collection Activity Reports (“CARs”) could be used to measure the residential arrears

be viewed as a ratio or percentage of the Companies’ total accounts receivable to accurately

C. 
Credit/Debit Card Payments and Fees 
The Companies proposed to include in base rates the estimated cost to recover fees incurred 
by KEDNY and KEDLI when residential customers make Credit Card and Debit Card (“CC/DC”) 
payments.  Tr. 1140, lines 6-9.  This is referred to as a “no-fee model.”  Id.  The Companies 
proposed no-fee model would eliminate the per-transaction cost to customers paying by CC/DC 
by shifting that cost into base rates.  Id.  Using existing per transaction costs for CC/DC payments, 


--- Page 287 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
282 of 310 
the annual costs associated with the new program would be $0.625 million and $1.964 million for 
KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively.  Tr. 1142, lines 1-6.   
Considering the uncertainty associated with the transaction fees and potential increase in 
customers making CC/DC payments in response to the no-fee model, the Companies also proposed 
a two-way deferral mechanism be put in place for the no-fee model.  Tr. 1142-1143, lines 20-21 
and lines 1-2.  If the costs to provide the no-fee model exceed the rate allowance, the Companies 
would defer the shortfall for future recovery from customers, or alternatively, if the costs are less 
than the rate allowance, the Companies would defer the excess for the benefit of customers.  Tr. 
1143, lines 2-6. 
Staff recommends that the Commission allow the Companies to accept credit/debit cards 
from customers for bill payment, “under the condition that the per-transaction fee does not exceed 
the current amount of $2.25 as described in the Companies’ response to IR DPS-368.”  Tr. 1494, 
lines 3-8.  Staff also recommends that the Commission impose a downward-only reconciliation.  
Tr. 1494, lines 8-10.  Under these conditions, with a downward-only reconciliation mechanism 
and an artificial cap on transaction fees, the Companies will not move forward with a proposal to 
implement the no-fee model to socialize credit card fees.  Tr. 1218, lines 9-11.  
Staff’s testimony raises a question about Staff’s interpretation of the scope of the 
Company’s proposal.  Staff’s proposal could be read to suggest that all customers (i.e., residential 
and commercial customers) could use credit/debit cards.  Tr. 1494, lines 4-5.  The Companies’ 
proposal, however, was limited to “residential customers.”  Tr. 1140, lines 6-8; Tr. 1215, lines 8-
9.  In developing the Companies’ proposal for the no-fee model for CC/DC, the Companies 
focused exclusively on residential CC/DC usage and fees.  Tr. 1216, lines 8-9.  As such, any 


--- Page 288 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
283 of 310 
consideration of a no-fee model for CC/DC usage should be expressly limited to residential 
customers. 
The SCSP’s recommendation that the per transaction fee not exceed $2.25, does not reflect 
key details from the Companies’ proposal.  Tr. 1216, lines 9-12.  The Companies forecast the cost 
for the no-fee model based on the two-tiered payment structure and a de facto limit on the total 
amount that can be charged in a single credit card transaction.  Tr. 1139, lines 17-20; Tr. 1142, 
line 1.  As acknowledged by Staff in testimony, the rates charged by the Companies’ vendor are 
$2.25 for transactions from $0 to $600 and $4.50 for transactions between $600 and $1,200.  Tr. 
1578, lines 5-11.  Staff specifically agreed that the $2.25 fee they proposed for a cap, would apply 
only to a subset of the existing transactions, those between $0 and $600.  Tr. 1578, lines 18-21.  
However, when asked if Staff thought the Companies should limit credit card transactions to just 
those between $0 and $600, Staff responded, “no.”  Tr. 1578, lines 22-25.  When asked if Staff 
agreed that it would make sense to cap the fee for transactions between $600 and $1,200 at $4.50, 
which Staff previously acknowledged was the rate for such transactions, Staff stated that they were 
“hoping the cost would be lowered.”  Tr. 1578-1579, lines 5-11 and lines 2-8.

base rates, will heavily increase, as is the case at other New York utilities.”  Tr. 1494, lines 19-22.

though the Companies committed to work with vendors to leverage increases in transaction 
volumes to achieve lower transaction fees, there is no guarantee this will occur, and indeed, as 
noted by the Shared Services Panel, “at this point in time, it’s possible that [the fees] may be 
increasing.”  Tr. 1218, lines 3-5; Tr. 1228, lines 19-20. 


--- Page 289 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
284 of 310 
Given the possibility, and indeed, likelihood that the number of transactions will “heavily 
increase”, the fact that the $2.25 transaction fee cap proposed by Staff is already lower than the 
fees currently being incurred by KEDNY and KEDLI, and could be significantly lower than the 
newly negotiated fees, Staff’s proposal could result in significant under-recovery of costs if the 
Companies were to implement the no-fee model with Staff’s recommendations.  
 
Staff acknowledges that customers are increasingly using CC/DC to make purchases and 
that the current transaction vendor fee is inconvenient and for most businesses, already included 
as a cost of doing business.  Tr. 1494, lines 14-18.  While the Companies agree that customers 
desire and have come to expect that they can pay for service with CC/DC payments under a no-
fee model, Staff provides no meaningful justification for why the Company should bear the 
substantial risk of imposing a no-fee model, in the absence of an appropriate two-way 
reconciliation mechanism.  For these reasons, Staff’s proposal for a no-fee model with an 
unrealistic $2.25 per transaction fee cap, and a downward-only reconciliation mechanism that 
imparts all of the risk on the Companies should be rejected.

2. 
Automatic Suspension Of Collection/Extension Of Time To Pay For 
Customers On Public Assistance 
Mr. Yates’ proposes to automatically grant 30-day extensions and suspension of all 
collections for customers no longer on utility guarantee/direct voucher programs or 90-day 
extensions to customers with reconciling transactions involving HRA and/or the Department of 
Social Services (“DSS”).  Tr. 1679, lines 7-11.  However, Mr. Yates’ proposal to suspend all 
collections actions against such customers is not required by the Home Energy Fair Practices Act 
(“HEFPA”) or the regulations promulgated thereunder.225  Mr. Yates’ seems to focus on situations 
where customers are no longer on a utility guarantee/direct voucher program, and thus, the 
protections associated with these programs would, by Mr. Yates’ admission, no longer apply.  In 
these circumstances, it is incumbent upon the customer to address continued eligibility with the 
respective agency providing the service, or apply for additional protection, which could be used to 
continue protections as appropriate.  However, the Companies are aware of no mandate in HEFPA 
or elsewhere or the Commission’s regulations that requires the Companies to suspend collections 
in anticipation of the possibility that a customer may, at some point in the future qualify for public 
assistance, or simply because the customer formerly was on direct voucher or a utility guarantee.  
To the extent customers face administrative issues as Mr. Yates suggests, such as whether or not 
an agency has paid a bill, provided notice to the customer, or whether a customer continues to be 
eligible for direct voucher benefits, these are matters for discussion between the customer and the 
agency providing the benefits.   Tr. 1196, lines 16-20.  There is no requirement under HEFPA for

could create additional confusion among customers regarding when payments are due, result in

Allegations by Mr. Yates that the Companies’ collection procedures do not ensure 
customers having difficulty paying their bills are offered DPAs meeting the statutory requirements 
of HEFPA are incorrect.  Tr. 1652, lines 25-27.  The Companies’ practices regarding DPAs are 
fully compliant with the PSL and HEFPA.  Tr. 1190-1191, lines 9-10 and lines 10-12.  The 
Companies offer several forms of payment arrangements, including short term collection 
arrangements, offers of a standard payment agreement, and ultimately, the offer of a written 
minimum DPA to eligible customers.  Tr. 1190, lines 11-16.  HEFPA requires utilities “to make 
reasonable efforts to contact eligible customers” for the purpose of: (i) offering a deferred payment 
agreement; and (ii) negotiating terms tailored to the customer’s financial circumstances.227  
Collectively, the Companies’ outbound calling campaigns, bill notices, and final termination 
notices, coupled with offers of payment arrangements and written DPAs, comply with the 
Companies’ obligation under HEFPA.  Tr. 1191, lines 1-4. 
Mr. Yates also alleges that KEDNY’s offer of a standard payment agreement automatically 
upon issuance of the final termination notice to an eligible customer in arrears is unreasonable and 
does not comply with HEFPA.  Tr. 1657, lines 14-19.  Mr. Yates’ assertion is incorrect.  KEDNY’s 
practice of mailing a written standard payment agreement to every eligible customer along with 
the first disconnect notice is compliant with HEFPA.  Tr. 1191-1192, lines 10-18 and line 1.  
HEFPA requires KEDNY to offer either a negotiated DPA tailored to the customer’s financial

F. 
Outreach and Education; Reporting  
In response to the Companies’ proposal to flatten the rate blocks for firm customers, Staff 
recommends the Companies conduct additional outreach and education to ensure customers are 
aware that higher gas usage may increase their monthly bill.   Tr. 1495-1496, lines 23-24 and lines

emails and/or letters/postcards.  Tr. 1496, 5-8.  However, conducting additional outreach and 
education campaigns such as letter campaigns and bill inserts can require additional postage and 
printing costs not contemplated in the Companies’ original outreach and education plans.  Tr. 1219, 
Lines 7-10.  It is not clear why additional outreach and education is required to inform customers 
that an “increase in gas usage may increase their monthly bill” since customers have historically 
paid for gas based on the volume used, and thus it should be intuitive to such customers that 
increasing usage will increase their monthly bill.  When questioned about whether the Companies 
would incur costs for incremental outreach, Staff suggested they had proposed “low cost options 


--- Page 297 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
292 of 310 
such as the website updates or customer emails, to limit cost issues.”  Tr. 1584, lines 4-6.  However,

“in the form of bill inserts, website updates, and customer emails and/or letters/postcards” Tr. 
1496, lines 5-8.  The cost to send customers millions of additional bill inserts, letters and postcards 
would cause the Companies to incur substantial incremental printing and postage costs.  Tr. 1219, 
lines 7-9.   Given the intuitive nature of the message and the potentially substantial incremental

XII.  LOW INCOME PROGRAM 
A. 
Calculating Discounts And Eligibility 
The New York City Low Income Panel (“NYCLIP”) made a number of recommendations 
regarding the tiered bill discount amounts and file matching programs under the Companies’ 
current Energy Affordability Programs (“EAP”), along with a number of suggested modifications 
to the Commission’s policies on low income programs.  Tr. 5343-5368.   
NYCLIP reads the Companies’ testimony as proposing to “maintain the same bill discounts 
that are in effect as of January 1, 2019.”  Tr. 5357, lines 6-8.  This statement is incorrect.  Tr. 1210, 


--- Page 299 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
294 of 310 
line 17.  Based on this misinterpretation, NYCLIP asserts customers would receive “proportionally 
lower discounts then they receive now.”  Tr. 5357, lines 11-14.  The Companies do not propose to 
maintain the discounts constant in future years.  Tr. 1210, lines 17-19.  As noted in the direct 
testimony of the Shared Services Panel, the amounts provided reflect “current” benefit amounts 
for each tier.  Tr. 1210, lines 19-20.  The current benefit amounts were used for purposes of 
forecasting only, not to establish actual tier discounts for future years.  Tr. 1210-1211, lines 19-21 
and line 1.  Pursuant to the Commission’s Low Income Order, and subsequent Implementation 
Order, the Companies perform annual updates to the tiered discount amounts and make necessary 
adjustments, on an annual basis, to set discounts at levels intended to keep the energy burden at or 
below six percent. 229,230    
NYCLIP proposes to modify how median incomes are determined for NYC customers.  Tr. 
5361, lines 1-4.  Under NYCLIP’s proposal a different median income would be used for NYC 
residents than elsewhere in the State.  Id.  This would deviate from methodology adopted by the 
Commission in the Low Income Order, which NYCLIP alleges “makes the situation worse for 
New York City Families.”  Tr. 5360, lines 6-8.  However, NYCLIP’s approach is contrary to the 
Commission’s objectives to standardize low income programs and establish discounts and budgets 
on a statewide basis.231   
Nevertheless, based on NYCLIP’s proposed changes to median income and proposed 
adjustments to the Companies’ average bills, NYCLIP presents a new set of proposed tier discount

is further reinforced in the Commission’s Rehearing Order,234 wherein the Commission asserts it 
seeks to standardize the programs, streamline the regulatory process and ensure consistency with 
the Commission’s statutory and policy objectives.235  NYCLIP’s approach would deviate from the 
Commissions’ stated objectives of seeking standardization, consistency and uniformity among 
programs statewide.236  The Commission also directed in the Rehearing Order that potential areas 
for re-examination and refinements will be examined and addressed on a generic basis by the Inter-
Agency Task Force established pursuant to the Low Income Order, which was established to 
“achieve greater program coordination, share information, eliminate duplicative efforts, lower 
costs and increase effectiveness, and advise in the development of low income energy-related 
policies and programs.”237  For these reasons, the Commission should reject NYCLIP’s low 
income proposals and continue its policy of a generic, statewide approach, which includes 
opportunities for refinements where applicable and in keeping with the ongoing affordability 
proceedings in Case 14-M-0465.

properly qualified and receive the utility low income discount sooner.  Tr. 5373, lines 6-9.  As part 
of its proposal to increase the frequency of file matches, NYCLIP recommends that the annual 
reimbursement cap be increased from $100,000 to $200,000.  Tr. 5374, lines 5-8.   
As NYCLIP acknowledges, more frequent matches would not necessarily equate to an 
increase in participation levels.  Tr. 5373, lines 20-21.  Indeed, the Companies believe the 
frequency of file matching will have little, if any, impact on the total number of customers enrolled, 
because each file match has the potential to remove customers as well as add them.  Tr. 1202, lines 
16-19.  Said differently, changing the frequency of file matches is just as likely to increase the 
frequency of customers being removed as it is to increase the frequency of customers being added.  
Tr. 1202-1203, lines 19-21 and line 1.  Thus, increasing the frequency of file matches would only 
serve to increase the administrative costs of the HRA file matching process, without providing any 
discernible benefits for customers.  Tr. 1203, lines 1-4.  Because NYCLIP’s proposal to double 
the file matching frequency and increase the cap on reimbursement would increase costs without 
providing any discernible benefit for customers, the proposal should be rejected.

H. 
Elimination of Reconnection Fees For Energy Affordability Program 
Customers (KEDNY) 
NYCLIP proposes that KEDNY eliminate its existing reconnection fee for low income 
customers because it will provide them with a better opportunity to remain utility customers and 
pay their utility bills.  Tr. 5369, lines 18-20.  NYCLIP suggests that reconnect fees create a further 
financial barrier for customers who have already demonstrated an inability to pay utility bills.  Tr. 
5369-5370, lines 20-21 and lines 1-2.  NYCLIP acknowledges that KEDNY already provides a 
one-time waiver of the reconnection fee, but then suggests that customers with an inability to pay 
utility bills and multiple service terminations should be reconnected without a fee.  Tr. 5369, lines 
13-14, 20-21.  By NYCLIP’s admission, such customers may be unable to pay utility bills.  Tr. 
5369, lines 20-21.  If so, provision of additional utility service would only serve to increase arrears 
and uncollectible expense, which burdens all customers.  Tr. 1201, lines 7-9.  NYCLIP also 
suggests that having KEDNY eliminate reconnect fees would harmonize KEDNY’s practices with 
those already adopted by KEDLI and avoid the possibility that customers in similar areas be 
subject to different rules on reconnection fees.  Tr. 5371, lines 1-5.  KEDNY and KEDLI, however, 
are separate and distinct companies, with separate tariffs and pricing structures, and, like other 
utilities within New York, there is no requirement that KEDNY and KEDLI have identical fees 
and terms for service.  Tr. 1201, lines 13-16.  For these reasons, NYCLIP’s proposal to eliminate 
reconnection fees for KEDNY should be rejected.

Manager web site.  Tr. 1157, lines 8-14.  Over the last year, the Companies implemented changes 
to the EPA automated upload system to improve the customer experience.   Tr. 1157, lines 19-20. 
To further improve customer experience with the EPA automated upload system, the Companies 
are working on implementing additional enhancements.  Tr. 1158, lines 9-11.  These enhancements 
include real-time web form validation, hourly uploads of customer aggregate usage, and separate 
heating and cooking aggregate usage uploads.  Tr. 1158, lines 11-13.  Further, the Companies are 
revamping the web form to allow multi-property submissions and will be providing quarterly 
aggregate usage uploads for up to four years.  Tr. 1158, lines 14-16.   
As part of the Companies’ ongoing efforts to implement and maintain the EPA automated 
whole building aggregate upload system, the Companies have secured the services of a third-party 
call center vendor to provide specialized call center support services that include addressing 
customer questions with the EPA automated upload system, assist customers with the upload 
process, and handle general data inquire issues.  Tr. 1158-1159, lines 20-12 and lines 1-4.  The 
vendor first began providing these services in March 2019, with an estimated annual cost of $0.224 
million, which could vary based on the volume of inbound and outbound email and calls.  Tr. 1159, 
lines 9-11.  The estimated annual cost to support ongoing automated building energy usage 
reporting is $0.176 million and $0.044 million for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively.  Tr. 1159,

XIII.  MANAGEMENT AUDIT COMPLIANCE 
The Companies and Staff submitted testimony from witnesses Philip A. DeCicco and John 
Holst, respectively, describing KEDNY and KEDLI’s compliance with recommendations in three 
Commission-initiated audits: the Comprehensive Management and Operations Audit of National 
Grid’s New York gas business in Case 13-G-0009 (“Gas Management Audit”); the Operations 
Audit of the Accuracy of New York State Utilities’ Self-Reported Data in Case 13-M-0314 (“Data 
Audit”); and the Focused Operations Audit of Major Utility Internal Staffing Levels and Use of 
Contractors for Selected Core Utility Functions at Major New York Energy Utilities in Case 13-
M-0449 (“Staffing Audit”).  As stated in those testimonies, the Commission confirmed the 
Companies successfully implemented all recommendations from those audits and, as a result, the 
audits are officially closed.  Tr. 4812-14, lines 1-4, 16-19, and 8-12; Tr. 5285-87, lines 10-15, 14-
18, and 15-19. 
In May 2018, the Commission issued Request for Proposals for an independent consultant 
to conduct an audit of National Grid’s New York electric and gas businesses in accordance with 
Section 66(19) of the PSL (the “2018 Management Audit”).  Tr. 4810, lines 19-21.  In September 
2018, the Commission selected Saleeby Consulting Group (“SCG”) to conduct the audit under the 
supervision of Staff and directed National Grid to enter into a contract with SCG.  Tr. 4811, lines 
1-5.  The 2018 Management Audit formally commenced in November 2018 with a final report 
scheduled to be completed and delivered to National Grid by September 30, 2019.  Tr. 4811, lines 
7-8.  


--- Page 314 ---
19-G-0309             
Companies’ Initial Brief  
19-G-0310 
 
 
309 of 310 
In accordance with the contract, National Grid committed to being completely cooperative 
and transparent with SCG, Staff, and the audit process, with the goal of demonstrating National 
Grid’s full capabilities and receiving meaningful feedback that would drive useful 
recommendations to improve its electric and gas operations for the benefit of customers.  To that 
end, over the course of almost a year, National Grid: 
• Spent thousands of man hours on the audit; 
• Answered more than 1,400 information requests;  
• Participated in almost 300 interviews, including interviews of senior executives, US 
President, Global CEO, and Board of Directors; 
• Supported field visits across the Company’s New York service territory; and 
• Held multiple technical sessions on various topics. 
The audit was given the highest priority at National Grid, with senior executives forming 
teams across the business to ensure the company’s responsiveness to the audit.  While National 
Grid has no doubt that SCG identified areas for potential improvement, based on feedback the 
company received in real-time during the interview process, the company was under the 
impression that SCG – which had experience conducting management audits and was selected by 
the Commission based on this background – was impressed with National Grid’s management 
team, organizational structure, and capabilities in several areas.  To that end, National Grid was 
looking forward to the release of SCG’s independent report based on its intensive, yearlong review 
of the company’s operations. 
On October 2, 2019, National Grid received a letter from Staff notifying the company that 
Staff was terminating the audit.248  This was the first time that National Grid had been made aware 
                     
248 A copy of the letter can be found in DMM in Case 18-M-0159 (DMM #2).

that Staff had any issue whatsoever with SCG.  On December 5, 2019, Staff informed National

“primarily on the material provided by National Grid to SCG during the audit through discovery 
responses and interviews.”249  While National Grid is not taking any position with regard to the 
allegations made by Staff in the October 2, letter, the company is understandably frustrated given 
the level of support dedicated to the audit.  National Grid will work with Staff to complete the 
audit report but is reserving its rights to contest any findings that may be contradictory to those 
made by SCG.   
XIV.  CONCLUSION 
 
For the foregoing reasons, the contested issues discussed herein should be resolved in the 
manner recommended by KEDNY and KEDLI.  The record shows that many of the positions 
advocated by Staff and the parties are contrary to the record evidence, unreasonable, and would 
result in a revenue requirement that fundamentally fails to provide the Companies with the 
revenues needed to provide safe and reliable service to customers, earn a reasonable return on their 
investments, continue to attract capital on commercially reasonable terms, and deliver on the
